Stateless vs Stateful

Open PDF

Stateless vs Stateful Evolution of a Web App | Stateless vs Stateful While everything appears to be progressing smoothly, we must confront the challenge of managing state. Given that this is your uncle's e-commerce store, dealing with shopping carts is a must. These carts are commonly implemented using user sessions, which are in turn built on in-memory cookies. This is where we encounter a potential problem. 1/5

Consider the following scenario: a user visits the site and adds a bag of dog food to their cart. Server #1 handles this request, generating a new cookie for the cart. However, the subsequent request might be processed by Server #2, which has no knowledge of the initial cookie. The result is a frustrated user whose items have inexplicably disappeared from their shopping cart, creating a less-than-ideal experience. Stateful The issue is that we are storing session data (aka "state") in each server, and therefore the servers are "stateful." How do we go about solving that? One option is to configure the load balancer to use "session affinity" or "sticky sessions." This involves configuring the load balancer to forward requests from a particular user to a specific server for the duration of their session. By doing so, the server maintains the session state and can continue to process requests for that session. This works, but there are some downsides. For example, if the server fails, the session data stored on that server may be lost. Additionally, this approach can make it challenging to scale horizontally because it requires the load balancer to maintain state information about each user's session and map it to a specific server. Finally, it can make it difficult to distribute traffic evenly across servers, which may result in some servers becoming overloaded while others are underutilized. Stateless Now, what about removing state from the web servers and making them "stateless"? There are a few ways to go about this. 2/5

Keeping States On the Client We could move the shopping cart state to the client (e.g., the user's browser or mobile app). One way to implement this is by using client-side storage (e.g., Local Storage/IndexedDB for browsers, Core Data for iOS devices etc). Here's how it works: 1. When a user adds an item to their cart, the browser stores the cart data in the client-side storage. 2. With each subsequent request, the browser sends the shopping cart data to the server, either as part of the request or in a custom header. 3. The server processes the request based on the provided cart data (e.g., calculating the total or applying discounts) and sends the response back to the client. In this stateless approach, the server does not maintain any knowledge of the shopping cart between requests, relying on the client to manage the state. How to Store Session On the Client: 1. Cookies: Use Cases: Ideal for session management, user authentication, and storing user preferences. For example, an e-commerce site might use cookies to remember items in a shopping cart or a user's language settings. Pros: Supported by all browsers and automatically included in HTTP requests. Cons: Limited in size (about 4KB), and can pose security risks if not properly handled (vulnerable to CSRF attacks). When to Use: Best for small pieces of data where persistence across sessions is needed and when server-side access to this data is required. 3/5

2. Local Storage and Session Storage: Use Cases: Local storage is great for storing user settings, such as theme preferences or layout settings in a web application. Session storage is useful for temporary data like form inputs in a multi-step form process. Pros: Easy to use, with a larger capacity (up to 5-10MB). Cons: Local storage data doesn't expire, which can lead to storage management issues. Session storage is limited to the session's duration. When to Use: Use local storage for long-term data storage and session storage for data relevant to a single browser session. 3. IndexedDB: Use Cases: Ideal for applications needing to store large amounts of data client-side, like offline-first applications. For instance, a note-taking app might use IndexedDB to store notes locally for offline access. Pros: Can store large amounts of structured data, works asynchronously. Cons: More complex API compared to local or session storage. When to Use: Best for complex applications where large amounts of data need to be stored and manipulated client-side. 4. Client-Side JavaScript Libraries: Use Cases: Redux is often used in complex React applications for managing global state, like user authentication status or shared user data across components. Vuex serves a similar purpose in Vue.js applications. Pros: Centralizes state management, making it easier to manage and debug. Cons: Can add complexity to the project, and might be overkill for simple applications. When to Use: Ideal for single-page applications (SPAs) with complex state management needs. 5. JWT (JSON Web Tokens): Use Cases: Commonly used in web applications for securely handling user authentication and transmitting user information. For example, after logging in, a user receives a JWT to make authenticated API requests. Pros: Enables stateless authentication, is self-contained, and can be easily transmitted across different domains. Cons: Storage can be challenging, and it is vulnerable to XSS attacks if not stored securely. When to Use: Best for applications where security, scalability, and cross-domain access are important, especially in RESTful APIs and SPAs. Server-side Shared Memory Another way is to have a shared memory system like Memcached or Redis in front of the web servers. Here's how it works: 4/5

1. When a user adds an item to their cart, the server creates a session and stores the cart data in a shared memory store such as Memcached or Redis. 2. The server sends a session identifier (e.g., a session cookie) to the user's browser. 3. With each subsequent request, the user's browser sends the session identifier back to the server. 4. The server uses the session identifier to fetch the corresponding session data (i.e., the user's shopping cart) from the shared memory store and performs the requested operations (e.g., adding or removing items). By using a shared memory store like Memcached or Redis, the session data can be accessed by multiple servers, allowing for better scalability in distributed environments. Additionally, since Memcached and Redis are in-memory data stores, they can offer faster access times compared to traditional databases, improving overall performance. How to Store State in Shared Memory Redis and Memcached are typically the go-to technologies for in-memory session storage. Cloud providers often offer fully managed distributed caching services. For example, AWS offers ElasticCache for Redis and Memcached. Which One to Use in Scalable Web Applications Stateless web server setup is what most modern production systems use. Compute is typically the cheaper resource, and we can scale it virtually limitlessly. We can even set up systems that auto-scales the stateless servers so that when demands are high we launch more servers. 5/5

Scaling Services › Horizontal Scaling

Horizontal Scaling

Open PDF

Horizontal Scaling Evolution of a Web App | Single to Scaling Single-Server Setup Let's start with everyone's first web app. A plain, simple app that runs on a server written in PHP or Node.js with a database like MySQL or MongoDB behind it. The server takes some requests, asks the database for the information, and returns it to the user's device. There are approximately 100k tutorials out there on how to create such an app. You have a web server and a database. They probably even live on the same machine. Life is simple and beautiful. 1/4

When it comes time to deploy an app like this, you'd rent a VPS from a company like DigitalOcean for five bucks a month, purchase a domain name, set up the DNS to point the domain name to the VPS's IP address, and voila, you just finished your new shiny website for your uncle's online store to sell pet products. Vertical Scaling Now your uncle's restaurant is getting really popular, and your $5/month 1GB memory 25GB SSD DigitalOcean VPS can no longer handle it. What do you do? The obvious thing to do is to get a larger, beefier server, say $10/month 4GB 80SSD one. This is called “vertical scaling”. 2/4

Now let’s say your uncle’s got great business acumen and his business continues to boom. It has gone from taking orders from friends to shipping within state then to all 50 states and starting to open up international customers. You keep on getting the next larger server. At one point though, this becomes problematic, uneconomical (and slow). Why? If that one server goes down, the entire web app goes down. And you are gonna get frantic calls from your angry uncle. There is a limit on how big a server can get. Servers get disproportionately expensive as their sizes get large. For example, the largest server AWS offers is the x1.16xlarge instance type (64 vCPUs, 976 GiB of memory). But let's face it, one server can only go so far. Eventually, you'll hit a wall, and that's where horizontal scaling comes in. Instead of upgrading to a single, super-powerful server, you're going to build an entourage of smaller servers. It's like having a team of personal assistants, all of them dedicated to keeping your website in tip-top shape. This entourage of smaller servers is put behind a foreman, the "load balancer," whose sole job is to forward requests to other servers to process and then forward the response back to the clients (user devices). 3/4

4/4

Scaling Services › Horizontal Scaling

Boost Performance Reliability and Scalability for Your Applications

Open PDF

Boost Performance, Reliability, and Scalability for Your Applications Load Balancer Now that we know why we want to have stateless servers, let's take a look at how to do it exactly. At a high level, we need two things: A load balancer to put in front of the servers that do the actual work to forward requests and coordinate which server is responsible for which request. A mechanism to launch more servers as traffic is high and wind down servers when traffic is low. The mechanism also helps in launching new servers when servers fail. Load balancer Load balancers are devices or software that act as a reverse proxy, distributing client requests across multiple servers based on predetermined algorithms. This distribution process aims to optimize resource utilization, minimize response time, and avoid overloading a single server. 1/5

Load balancer Load Balancing Algorithms There are various load balancing algorithms, including: 2/5

1. Round Robin: Requests are distributed sequentially to available servers. Load balancer round robin 2. Least Connections: Requests are routed to the server with the fewest active connections. Load balancer least connection 3/5

3. Least Response Time: Requests are directed to the server with the lowest response time. Load balancer least response time 4. IP Hash: Requests are distributed based on the client's IP address, ensuring that a specific client is always routed to the same server. Load balancer IP Hash On top of these, there are also Weighted Round Robin: Like Round Robin, except server loads are based on their compute capacity. 4/5

URL Hash: Like IP hash, except the URL in the request is used. This is useful when we want to optimize each server for a set of URLs. For example, cache may be stored on the servers themselves and we can maximize cache hit rate using URL hash load balancing. Load balancers play a critical role in ensuring high availability, fault tolerance, and scalability in large-scale applications. They can be implemented as hardware appliances, virtual appliances, or software solutions. Next, let's take a look at the most popular software load balancer, Nginx. NGINX NGINX is a powerful, open-source web server, reverse proxy server, and load balancer that has gained popularity due to its performance, reliability, and flexibility. It can handle a large number of concurrent connections while maintaining low memory and CPU usage, making it a popular choice for high-traffic websites and applications. In the next section, we will build a small demo using Nginx to demonstrate how load balancing works. 5/5

Scaling Services › Horizontal Scaling

A Step-by-Step Guide to Distributing Traffic Across Web Servers

Open PDF

A Step-by-Step Guide to Distributing Traffic Across Web Servers Load balancing Codelab Let’s build a quick demo using NGINX to distribute traffic among three web servers. Using NGINX as a load balancer To follow along with this demo on your computer, you'll need to have Flask and NGINX installed. 1/3

Creating a Simple Flask Web Server First, let's create a simple Flask web server that returns "Hello, {port number}!" as a response: # demo.py from flask import Flask import sys app = Flask('demo') @app.route('/') def hello_world(): return f'Hello, {app.config["PORT"]}! ' if __name__ == '__main__': port = int(sys.argv[1]) if len(sys.argv) > 1 else 8000 app.config['PORT'] = port # Set the port in app config app.run(host='127.0.0.1', port=port) Now, we can start three server instances: python demo.py 8001 python demo.py 8002 python demo.py 8003 Configuring NGINX as a Load Balancer Next, we will configure NGINX as a load balancer to distribute traffic among the three web servers. Create a new configuration file /etc/nginx/conf.d/demo.conf: upstream demo { server localhost:8001; server localhost:8002; server localhost:8003; } server { listen 8080; location / { proxy_pass } } Restart the NGINX service using sudo systemctl restart nginx. Now, you should be able to access By default, the load balancing method is round-robin, so the responses from the load balancer should be "Hello, 8001!", "Hello, 8002!", and "Hello, 8003!" in sequence.. It should still be accessible and always return Hello, 8001!, the message returned by the first server. This is why load balancers and high availability are important: two web servers are down, and we are still serving requests from the remaining one without the user noticing any downtime. We can scale a service by adding more web servers and putting them behind load balancers. 2/3

Using NGINX as a load balancer with crashed servers A Note on Layer 4 vs Layer 7 Load Balancing If we get into the details of computer networking, there are two ways to load balance in terms of OSI networking model - Layer 4 and Layer 7 load balancing. The one we just discussed is Layer 7 load balancing. Layer 4 is the Transport Layer. Layer 4 load balancing is done on the packet-level and requires access to lower level routing devices. Layer 7 is Application Layer load balancing and is done entirely on the HTTP level. There are pros and cons of load balancing at each level. If you are interested, you can read more about it here. Software engineers mostly deal with Layer 7 since web applications we develop live on Layer 7. So don't worry about it if you don't understand the lower level computer networking terminologies (that's more of the jobs of network engineers and maybe SREs). In the following sections, we will explore the Layer 7 load balancing with Nginx. 3/3

Scaling Services › Horizontal Scaling

Auto Scaling

Open PDF

Auto Scaling In the previous sections, we've talked about how to distribute traffic through load balancers and how load balancers can help distribute traffic to multiple instances using pre-defined rules. However, what if the traffic is bursty or uneven through time? Imagine a social media platform that experiences a sudden spike in traffic during a popular event such as a sports game or an e-commerce website that experiences a sudden spike in traffic during a black friday sale. We could always have a fixed number of instances to handle the maximum traffic possible, but that would be wasteful. What we can do instead is to have a system that can automatically scale the number of instances up and down based on the current traffic or the predicted traffic. This is where auto scaling comes in. Auto Scaling is a cloud computing feature that automatically adjusts the number of computing resources (typically virtual machines or containers) based on the current demand. It works hand in hand with load balancers to ensure your application remains responsive and cost- efficient. How Auto Scaling Works The auto-scaling process begins with continuous monitoring of your application's metrics, including CPU utilization, memory usage, network traffic, request count, and any custom metrics you define. Based on predefined rules or policies, the system makes decisions about when to scale resources up or down. 1/5

When demand increases, the system initiates a scale-out (or scale-up) action to add more resources. Conversely, when demand decreases, it performs a scale-in (or scale-down) action to remove unnecessary resources. These scaling actions can take two primary forms: Horizontal scaling (Scale Out/In) involves adding or removing instances of resources, such as servers. This is the most common form of auto-scaling and is particularly well-suited for distributed applications. A typical example would be adding more web servers to handle increased traffic. Auto Scaling Groups (ASG) An Auto Scaling Group is a logical grouping of instances that share similar characteristics. It maintains a specified number of instances even if an instance becomes unhealthy. Here's how it works: 1. Desired Capacity: You set a desired number of instances that should be running 2. Minimum/Maximum Limits: You define boundaries for how small or large the group can scale 3. Health Checks: ASG continuously monitors instance health and automatically replaces failed instances 4. Scaling Policies: Define conditions for scaling (e.g., "add instance when CPU > 70%") 5. Launch Template: Specifies the instance configuration, i.e. what type of instance to launch 2/5

Auto Scaling Policies Auto scaling can be implemented through two main approaches: reactive and predictive scaling. Reactive scaling responds to current demand and scales based on real-time metrics, making it ideal for unpredictable workloads. Predictive scaling, on the other hand, uses machine learning to forecast demand and scales preemptively, making it more suitable for workloads with predictable patterns, such as daily or weekly cycles. A typical interview question is how to handle peak traffic during a black friday sale. The answer is to use predictive scaling to scale out the number of instances before the sale starts and scale in the number of instances after the sale ends. We can use previous year's data to predict the traffic during the black friday sale. 3/5

Benefits of Auto Scaling The implementation of auto scaling brings several key advantages to your infrastructure. From a cost perspective, you only pay for the resources you actually need, with automatic capacity reduction during low-demand periods. High availability is enhanced as the system maintains performance during traffic spikes and automatically replaces unhealthy instances. Users benefit from a consistent application experience with reduced latency, even during high- demand periods. Additionally, the automated nature of resource management significantly reduces the operational overhead and need for manual intervention. Best Practices When setting up your scaling rules, choose metrics that truly reflect your application's health and avoid configuring too-frequent scaling actions. Implement appropriate cooldown periods between scaling actions to prevent rapid oscillation between scaling up and down. It's crucial to thoroughly test your scaling policies by simulating different load scenarios and verifying that the scaling behavior works as expected. Regular monitoring of costs through billing alerts and periodic review of scaling patterns helps maintain optimal resource utilization. Integration with Load Balancers Auto scaling and load balancers work in tandem to provide a comprehensive scaling solution. When new instances are added, the auto scaling system automatically registers them with the load balancer, which then begins distributing traffic to these new instances. During instance removal, the system ensures connections are gracefully drained before removing instances from the load balancer. Other Use Cases While compute instances (VMs/containers) are the most common resources to auto-scale, modern cloud platforms have evolved to support auto-scaling across many different components of your infrastructure. Database resources can automatically scale to handle varying workloads. This includes dynamically adding or removing read replicas to distribute query load, adjusting storage capacity as data grows, scaling connection pools based on demand, and modifying query processing capacity to maintain performance. Network resources aren't left behind – they can scale automatically too. Load balancers can adjust their capacity based on traffic patterns, while network bandwidth and connection limits can flex to accommodate changing demands. API gateways can dynamically adjust their rate limits based on usage patterns and service capacity. 4/5

Serverless components are inherently scalable, with function concurrency adjusting automatically to handle incoming requests. Event processing systems can scale their processing capacity based on queue depth, and worker pools can grow or shrink based on workload. Cloud Provider Implementation Major cloud providers have their own implementations of auto scaling services. AWS offers Auto Scaling Groups, Azure provides Virtual Machine Scale Sets, and Google Cloud implements Managed Instance Groups. While each platform offers similar core functionality, they differentiate themselves through platform-specific features and integrations. 5/5

Scaling Services › Read Write Separation

Read-Write Separation

Open PDF

Read-Write Separation A system typically have different traffic patterns for read and write operations. For example, a social media platform might have millions of users scrolling through their feeds, but only a fraction actively creating new posts. Similarly, an e-commerce platform might see far more product browsing than actual purchases, and a content platform might have many more consumers than creators. An IOT device, one the other hand, might have a very high number of writes, but a low number of reads. This asymmetry in operation types creates an opportunity to optimize the system. We can handle read and write operations in different ways, and scale them independently. Database Replication The classic read-write separation pattern is database replication. In this model, a primary database takes responsibility for all write operations while multiple replica databases handle read queries. These replicas continuously synchronize with the primary, creating a distributed system that can handle significantly higher read loads. 1/3

This approach brings several advantages to the table. Beyond the obvious benefit of improved read performance through load distribution, it also enhances system availability and fault tolerance. If one replica fails, others can continue serving read requests while the failed instance recovers. Additionally, you can scale your read capacity horizontally by adding more replicas as needed. However, replication isn't without its challenges. One of the most significant issues is replication lag - the delay between when data is written to the primary and when it becomes available in the replicas. This lag can lead to temporary data inconsistencies where readers might see outdated information. Organizations must also prepare for primary database failures by implementing robust failover mechanisms. We will cover replication in more detail in the replication section. CQRS: Advanced Read-Write Separation For applications with more complex requirements, Command Query Responsibility Segregation (CQRS) offers a sophisticated approach to read-write separation. Rather than simply replicating data, CQRS separates the entire data access architecture into distinct command (write, i.e. Create, Update, Delete) and query (read, i.e. Read) paths. 2/3

In a CQRS architecture, write operations use a normalized data model optimized for consistency and data integrity. Meanwhile, read operations work with denormalized views specifically designed for efficient querying. These two models are kept in sync through asynchronous processes, allowing each side to be optimized independently. This separation enables remarkable flexibility in how data is stored and accessed. The write side might use a traditional relational database to ensure ACID compliance, while the read side could leverage a document database for faster queries. This flexibility makes CQRS particularly valuable for applications with complex domain models or unique performance requirements. 3/3

Scaling Services › Read Write Separation

CQRS Pattern in System Design

Open PDF

CQRS Pattern in System Design Command Query Responsibility Segregation (CQRS) Pattern Introduction and Motivation Since read and write typically have different traffic patterns, we can optimize the system by handling read and write operations in different ways, and scale them independently. The idea is similar to database replication except CQRS does it on the application level. How the CQRS Pattern Works Separating Commands and Queries CQRS draws a clear line between actions that change the state of the system—known as commands (mutations or CUD)—and operations that retrieve data—known as queries (reads). 1/5

Eventual Consistency A key component of many CQRS implementations is eventual consistency. When a command is executed, the system emits events that describe the change that just occurred. These events update read-optimized models asynchronously, ensuring that the write side is never slowed down by the need to immediately reflect these changes in query results. While this can lead to transient differences between the write and read representations, it allows each side to evolve independently and efficiently. Real-world Examples MySQL and Elasticsearch Implementation A classic combination for systems requiring both ACID compliance and full-text search: Command Side: MySQL handles transactional data: Order processing and inventory management User account operations Financial transactions Referential integrity enforcement Query Side: Elasticsearch powers search functionality: Full-text product search Complex filter combinations Faceted navigation Real-time search suggestions Analytics and reporting 2/5

DynamoDB with Different Access Patterns The command and query side can also be the same database, storing different tables. The query side is essentially a denormalized view of the data optimized for reads. A notification system using CQRS provides several key advantages: Command Side: Simple, fast writes for new notifications // notifications-write table - optimized for writes { "notification_id": "uuid- 123", "user_id": "user_789", "type": "friend_request", "sender_id": "user_456", "created_at": "2024-03-20T10:30:00Z", "status": "unread" } Fast inserts without complex data transformations No need to update aggregates on write Query Side: Pre-computed views for efficient reads // notifications-read table - optimized for reads { "user_id": "user_789#2024- 03", // Partition key for monthly feeds "unread_count": 5, "notifications": [ { "id": "uuid-123", "type": "friend_request", "sender": { "id": "user_456", "name": "John Doe", "avatar_url": " }, "created_at": "2024-03- 20T10:30:00Z", "status": "unread" } //... more notifications ] } Pre-joined data eliminates need for multiple queries Pre-calculated aggregates (unread_count) Optimized for specific access patterns Can scale reads independently 3/5

Without CQRS, you might face these challenges: 1. Complex writes that need to update multiple aggregates 2. Slow reads requiring joins across multiple records 3. Table design compromises trying to serve both write and read patterns 4. Scaling issues when read and write loads differ significantly With CQRS: 1. Write service focuses on recording notifications quickly 2. Async processor enriches data (e.g., fetching sender details) 3. Read model maintains pre-computed views 4. Each side can scale independently based on its specific load Pros and Cons of CQRS Advantages Faster Reads: The read model can be optimized specifically for queries by using denormalized data structures and pre-computed views. Since read and write operations are separated, each can be scaled independently based on their specific load patterns. This means heavy read traffic won't impact write performance and vice versa. Enables Complex Queries: CQRS allows using specialized databases for different purposes - for example, using Elasticsearch for full-text search while keeping transactional data in MySQL. This flexibility enables complex querying capabilities without compromising write performance. Decouples Read/Write Operations: Read and write models can evolve independently based on their specific requirements. Teams can choose the most appropriate technology for each side and optimize them separately, leading to better overall system performance. Drawbacks Complexity: Implementing CQRS adds significant complexity to the system. Teams need to maintain multiple models and handle synchronization between them. This complexity can make the system harder to understand and maintain. Eventual Consistency: Since updates to the read model happen asynchronously, there's always a period where the read side may show outdated data. Applications need to be designed with this in mind, and users need to understand that changes might not be immediately reflected. Infrastructure Overhead: Running multiple databases and message queues requires additional infrastructure and operational costs. Teams need to manage more components and handle the complexity of coordinating deployments across these different systems. 4/5

Lightweight CQRS Variants Not every CQRS deployment needs two databases and an asynchronous projection pipeline. In smaller systems the command and query sides often share the same datastore and keep their models in sync within a single transaction. This preserves strong consistency and reduces infrastructure overhead while still letting you maintain separate command/query models in the application layer. Teams frequently evolve from this lightweight form to the more common "commands in the OLTP store, queries in a read store" architecture only when scaling pressures or specialized query workloads justify the extra moving parts. Implementing CQRS on Popular Cloud Platforms On Azure, you might implement the write side with Azure Functions or Azure App Services to process commands, while publishing domain events to Azure Service Bus or Event Grid. The read side could use Azure Cosmos DB or Azure SQL Database for storing denormalized views, with another set of Functions handling event projections and maintaining query-optimized data structures. Amazon Web Services (AWS) With AWS, you can utilize AWS Lambda functions to handle commands and emit events via Amazon SNS or SQS. The read side might use Amazon DynamoDB, enhanced by Global Secondary Indexes, for fast queries. You can also incorporate Amazon ElastiCache or Amazon Elasticsearch Service to speed up queries, while AWS EventBridge helps coordinate event flows and updates. Google Cloud Platform (GCP) In GCP, Google Cloud Functions can process commands and publish events through Pub/Sub. These events trigger additional Cloud Functions to update read-optimized structures in Cloud Firestore or BigQuery. This combination allows you to achieve high query performance for analytics and reporting while keeping domain logic neatly separated. 5/5

Scaling Services › Caching

Time-Bounded Copies for System Design

Open PDF

Time-Bounded Copies for System Design Caching: The Mental Model A user opens a product page. The database query takes 50ms. A second later, a different user opens the same product. The database runs the identical query—same table, same row, same result. Thousands of users view that product every minute. Each request hits the database for the same data. Serving it from memory is orders of magnitude faster. The Speed Gap Understanding caching starts with understanding storage speed. The table below uses nanoseconds (1 ms = 1,000,000 ns). Each step down the hierarchy introduces a significant latency jump—sometimes 10x, sometimes orders of magnitude more. 1/5

Storage Hierarchy: Relative Access Time Each level is roughly 10-100x slower than the one above L1 Cache 0.5 ns 1x L2 Cache 7 ns 14x RAM 100 ns 200x SSD 150,000 ns 300,000x Disk (DB) 8,000,000 ns 16Mx Source: Peter Norvig, "Teach Yourself Programming in Ten Years" Operation Latency L1 cache reference 0.5 ns L2 cache reference 7 ns Main memory (RAM) reference100 ns Read 1 MB sequentially from memory250,000 ns Disk seek 8,000,000 ns Read 1 MB sequentially from disk20,000,000 ns Round-trip US to Europe 150,000,000 ns Source: Peter Norvig's latency numbers The gap between RAM and disk seek is five orders of magnitude. Serving data from memory rather than disk can increase throughput by orders of magnitude for the same hardware. This performance difference determines whether a system survives production load or collapses under it. Why Caching Works: Power Laws Caching would be useless if every request were unique. In many read-heavy workloads, access patterns follow power-law distributions—a small fraction of data serves most requests. 2/5

A video platform might host 10 million videos. The top 1,000 account for 60% of all views. An e- commerce site lists millions of products, but the homepage and top categories generate 80% of page loads. A social network stores billions of posts, but trending content drives most reads. This concentration means a small cache delivers outsized impact. In a highly skewed catalog, caching just the top 1,000 items out of 10 million could absorb the majority of reads. The exact savings depend on how steep the popularity curve is (Zipf distribution), but the principle holds: real-world popularity is uneven, not uniform. The Mental Model: Time-Bounded Copies At its core, a cache is a time-bounded copy of data stored closer to the requester. This definition has three parts worth examining. Copy. In typical cache-aside designs, the cache is not the source of truth—the database remains authoritative. The cache holds a duplicate that might diverge from the original after an update. Time-bounded. Cached entries don't live forever. Most expire after a set duration (TTL—time- to-live), while others are explicitly invalidated when the source data changes. Either way, the cache should eventually reflect the current state—though failed invalidations can leave stale entries behind. Without some expiration mechanism, stale data persists indefinitely. Closer to the requester. "Closer" means fewer network hops, faster storage, or both. A browser cache sits on the user's device—zero network latency. A CDN cache sits in a nearby data center—roughly 10ms instead of 150ms cross-continent. An application-level cache sits in RAM—an in-process cache responds in microseconds, a networked cache (Redis) in sub- millisecond to a few milliseconds, both far faster than a disk-bound database query. The mental model is straightforward: pay a small freshness cost to avoid repeatedly fetching the same data from a slow source. Cache Hit vs Cache Miss Cache Hit App Server 1. check Cache 2. return cached data ~0.5 ms (fast) Cache Miss App Server 1. check Cache (miss) 2. query Database 3. store in cache 4. return data ~50 ms (slow) 3/5

On a cache hit, the application reads from the cache and skips the database entirely. On a miss, it queries the database, stores the result for next time, then returns. The difference in latency drives the three benefits below. Three Benefits Caching provides three distinct advantages. Each matters for different reasons in system design interviews. Latency reduction. Users experience faster responses. A cache hit returns data in microseconds from memory. A cache miss hits the database at milliseconds. The difference compounds across a page load that makes dozens of backend calls. Shaving 40ms off each call saves nearly a full second on a page with 20 queries. Throughput increase. The database handles fewer requests. If 90% of reads hit the cache, a database rated for 5,000 queries per second effectively supports 50,000. The same hardware serves 10x more users without adding read replicas or upgrading instances. Cost reduction. Fewer database queries means less compute and I/O. A system that would need 10 read replicas at full load might need 2 when caching absorbs most reads. Memory is expensive per gigabyte, but a few gigabytes of cache replacing terabytes of repeated disk I/O is a net savings. In interviews, frame caching decisions around which of these three benefits matters most for the scenario. A real-time game prioritizes latency. A high-traffic API prioritizes throughput. A cost-sensitive startup prioritizes infrastructure savings. The following simulator shows how cache hit rate affects average latency and effective database load. Try different hit rates to see why even modest caching delivers large gains. Cache Hit Rate Impact def simulate_cache_impact(hit_rate_percent): """Show how cache hit rate affects latency and DB load.""" hit_rate = hit_rate_percent / 100.0 # Example latencies (illustrative, not benchmarks) cache_latency_ms = 0.5 # Networked cache hop (e.g., Redis over LAN) db_latency_ms = 50.0 # Database query (disk I/O + query processing) # Average latency = (hit_rate * cache_latency) + (miss_rate * db_latency) avg_latency = (hit_rate * cache_latency_ms) + ((1 - hit_rate) * db_latency_ms) # If system gets 10,000 requests/sec, how many hit the DB? db_queries = int(total_rps * (1 - hit_rate)) print(f"=== Cache Hit Rate: {hit_rate_percent}% ===") print(f" Average latency: {avg_latency:.1f} ms") print(f" DB queries (of {total_rps:,} req/s): {db_queries:,}/s") print(f" DB load reduction: {hit_rate_percent}%") 4/5

# Compare different hit rates for rate in [0, 50, 80, 90, 95, 99]: simulate_cache_impact(rate) Loading Python environment... This may take a few seconds on first load. The Freshness Trade-off Every cache introduces a fundamental tension: speed versus freshness. Serving a cached copy is fast, but the copy might be stale. The database could have been updated a second ago, and the cache still holds the old value. This trade-off is acceptable for most read-heavy workloads. A product description cached for 5 minutes is fine—product descriptions rarely change. A user's notification count cached for 60 seconds is tolerable—slight delay is acceptable. A stock price cached for 5 minutes is dangerous—financial data demands real-time accuracy. The question is never "should we cache?" but "how stale can this data be?" Different data has different staleness tolerances. The caching section that follows teaches patterns for managing this trade-off at each layer of the system. What's Ahead This section covers caching from the ground up. The full caching hierarchy—browser, CDN, application, database—maps out how each layer applies. From there, the section progresses through read and write patterns, consistency challenges, scaling strategies, and failure modes. Each article builds on the previous one. By the end, you'll have a complete mental toolkit for caching decisions in any system design interview. 5/5

Scaling Services › Caching

Browser CDN Application and Database Layers

Open PDF

Browser, CDN, Application, and Database Layers The Multi-Layer Defense The previous article established caching as time-bounded copies stored closer to the requester. But "closer" can mean different things. A copy on the user's device is closer than one in a nearby data center, which is closer than one on the origin server. Each layer intercepts requests at a different point, and each trades freshness for a different amount of speed. The Four Layers A request travels through up to four caching layers before reaching the database. Each layer can short-circuit the journey by returning a cached response. 1/7

Caching Tiers: From User to Database User Browser Cache ~0 ms CDN / Edge Cache 5-30 ms Application Cache 1-5 ms Database 10-100 ms Static assets, HTTP responses Images, videos, static pages Query results, session data Source of truth Request travels left → right. Each layer can short-circuit the journey. ← Faster (closer to user) Fresher (source of truth) → The layers closest to the user are fastest but hardest to invalidate. The layers closest to the database are freshest but add the most latency. Understanding which layer to use—and which to skip—is a core system design skill. Browser Cache The browser stores HTTP responses locally on the user's device. When a user revisits a page, the browser checks its local cache before making any network request. A cache hit costs zero network latency. The server controls browser caching through HTTP headers. The Cache-Control header specifies how long a response stays valid. A header like Cache-Control: max-age=3600 tells the browser to reuse the response for one hour without contacting the server. 2/7

Browser Cache: Two Strategies Cache-Control: max-age "Reuse for N seconds, don't ask server" Browser Local Cache app.a3f2b1.js No network request ~0 ms latency Best for: JS bundles, CSS, images, fonts Content-hashed URLs: app.a3f2b1.js ETag Revalidation "Ask server: has this changed?" Browser Server If-None-Match: "abc" 304 Not Modified Network round-trip, no body Saves bandwidth, confirms freshness Best for: API responses, HTML pages Data that changes unpredictably Browser caching works best for static assets: JavaScript bundles, CSS files, images, and fonts. These change only during deployments. A version hash in the URL (e.g., app.a3f2b1.js) lets you cache aggressively—set max-age to a year, and the filename changes when content changes. Browser caching is trickier for dynamic data. A user's notification count changes every few seconds. The shopping cart updates on every add. Short TTLs or ETag revalidation (where the browser asks "has this changed?" and the server responds with a lightweight 304 Not Modified) can help, but for rapidly changing state the request typically travels deeper. CDN / Edge Cache A CDN (Content Delivery Network) stores content on servers distributed across geographic regions. When a user in Tokyo requests an image, the CDN serves it from a Tokyo edge server instead of the origin in Virginia. Latency drops from roughly 150ms to under 10ms. CDNs cache at the HTTP layer. When an edge server receives a request it hasn't seen, it fetches from the origin, stores the response, and serves future requests locally. The origin sets caching rules through the same Cache-Control headers the browser uses. 3/7

CDN Edge: Cache Hit vs Cache Miss Hit: Edge has content User GET /img.png CDN Edge cached copy found 5-30 ms Origin (not contacted) Miss: Edge fetches from origin User GET /img.png CDN Edge not in cache 2. fetch from origin Origin 3. response + store in edge cache4. return 100-200 ms (origin RTT)Next request → cache hit CDNs excel at static content at scale: images, videos, downloadable files, and pre-rendered pages. They also handle traffic spikes—edge servers can absorb the vast majority of requests when hit rates are high. CDNs struggle with personalized or rapidly changing content. A page that shows the user's name, cart items, or real-time prices can't be cached at the CDN without careful partitioning. That boundary becomes clearer in the CDN vs application cache discussion. Application Cache The application cache is an in-memory data store (like Redis or Memcached) that sits between the application servers and the database. This is the layer most relevant to system design interviews. When an application server needs data, it checks the cache first. On a hit, it reads from memory—typically sub-millisecond to a few milliseconds over the network. On a miss, it queries the database, stores the result in cache, and returns it. Subsequent requests for the same data hit the cache. 4/7

Application Cache: Check Cache Before Database App Server needs user:42 1. check In-Memory Cache user:42 → {name, prefs} product:99 → {title, price} feed:42 → [post_ids...] Hit → return in <1 ms 2. miss → query DB Database 3. store result in cache for next time Stores dynamic data: user profiles, sessions, feeds — what CDNs can't cache Application caching handles what CDNs struggle with: dynamic, personalized data with complex access patterns. User profiles, product details, session state, feed timelines, search results—all fit naturally into an application cache. The application cache is also the layer you control most precisely. You decide what to cache, when to expire it, and how to invalidate it on writes. This flexibility comes with complexity. The articles on read patterns, write patterns, and consistency explore these decisions in depth. Database Buffers Database Buffer Pool: The Invisible Cache Database Engine Query Buffer Pool (RAM) Page 1 Page 2 Page 3 hot hot warm ~0.1 ms (memory read) Disk ~8 ms (disk seek) miss Managed automatically by the DB — "warm" DB = working set fits in buffer pool Databases maintain their own internal caches. A relational database keeps frequently accessed pages in a buffer pool (a region of RAM). When a query touches data already in the buffer pool, it reads from memory rather than disk. This layer is largely invisible to application developers. The database manages it automatically. But it matters for capacity planning—a database with enough RAM to hold its working set in the buffer pool performs dramatically better than one that constantly reads from disk. 5/7

You rarely configure this layer in system design interviews. But understanding it explains why a "cold" database (just restarted, empty buffer pool) is slower than a "warm" one, and why cache failures that redirect traffic to the database cause cascading slowdowns. Layers Compose In production, multiple layers work together. A typical web request might proceed: 1. Browser checks its local cache → miss (first visit) 2. CDN edge server checks its cache → hit for static assets, miss for personalized API calls 3. Application server checks the in-memory cache → hit for popular data 4. Database query (only if all layers miss) → reads from buffer pool or disk Each layer reduces the load on layers below it. The browser absorbs repeat visits. The CDN absorbs geographic distribution. The application cache absorbs hot data. The database handles only cold or freshly written data. The power of layered caching is multiplicative. If the browser catches 30% of requests, the CDN catches 40% of what remains, and the application cache catches 90% of what passes through—the database sees only 4.2% of original traffic. Layered Filtering: 100K req/s → 4.2K to Database 100,000 req/s (incoming) 70,000 req/s ← Browser absorbed 3 42,000 req/s ← CDN absorbed 40% 4,200 ← App cache absorbed 90% DB Traffic narrows at each layer Choosing the Right Layer Not every system needs all four layers. The choice depends on your data and access patterns. Static, public, rarely changing: Browser + CDN. Marketing pages, product images, documentation. Dynamic, read-heavy, tolerates seconds of staleness: Application cache. User profiles, product catalogs, feed rankings. 6/7

Dynamic, user-specific, low latency required: Application cache with short TTL (time-to- live). Session data, notification counts, dashboard metrics. Frequently changing, consistency-critical: Skip caching or use very short TTL with active invalidation. Inventory counts, account balances, real-time bidding. From there, the boundary between CDN caching and application caching becomes the central architectural decision for read-heavy systems. 7/7

Scaling Services › Caching

Choosing the Right Caching Layer

Open PDF

Choosing the Right Caching Layer The Edge: CDN vs Application Cache The previous article mapped four caching layers. In practice, the critical architectural decision is the boundary between CDN caching and application caching. Both reduce origin load. On a cache hit, both serve without hitting the origin database. But they solve different problems, and choosing wrong means either serving stale personalized data or wasting infrastructure on requests a CDN could have absorbed. The Geographic Problem A user in Tokyo requests a product image. Without a CDN, the request travels to the origin server in Virginia—a round trip of roughly 150ms dictated by the speed of light through fiber. With a CDN, the request hits a Tokyo edge server and returns at local-network latency. 1/5

CDN Edge vs Direct Origin Without CDN: Every request travels to origin User Tokyo ~150 ms round-trip Origin (Virginia) With CDN: Edge serves from nearby cache User Tokyo ~10 ms CDN Edge (Tokyo) cache miss only Origin (Virginia) Latency improvement: 150 ms → 10 ms (15x faster) Origin only handles cache misses, not every request This latency gap is physics, not engineering. No amount of server optimization eliminates the speed-of-light delay across the Pacific. A CDN solves it by placing cached copies at the network edge, geographically close to users. What CDNs Handle Well CDNs cache at the HTTP level. They store complete HTTP responses (headers + body) keyed primarily by URL (plus host and selected headers via Vary). This works naturally for content that is: Public and identical for all users. A product image, a JavaScript bundle, a marketing page. Every user receives the same bytes. Each CDN edge stores a local copy and serves it to nearby users without hitting the origin. Rarely changing. A CSS file changes on deployment, not between requests. The CDN can cache it for days or weeks. When it changes, you deploy with a new filename (content-hashed URLs) so the old cache is never stale—it's simply never requested again. Tolerance for staleness. A blog post cached for 5 minutes at the edge means a typo fix takes 5 minutes to propagate globally. For most static content, this is acceptable. CDNs are the right choice for images, videos, fonts, compiled assets, documentation pages, and any content where the response is the same regardless of who requested it. Where CDNs Fall Short CDNs struggle when responses vary by user, session, or real-time state. 2/5

Personalized responses. A user's feed, cart, or notification count differs per user. Caching at the CDN means either serving everyone the same generic response (losing personalization) or maintaining billions of per-user cache entries at every edge location (impractical). High cardinality keys. A search API with millions of possible query combinations has a poor cache hit rate at any single edge location. The edge server in Tokyo might see each unique query only once, making caching pointless. Consistency-sensitive data. Inventory counts, auction bids, and account balances need real- time accuracy. Even a 5-second CDN TTL means a user might buy an item that's already sold out. For these cases, the application cache is the right layer. It sits behind the CDN, closer to the application servers, and caches at the data level rather than the HTTP level. Application Cache: Data-Level Caching An application cache (typically an in-memory store like Redis or Memcached) stores individual data objects—a user record, a product's metadata, a computed feed—rather than complete HTTP responses. HTTP Cache (CDN) vs Data Cache (Application) HTTP Cache (CDN) Caches complete responses, keyed by URL /products/99 → full HTML page /api/products/99 → full JSON response Limitation: Price changes → must invalidate both cached URLs separately 1 URL = 1 cache entry Different URLs can't share data Data Cache (App) Caches data objects, keyed by entity product:99 → {title, price, description, images} Serves multiple responses: Product page Search results Cart summary Recommendations 1 invalidation updates all pages This distinction matters. An HTTP-cached page couples the cache key to the URL. A data- cached object can be assembled into any response. The same cached user profile serves the profile page, the settings page, and the activity feed. One invalidation updates all three. Application caching handles what CDNs cannot: per-user data, complex aggregations, and computed results that don't map cleanly to a single URL. 3/5

Origin Shielding When a CDN cache misses, it fetches from the origin. If you have 200 edge servers worldwide and a popular page expires simultaneously at all of them, 200 requests hit your origin at once. With thousands of URLs, this becomes a continuous barrage. Origin Shielding Without Shield: N edges → N origin requests Edge Tokyo Edge London Edge Sydney... Edge NYC Origin With Shield: N edges → 1 shield → 1 origin request Edge Tokyo Edge London Edge Sydney... Edge NYC Shield Origin 1 req Origin shielding adds a caching layer between the CDN edge and your origin. Instead of 200 edge servers each fetching from your origin, they route through a designated shield POP (or small set of shield POPs). The shield either has the content cached or fetches it from the origin once per object. The result: your origin sees far fewer requests—often close to one per object per shield— instead of one per edge. The shield absorbs the fan-in from global edge servers. This is particularly valuable during cache expiration events or traffic spikes when many edges simultaneously discover their caches are stale. Most CDN providers offer origin shielding as a configuration option. In system design interviews, mention it when discussing CDN architecture for high-traffic systems—it demonstrates awareness of the thundering herd problem at the edge layer. Decision Framework Use this framework to decide which layer handles each type of data: Data Type CDN App Cache Why Static assets (JS, CSS, images) Yes No Same for all users, changes rarely 4/5

Data Type CDN App Cache Why Public pages (marketing, docs) Yes No Identical responses, tolerate staleness API responses (personalized)No Yes Vary by user, need data-level control Hot database objects No Yes Fine-grained invalidation needed Semi-static API (product catalog) Yes Yes CDN for pages, app cache for partial updates "Yes" in the CDN column assumes the response is public and identical for all users. Personalized or auth-gated responses typically bypass the CDN (or require careful cache-key variation) even if the data type seems static. For most systems, the split is straightforward: CDNs for static assets and public pages, application cache for everything else. The interesting cases are hybrid—a product page where the static layout shell is CDN-cached but personalized fields (price, inventory, recommendations) are fetched separately from the application cache. This "fragment" approach lets each piece expire independently: the shell lives at the CDN for hours while dynamic data refreshes every few seconds from the app cache. What's Next The previous articles covered what caching is and where caches live. The remaining articles dive into implementation: how data enters the cache (read patterns), how it stays fresh (write patterns and invalidation), and what happens when things go wrong (failure modes). From here, cache key design shows how to structure the keys that identify cached data. 5/5

Scaling Services › Caching

Cache Key Design

Open PDF

Cache Key Design The previous articles covered where caches live. Now the question shifts to implementation: when you store data in the cache, what do you use as the key? A poorly designed key leads to collisions (two pieces of data overwriting each other), stale reads after schema changes, and debugging nightmares in production. Key Structure A cache key is a string that uniquely identifies a cached value. The simplest approach—using the database primary key directly—fails quickly. If both your user service and your product service cache an item with ID 42, they collide. One overwrites the other. A structured key solves this. The standard pattern uses colon-separated segments: Cache Key Anatomy user : v2 : profile : 42 Namespace prevents collisions between services Version invalidates on schema change Entity what type of data is cached Identifier which specific record user:v2:profile:42 → {"name": "Alice", "email": "..."} Each segment serves a purpose: 1/3

Namespace isolates different services or domains. A user service uses user:, a product service uses product:. Even within a shared cache cluster, keys never collide across services. Version enables clean schema migrations (explained in the next section). Entity identifies what type of data this key holds. A user service might cache profiles, sessions, and preferences separately: user:v1:profile:42, user:v1:session:42, user:v1:prefs:42. Identifier pinpoints the specific record. Usually the database primary key or a unique identifier like an email or slug. Versioning Schema changes break caches silently. Your user profile cached as {name, email} gains an avatar field in the next deploy. Old cached entries lack this field. The application reads stale data missing the avatar, and users see broken UI until the cache entry expires. Key Versioning: Schema Change Without Stale Data Before (v1 schema) user:v1:profile:42 → {name, email} Deploy: add "avatar" field After (v2 schema) user:v2:profile:42 → {name, email, avatar} v1 keys expire naturally (never read) New code reads v2 keys only — no stale v1 data served, no cache flush needed Embedding a version in the key solves this cleanly. When the schema changes, bump the version: user:v1:profile:42 becomes user:v2:profile:42. New code reads only v2 keys. Old v1 entries sit untouched until their TTL expires and the cache evicts them. No cache flush required. No stale data served. This approach costs slightly more memory during the transition period (both v1 and v2 entries coexist). In practice, the overlap is brief—old entries expire within minutes or hours depending on TTL. Key Size Cache keys consume memory. Every key is stored alongside its value. A 200-byte key on a million entries costs at least 200MB for raw key bytes, plus per-entry metadata and allocator overhead (often substantially more). As a rule of thumb, keep keys relatively short—on the order of 100 bytes or less. Practical guidelines: 2/3

Keep keys short but readable. u:v2:prof:42 saves bytes but makes debugging harder. user:v2:profile:42 is clear and still compact. Avoid embedding large values in keys. A search query like search:v1:results: {"query":"long user input...","filters": {"category":"electronics","price_min":10}} is fragile and wastes space. Hash the query parameters instead: search:v1:results:sha256_prefix. Use consistent separators. Colons (:) are the convention in Redis and most caching libraries. Avoid spaces, newlines, or characters that need escaping. Common Patterns Different data types suggest different key structures: Use Case Key Pattern Example Entity by ID service:version:entity:id user:v2:profile:42 Computed result service:version:computation:input_hashsearch:v1:results:a7f3b2 Per-user stateservice:version:user:uid:entitycart:v1:user:42:items Sorted collection service:version:entity:sort_keyfeed:v1:timeline:42 Configurationconfig:version:key config:v3:feature_flags The pattern stays consistent: namespace the service, version the schema, identify the entity, then the specific record. What Can Go Wrong Two failure modes appear most in production: Key collisions. Two different pieces of data map to the same key. This happens when keys are too generic (cache:42) or when hash functions produce duplicates. The fix is explicit namespacing—always include the service and entity type. Unbounded key spaces. A search cache that stores results for every unique query grows without limit. If users can type arbitrary queries, the key space is infinite. The fix is eviction policies (covered in a later article) combined with careful TTLs—cache search results for minutes, not hours. From there, read patterns show how the application decides when to read from cache versus database, and how to populate the cache on a miss. 3/3

Scaling Services › Caching

Read-Through

Open PDF

Read-Through Read Patterns: Fetching Data You have a cache, structured keys, and data in the database. The question now: who is responsible for populating the cache? When a request arrives and the cache is empty, something must fetch from the database and store the result. The two main patterns differ in where that responsibility lives. Cache-Aside (Lazy Loading) In cache-aside, the application manages all cache interactions explicitly. The application code checks the cache, handles misses by querying the database, and writes results back to the cache. 1/4

Cache-Aside: Application Manages the Cache App Server 1. check Cache 2a. hit → return data 2b. miss Database 3. app queries DB 4. app stores result in cache Request Response / write-back The application controls all cache logic — when to read, store, and invalidate The sequence on every read: 1. Application checks the cache for the key 2. Hit: return the cached value directly 3. Miss: application queries the database 4. Application writes the result into the cache 5. Application returns the result to the caller The application owns the entire flow. The cache is a passive data store—it doesn't know anything about the database. This is the most common pattern in production systems. Strengths. The application controls exactly what gets cached. Only data that is actually requested enters the cache (no wasted memory on unused entries). The cache layer is simple —any key-value store works. If the cache goes down, the application falls back to the database directly. Weakness. Cache logic spreads across the codebase. Every data access point needs the same check-cache → miss → query-DB → store pattern. This creates duplication unless you abstract it into a helper function. In read-through, the cache itself handles misses. The application simply asks the cache for data. If the cache doesn't have it, the cache fetches from the database using a configured "loader" function. 2/4

Read-Through: Cache Manages Itself App Server 1. get(key) Cache (with data loader) hit? → return miss? → fetch from DB 2. fetch Database 3. return data App only knows "get(key)" — cache handles miss logic internally Requires a cache library with a "loader" function (e.g., Guava LoadingCache, Caffeine) The sequence on every read: 1. Application calls cache.get(key) 2. Cache checks its store. Hit: return immediately 3. Miss: cache calls the loader function, which queries the database 4. Cache stores the result and returns it to the application The application only knows get(key). It never interacts with the database for cached data types. The cache library handles miss logic, storage, and expiration internally. Strengths. Application code is cleaner—no cache-miss handling at each call site. The cache handles misses consistently across all call sites. Some implementations coalesce concurrent misses for the same key (preventing the thundering herd problem where, say, 100 requests all query the database for the same missing entry). Weakness. Requires a cache library that supports loader functions (e.g., Guava LoadingCache, Caffeine in Java, or custom wrappers). The cache layer is more complex—it now has a dependency on the database. If the loader fails, the cache must handle errors rather than just returning "miss." When to Use Which Consideration Cache-Aside Read-Through Simplicity of cache layer Simple (passive store) Complex (needs loader config) Application code More verbose (miss handling everywhere) Cleaner (just get(key)) Cache failure behaviorApp falls back to DB naturallyMust handle loader errors 3/4

Consideration Cache-Aside Read-Through Concurrent miss protection Manual (you build it) Often built-in (request coalescing) Technology requirements Any key-value store Cache library with loader support Most distributed systems use cache-aside because it works with any cache backend (Redis, Memcached, or a simple in-process map) and keeps the cache layer generic and DB-unaware. The application retains full control over loading logic. Read-through shines in applications with many data access points where duplicating miss- handling logic would be error-prone. It's common in monolithic Java applications using Guava or Caffeine, where the cache is in-process and the loader function is just a method call. In system design interviews, default to cache-aside unless asked specifically about read- through. Most architectural diagrams assume the application manages cache interactions. This is implemented in a hands-on lab showing the mechanics of check → miss → store in working code. 4/4

Cache Lab Implement Cache-Aside Read Pattern

Cache Lab: Implement Cache-Aside Read Pattern Lab: The Read Drill The previous article explained cache-aside and read-through as concepts. This lab implements cache-aside from scratch so you can see the hit/miss mechanics in action. The Exercise Implement a get_user() function that follows the cache-aside pattern: 1. Check the cache for the key 2. On hit: return cached data (fast path) 3. On miss: query the "database," store in cache, return (slow path) The code below simulates a database with artificial latency. Run it to observe how repeated reads shift from slow (miss) to fast (hit). Cache-Aside: get_user() Implementation # Simulated database (slow — each read costs 50ms) 1: {"name": "Alice", "email": "alice@example.com", "role": "admin"}, 2: {"name": "Bob", "email": "bob@example.com", "role": "user"}, 3: {"name": "Charlie", "email": "charlie@example.com", "role": "user"}, """Simulate a slow database read.""" time.sleep(0.05) # 50ms latency return DATABASE.get(user_id) 1/2

# The cache (a simple dictionary) """Cache-aside: check cache first, fall back to DB on miss.""" key = f"user:v1:profile:{user_id}" # Step 1: Check cache return cache[key], "HIT" # Step 2: Cache miss — query database data = db_query(user_id) return None, "MISS (not found)" # Step 3: Store in cache for next time return data, "MISS (stored)" # --- Run the experiment --- print("=== First Round: All Misses (cold cache) ===") Observe how the first round takes ~50ms per unique user (database read), while the second round completes nearly instantly (memory lookup). The first three calls in round one are misses. Calls 4-6 in round one are hits because users 1, 2, 3 were already cached. Try It: Cache Invalidation What happens when data changes?
