The write-pattern section explores strategies for keeping the cache consistent when data changes, without requiring manual invalidation at every write. 2/2

Scaling Services › Caching

Write-Through

Open PDF

Write-Through Write Patterns: Mutating Data The previous articles covered how data enters the cache on read. But reads only solve half the problem. When the application writes new data or updates existing records, the cache and database can diverge. The write pattern determines how—and when—those two stores synchronize. In write-through, every write goes to both the cache and the database as a single synchronous operation. The application sends the write to the cache layer. The cache stores the value and immediately forwards it to the database. Only after the database confirms does the cache acknowledge success to the application. Write-Through: Synchronous Write to Both App Server 1. write Cache 2. write (sync) Database 3. DB confirms4. ack Total latency = cache write + DB write (synchronous) 1/4

This keeps cache and database synchronized on every successful write. Subsequent reads typically find fresh data in the cache while the entry remains cached—though eviction, TTL, or node failures can still cause misses. The cost is write latency. Every write now pays two round trips: one to the cache, one to the database. The extra hop adds a millisecond or more to each write. For read-heavy workloads with occasional writes, this tradeoff is acceptable. For write-heavy workloads, the added latency compounds. There is also a cache pollution problem. Write-through stores every written value in cache, regardless of whether anyone reads it. An analytics pipeline writing thousands of events per second fills the cache with entries that no read request ever touches. Memory fills with write- once data while frequently-read entries get evicted. Amazon DAX. DynamoDB Accelerator implements write-through transparently. The application uses a compatible API—DAX performs write-through, updating the cache and forwarding to DynamoDB. Reads hit DAX first. Write-Behind (Write-Back) In write-behind, the cache acknowledges writes immediately and flushes to the database later. The application writes to the cache. The cache acknowledges the write quickly. A background process batches cached writes and persists them to the database on a schedule or when a threshold is reached. Write-Behind: Async Flush to Database Immediate (fast path) App Server 1. write Cache 2. ack (instant) Later (background flush) Write Queue (batched writes) 3. flush Database Risk: crash before flush = data loss Write latency drops dramatically. The application waits only for the cache acknowledgment (typically much faster than a database round trip). Under heavy write bursts, the cache absorbs the spike and smooths it into steady database writes. 2/4

Batching provides a second benefit: write coalescing. For overwrite-style updates where intermediate states don't matter, if the same key updates 10 times in one second, only the final value needs to reach the database. Instead of 10 database writes, the flush sends 1. This reduces database load significantly for frequently-updated counters, session data, or user presence indicators. The risk is data loss. In the simplest form, between the cache acknowledging the write and the background flush completing, data exists only in memory. If the cache node crashes during this window, unflushed writes are lost. Replication or durable write-ahead logging can mitigate this at the cost of added latency. Write-behind fits workloads where speed matters more than durability: session stores, view counters, real-time analytics, recommendation scores. It does not fit financial transactions, order processing, or any data where loss is unacceptable. Write-Around In write-around, writes go directly to the database, bypassing the cache entirely. The application invalidates (deletes) the cache entry for the affected key. The next read triggers a cache miss, fetches fresh data from the database, and repopulates the cache. Write-Around: Bypass Cache on Write App Server 1. write to DB Database 2. delete key Cache ✗ Next read: 3. miss → fetch from DB Only read data enters cache — no pollution from writes This is the write-side partner of cache-aside. The application manages the cache explicitly: invalidate on write, populate on read. No write ever enters the cache directly. Write-around avoids the cache pollution problem. Only data that is actually read enters the cache. A batch import of 100,000 records does not touch cache memory. Only the records that users subsequently request get cached. The tradeoff is a cache miss on the next read after every write (assuming the invalidation succeeds). The user who updates their profile will experience one slow read (cache miss + database fetch) on the next page load. For most applications, this single-miss penalty is 3/4

negligible compared to the memory savings. Choosing a Write Pattern The choice depends on your consistency requirements and write volume. ConsiderationWrite-Through Write-Behind Write-Around Consistency Strong (synchronous)Eventual (async flush) Strong (invalidate + refetch) Write latency Higher (cache + DB) Lowest (cache only) Normal (DB only) Data loss riskNone Yes (crash before flush) None Cache pollution Yes (all writes cached)Yes (all writes cached) No (only reads populate) Best for Read-heavy, consistency- critical Write-heavy, loss- tolerant Mixed workloads, large writes Many production systems use write-around (cache-aside for writes). It keeps the cache lean, avoids data loss risk, and pairs naturally with the cache-aside read pattern from the previous article. Write-through adds value when reads of recently-written data are frequent and consistency is critical. Write-behind is reserved for high-throughput scenarios where the durability tradeoff is explicitly accepted. When these patterns fail, the consistency problems that arise from writing to two systems (cache and database) become the focus. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

The Dual-Write Problem

Open PDF

The Dual-Write Problem The Consistency Problem Write-around deletes the cache key after writing to the database. Write-through updates both synchronously. Both seem correct. But when multiple requests hit the system concurrently, subtle race conditions leave stale data in the cache—sometimes indefinitely. Any system with a cache and a database maintains two copies of the same data. A write must update both. The question is: in what order, and what happens if something fails between the two operations? Consider an application using write-around. The write flow is: (1) update the database, (2) delete the cache key. Two things can go wrong. Crash between steps. The application updates the database but crashes before deleting the cache key. The cache now holds the old value. Every subsequent read returns stale data until the entry expires via TTL or another write invalidates it. Concurrent read during write. This is more subtle and harder to prevent. 1/4

Race Condition: Concurrent Read + Write Thread A (write) Thread B (read) State 1. Cache miss 2. Query DB (reads old value) 3. Write new value to DB DB: new 4. Delete cache key Cache: empty 5. Store OLD value in cache Cache: old (STALE!) Cache holds stale data until TTL expires or next write Thread B's read started before Thread A's write committed time The sequence: Thread A updates the database (user email changes to new@example.com). Thread B reads the cache, finds a miss, and queries the database—but its query started before Thread A's write committed. Thread B gets the old value (old@example.com). Thread A deletes the cache key. Thread B writes the old value into the cache. The cache now contains stale data that persists until the next invalidation. This race exists regardless of which write pattern you use. It happens whenever a read-miss and a write overlap in time. Delete vs Update: Why Invalidation Wins When a write occurs, the application has two choices for the cache: 1. Update the cache with the new value (write-through approach) 2. Delete the cache key and let the next read repopulate (write-around approach) Updating sounds better—the cache is always fresh. But updating introduces a new race condition. 2/4

Delete vs Update: Out-of-Order Writes Update Cache (risky) Write A: price=100 Write B: price=120 DB commits: A then B DB = 120 (correct) Cache updates arrive: B then A cache ← 120 cache ← 100 Cache = 100 (WRONG) Delete Key (safe) Write A: price=100 Write B: price=120 DB commits: A then B DB = 120 (correct) Both delete key (order irrelevant) del key (idempotent) Next read → DB → 120 (CORRECT) Deletion is idempotent and order-independent. Updates require ordering guarantees. Suppose two writes arrive in quick succession: Write A sets price=100, Write B sets price=120. Both update the database (B wins because it commits last). Both update the cache. But if Write A's cache update arrives after Write B's, the cache ends up with price=100 while the database has price=120. The cache is wrong. A TTL will eventually expire the stale entry, but without one the error persists until the next write. Deletion avoids this entirely. If both writes delete the key, the cache is simply empty. The next read fetches the correct value (120) from the database. Deletion is idempotent—deleting a key that's already gone is a no-op. Updating is order-dependent—the last update wins, and "last" in a distributed system is hard to guarantee. This is why many systems prefer delete-on-write (write-around) over update-on-write for simplicity. Mitigation Strategies No pattern eliminates the consistency gap completely in a distributed system. But several strategies reduce the window and impact. Short TTLs. Every cache entry expires after a bounded time (seconds to minutes). Even if a stale write sneaks in, it self-corrects when the TTL expires. The tradeoff: shorter TTLs mean more cache misses and higher database load. Write-through for critical data. For data where staleness is unacceptable (account balances, inventory counts), use write-through. The synchronous double-write reduces the inconsistency window significantly, though partial failures can still cause divergence. The cost is write latency. 3/4

Delayed double-delete. After writing to the database and deleting the cache key, wait a short interval (e.g., 500ms) and delete the key again. This catches the race where a concurrent read repopulates the cache with stale data during the write. The second delete clears the stale entry. Version stamps. Attach a version number or timestamp to each cache entry. On write, increment the version. On cache population, only store the value if the version is newer than what's already cached. This prevents old values from overwriting new ones. The complexity is maintaining consistent version ordering across services. The Consistency Spectrum Different data types tolerate different staleness windows. Data Type Acceptable StalenessPattern Account balance None Write-through or skip cache Product price Seconds Write-around + short TTL User profile Minutes Write-around + moderate TTL Recommendation scoresHours Write-behind + long TTL Static content Days CDN with explicit purge The right answer is rarely "make everything consistent." Strong consistency costs write latency and system complexity. Choose the staleness tolerance for each data type and apply the simplest pattern that meets it. This leads to a hands-on lab implementing a write-behind worker. You'll see write coalescing in action—and what happens when the worker crashes before flushing. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

Write-Behind Worker with Crash Simulation

Open PDF

Write-Behind Worker with Crash Simulation Lab: The Write Drill The previous article explained why caches and databases can disagree. This lab implements a write-behind worker from scratch so you can see write coalescing and the data loss risk in working code. Exercise 1: Write-Behind Worker Implement a write-behind cache that buffers writes in memory and flushes to the "database" in batches. The flush happens when the buffer reaches a threshold or when you trigger it manually. Write-Behind: Buffered Flush Notice how all 5 writes complete instantly (sub-millisecond each). The flush happens once and writes all pending entries to the database. During the window between writes and flush, the database is stale—but reads from the cache always return the latest value. Exercise 2: Write Coalescing When the same key is written multiple times before a flush, only the final value needs to reach the database. This reduces database writes dramatically for frequently-updated keys. 1/2

Write Coalescing: Same Key, One DB Write The buffer uses a dictionary instead of a list. Each new write to the same key overwrites the previous value. On flush, only one database write per key is needed regardless of how many times it was updated. Exercise 3: Crash Simulation The risk of write-behind is data loss. If the process crashes before flushing, buffered writes are gone. This exercise simulates that failure. Crash Before Flush: Data Loss The crash wipes the in-memory buffer. All writes between the last flush and the crash are permanently lost. The mitigation—flushing more frequently—reduces the window of vulnerability but never eliminates it entirely. This is the fundamental tradeoff of write-behind: speed in exchange for durability risk. For data where loss is unacceptable, use write-through (synchronous) or write-around with immediate database writes. Write-behind is appropriate only when the application can tolerate losing the most recent unflushed writes—session data, analytics counters, or recommendation scores where approximate values are acceptable. 2/2

Scaling Services › Caching

Stale-While-Revalidate

Open PDF

Stale-While-Revalidate Invalidation & Freshness The consistency article showed that caches can hold stale data. Invalidation is the mechanism that removes or refreshes stale entries. Get it wrong and users see outdated data. Get it too aggressive and the cache provides no benefit—every request falls through to the database. Time-To-Live (TTL) A TTL attaches a countdown to each cache entry. When the timer expires, the entry is automatically removed. The next read triggers a cache miss, fetches fresh data from the database, and stores it with a new TTL. TTL Lifecycle: Entry Birth → Expiry → Refetch Fresh (TTL counting down) Miss → DB fetch Fresh (new TTL) Store TTL=300s Expire TTL=0 Read (miss) Re-store TTL=300s reads (hits) reads (hits) Staleness window: data may be outdated for up to the full TTL duration time → 1/4

TTL is the simplest form of invalidation. It requires no coordination between services—each entry self-destructs on schedule. The tradeoff is staleness: data can be outdated for up to the full TTL duration before refreshing. Choosing a TTL value depends on how stale the data can be without harming the user experience. Data Type Typical TTL Reasoning Session tokens30 minutes Match session timeout policy Product catalog5–60 minutesPrices change infrequently but must update same-day User profile 5–15 minutesChanges are rare; brief staleness is acceptable Search results1–5 minutes Results change frequently; short freshness window ConfigurationHours to daysRarely changes; long cache life saves load Feature flags30–60 secondsChanges should propagate quickly across instances Setting TTL too short defeats the purpose of caching—the database absorbs nearly full load. Setting it too long means users see outdated data. Most teams start with a moderate value (5– 15 minutes) and adjust based on observed staleness complaints and cache hit rates. Explicit Invalidation TTL is passive—it waits for time to pass. Explicit invalidation is active—the application deletes or overwrites a cache entry immediately when the underlying data changes. This is the "delete key" step in the write-around pattern. Explicit invalidation provides tighter freshness than TTL alone. If the delete is synchronous and race-free, staleness is limited to the brief window between the database write and the cache delete. The challenge is ensuring every write path triggers invalidation. If the application updates user data through an API endpoint, a background job, and an admin panel, all three must delete the cache key. Missing one path creates a silent staleness bug that's hard to diagnose. Event-driven invalidation simplifies this. Instead of each writer knowing about the cache, the database emits change events (via change data capture or application-level events). A subscriber listens for changes and invalidates the corresponding cache keys. This centralizes invalidation logic in one place. 2/4

Event-Driven Invalidation: Centralized Cache Clearing API Endpoint Background Job Admin Panel Database change event CDC / Events (single subscriber) delete key Cache Benefit: single invalidation point No matter which service writes, the event subscriber invalidates the cache. New write paths don't need to know about caching at all. TTL as a Safety Net Production systems combine both approaches. Explicit invalidation handles the normal case— data changes, cache updates immediately. TTL acts as a safety net—if invalidation fails (message lost, subscriber crashes, key name mismatch), the stale entry self-destructs after the TTL window. This layered approach means the worst case is bounded staleness (the TTL duration) rather than unbounded staleness (stale forever until the next write). Standard TTL creates a binary state: the entry is either fresh (return it) or expired (cache miss, fetch from database). The moment of expiration causes a latency spike—the requesting user waits for the database round trip. Stale-while-revalidate softens this edge. When an entry expires, the cache returns the stale value immediately and triggers a background refresh. The user gets fast (slightly stale) data. Once the background refresh completes, subsequent requests get fresh data. Stale-While-Revalidate vs Standard TTL Standard TTL TTL expires: Request arrives after expiry: User waits for DB fetch... (latency spike) Response: fresh data after delay User feels the miss Stale-While-Revalidate Soft TTL expires: Request arrives after soft expiry: Return stale data instantly + async refresh in background Response: stale data, no delay Next request gets fresh data User never waits 3/4

This pattern largely avoids the latency spike at expiration. The tradeoff is that one request after expiration receives stale data. For most use cases (product pages, search results, recommendations), this brief staleness is invisible to the user. Some HTTP caches and CDNs support this via the stale-while-revalidate directive (RFC 5861) in Cache-Control headers. Application-level caches require custom logic: check if the entry is past its soft TTL, return it anyway, and spawn an async task to refresh. TTL Jitter When many entries share the same TTL (common when a batch of data is cached simultaneously), they all expire at the same moment. This creates a synchronized miss storm—thousands of requests hit the database at once, overwhelming it. Adding jitter spreads expirations randomly. Instead of a fixed 300-second TTL, each entry gets a TTL between 270 and 330 seconds (300 ± 10%). Expirations distribute evenly across the window rather than clustering at one instant. TTL Jitter: Spreading Expirations Without jitter (TTL = 300s for all): t=300s ALL expire at once! DB load spike: 1000 queries at once With jitter (TTL = 270–330s, ±10%): 270s 300s 330s spread across 60 seconds DB load smooth: ~17 queries/sec Same 1000 expirations — spike of 1000 vs steady 17/sec This is the same technique used to prevent the thundering herd problem discussed in the failure modes article later in this section. From here, eviction policies decide which entries to sacrifice when the cache is full and no entries have expired yet. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

Eviction Sizing

Open PDF

Eviction & Sizing TTL removes entries that have outlived their freshness. But what happens when the cache fills up and no entries have expired yet? New data needs space. The eviction policy decides which existing entry to sacrifice. Why Eviction Matters Memory is finite and expensive. A Redis instance with 16 GB of RAM can hold roughly 100– 200 million small key-value pairs (depending on value size). When the cache reaches capacity, every new write must evict an existing entry. The eviction policy determines whether the sacrificed entry was useful (causing a future miss) or dead weight (never to be read again). A good eviction policy maximizes hit rate—the proportion of reads served from cache rather than the database. A bad policy evicts entries that will be needed again soon, turning cache writes into wasted effort. LRU: Least Recently Used LRU evicts the entry that has not been accessed for the longest time. The assumption: entries accessed recently are likely to be accessed again soon. Entries untouched for a long period are probably cold. 1/4

LRU vs LFU: Scan Pollution Problem LRU (vulnerable to scans) Before batch scan: hot-A hot-B hot-C ← popular After batch reads 50K keys: scan-X scan-Y scan-Z ← never read again Hot entries evicted! Hit rate drops until re-cached. LFU (scan-resistant) Hot entries (high frequency): hot-A freq=500 hot-B freq=340 After batch reads 50K keys: hot-A freq=500 (safe) scan-X freq=1 (evicted first) Hot entries survive! Scan entries evicted on next write. LRU works well for workloads with temporal locality—when recent activity predicts near-future activity. Web sessions, user profiles, and page content follow this pattern. A user browsing a product category will likely revisit the same products within minutes. Weakness. A scan operation (one-time traversal of many keys) pollutes the cache. A batch job that reads 50,000 user records once pushes out the frequently-accessed hot entries. After the scan, the cache can fill with entries unlikely to be read again. Hit rate drops until the hot entries are re-cached through natural traffic. Redis supports approximated LRU (via allkeys-lru or volatile-lru policies). Rather than maintaining a sorted list of all keys by access time (expensive in memory), Redis samples a small number of keys and evicts the least recently used among the sample. This trades perfect accuracy for low overhead. Note: Redis defaults to noeviction—you must configure an eviction policy and set maxmemory for eviction to occur. LFU: Least Frequently Used LFU evicts the entry with the fewest accesses. It counts how often each entry is read and removes the one with the lowest count. The assumption: entries accessed many times are important; entries accessed rarely are disposable. LFU resists the scan problem that plagues LRU. A batch job that reads 50,000 keys once gives each a frequency count of 1. The hot entries with counts of 500+ are safe from eviction. Weakness. LFU has a cold-start problem. A newly cached entry starts with a count of 1. If the cache is full of entries with high counts, the new entry gets evicted immediately—even if it's about to become the most popular key. Some LFU implementations solve this with frequency decay: counts decrease over time, so historically popular but now-cold entries gradually lose their protection. 2/4

Redis implements LFU with a logarithmic counter and decay factor (lfu-log-factor and lfu- decay-time configuration). The logarithmic counter means the count saturates quickly—the difference between 100 and 1000 accesses is small, preventing older entries from being permanently immune. When to Use Which Workload Pattern Best Policy Reasoning User sessions, page views LRU Recent access predicts near-future access Product catalog, trending items LFU Popular items should survive regardless of recency Mixed with batch jobsLFU Resists scan pollution Streaming data, event logs Short TTL One-time-read data benefits little from caching; use short TTL if caching for brief replays Unknown or uniform access LRU Simpler, good default For most web applications, LRU is the safer default. Switch to LFU when you observe scan pollution (hit rate drops during batch operations) or when a small set of keys accounts for the majority of traffic. Memory Math: Sizing the Cache How much memory does the cache need? Too small means frequent evictions and low hit rate. Too large means paying for unused RAM. The calculation starts with the working set—the number of distinct keys accessed within one TTL window. Step 1: Estimate the number of hot keys. If the application serves 10,000 unique product pages per hour and the TTL is 15 minutes, the working set is roughly 2,500 keys (one quarter- hour's worth of unique accesses). Step 2: Estimate average entry size. Each cached entry includes the key, value, and Redis metadata overhead (~70–100 bytes per key in Redis). A product page cache entry might be: Key: 30 bytes (product:v1:detail:12345) Value: 2 KB (serialized JSON) Overhead: 80 bytes Total: ~2.1 KB per entry 3/4

Step 3: Calculate total memory. 2,500 keys × 2.1 KB = ~5.3 MB. Add 20–30% headroom for fragmentation and transient spikes: ~7 MB. For larger systems, the same formula scales. A cache serving 1 million unique keys at 2 KB each needs ~2 GB plus headroom—roughly 2.5–3 GB allocated. The 80/20 heuristic. Many read-heavy workloads are skewed—often approximated by a rule where 20% of keys serve 80% of traffic (Zipf-like distribution). Confirm your workload's skew from access logs before sizing. Caching only the hot 20% captures most of the benefit. If the total dataset is 10 million keys, caching 2 million keys (the hot set) may achieve a 90%+ hit rate. This means the cache can be much smaller than the total dataset. Monitoring Eviction Health Two metrics signal whether the cache is correctly sized: Hit rate. The percentage of reads served from cache. Below 90% for a well-tuned cache suggests the working set exceeds cache capacity. Above 99% may mean the cache is oversized (paying for unused memory). Eviction rate. How many entries Redis evicts per second. A steady eviction rate at maximum memory means the cache is at capacity—normal behavior. A spike in evictions correlates with batch operations or traffic bursts that push out hot entries. If the eviction rate is high and hit rate is dropping, the cache needs more memory or a better eviction policy. If evictions are rare and hit rate is consistently above 99%, the cache may be over-provisioned. From here, the discussion moves from single-node caching to distributed caching—how to shard data across multiple cache nodes when one machine can't hold the entire working set. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

Distributed Caching

Open PDF

Distributed Caching Modulo Remapping: Adding 1 Node Disrupts Most Keys hash % 4 % 5 Result key-A 5 Node 1 Node 0 remapped key-B 12 Node 0 Node 2 remapped key-C 7 Node 3 Node 2 remapped key-D 8 Node 0 Node 3 remapped key-E 14 Node 2 Node 4 remapped key-F 9 Node 1 Node 4 remapped key-G 3 Node 3 Node 3 same node key-H 20 Node 0 Node 0 same node 6 of 8 keys remapped (75%) — each becomes a cache miss on the new node A single Redis node with 64 GB of RAM serves well for small-to-medium workloads. But when the working set exceeds one machine's memory or throughput capacity, you need multiple cache nodes. The question becomes: which node holds which keys? Why Shard the Cache Two forces push toward multiple nodes: Memory limit. A single machine has finite RAM. If the working set is 200 GB, no single node can hold it. Spreading keys across 4 nodes gives 50 GB each. Throughput limit. A single Redis node handles roughly 100,000–200,000 operations per second (depending on value size, command type, and hardware). If the application's workload exceeds what a single node can sustain, sharding becomes necessary regardless of memory capacity. Sharding splits the key space across multiple nodes. Each node is responsible for a subset of keys. The client determines which node to contact for a given key, routes the request, and gets a response. From the application's perspective, the distributed cache behaves like one large cache. Hash-Based Routing The simplest approach: hash the key and use modulo to pick a node. node = hash(key) % num_nodes 1/5

Modulo Remapping: Adding 1 Node Disrupts Most Keys hash % 4 % 5 Result key-A 5 Node 1 Node 0 remapped key-B 12 Node 0 Node 2 remapped key-C 7 Node 3 Node 2 remapped key-D 8 Node 0 Node 3 remapped key-E 14 Node 2 Node 4 remapped key-F 9 Node 1 Node 4 remapped key-G 3 Node 3 Node 3 same node key-H 20 Node 0 Node 0 same node 6 of 8 keys remapped (75%) — each becomes a cache miss on the new node This distributes keys evenly. But adding or removing a node changes num_nodes, which changes the modulo result for nearly every key. A 4-node cluster expanding to 5 nodes remaps ~80% of keys. Those remapped keys become misses on the new node—the database absorbs a burst of traffic until the cache re-warms. Consistent Hashing Consistent hashing solves the remapping problem. When a node is added or removed, only the keys previously assigned to the affected node are remapped—roughly 1/N of the total keys, where N is the number of nodes. The other (N-1)/N keys stay on their current nodes. Consistent Hashing: Adding a Node Before: 3 nodes A BC k1 k2 k3 k4 k1,k2 → B | k3 → C | k4 → A → add node D After: 4 nodes A D new BC k1 k2 k3 k4 k1 → B | k3 → C | k4 → A k2 → D (only k2 moved!) Only keys between C and D (clockwise) are reassigned — all others stay put 2/5

The concept: nodes and keys both map onto a circular hash space (a ring). Each key is assigned to the first node encountered when walking clockwise from the key's position on the ring. Adding a new node between two existing nodes only takes keys from its clockwise neighbor—all other assignments are unchanged. The Consistent Hashing article in the Data Partitioning section covers the algorithm in detail, including virtual nodes for load balancing. The same technique applies directly to cache sharding. Redis Cluster: Hash Slots Redis Cluster uses a fixed set of 16,384 hash slots instead of a continuous ring. Each key maps to a slot via CRC16(key) % 16384. Each node owns a range of slots. When scaling from 3 to 4 nodes, you (or your managed service) reshard some slots from existing nodes to the new one. Keys in moved slots are migrated—clients may see brief redirections during the migration, but other slots are unaffected. NodesSlots per NodeKey Remapping on Add 3 → 4 5,461 → 4,096~25% of keys 4 → 5 4,096 → 3,277~20% of keys 6 → 7 2,731 → 2,341~14% of keys Redis Cluster provides tooling to assign and rebalance slots; managed services often automate this. The client library discovers the slot map from any node and routes requests directly to the correct node without a proxy. Hash tags allow related keys to land on the same node. The key {user:123}:profile and {user:123}:settings both hash on user:123, placing them on the same slot. This enables multi-key operations (like MGET) that require co-located keys. 3/5

Client-Side vs Proxy-Based Routing Client-Side vs Proxy-Based Routing Client-Side (Redis Cluster) App + Smart Client (knows slot map) Node 1 Node 2 Node 3 ✓ 1 network hop (fast) ✓ No single point of failure ✗ Cluster-aware client required ✗ Client manages topology Proxy-Based (Twemproxy/Envoy) App + Simple Client Proxy Node 1 Node 2 Node 3 ✓ Simple client (no cluster awareness) ✓ Centralized access control ✗ Extra network hop (+0.5ms latency) ✗ Proxy is a bottleneck/SPOF Two architectures for routing requests to the correct shard: Client-side routing. The application client library knows the shard map and routes requests directly. Redis Cluster uses this approach. The client is "smart"—it maintains a local copy of the slot-to-node mapping and sends each command to the correct node. This avoids an extra network hop but requires cluster-aware client libraries. Proxy-based routing. A proxy sits between the application and the cache nodes. The application sends all requests to the proxy, which routes them to the correct shard. The application uses a simple, non-cluster-aware client. The proxy adds one network hop of latency but simplifies the client. Tools like Twemproxy and Envoy support this pattern. In Redis Cluster deployments, client-side routing is typical because the latency savings of direct connections outweigh the slight client complexity. Proxy-based routing is useful when the application cannot use a cluster-aware client or when you need centralized access control. Hot Key Problem Sharding distributes keys evenly by hash value, not by access frequency. If one key receives 50% of all reads (a viral product page, a breaking news article), its shard handles 50% of total load while other shards idle. The hot shard becomes a bottleneck regardless of how many total nodes exist. 4/5

Hot Key Problem: Sharding Doesn't Help App Server 1 App Server 2 App Server 3 App Server 4 product:viral (50K reads/sec) Shard 1 100 req/s (idle) Shard 2 🔥 50K req/s (overloaded!) Shard 3 150 req/s (idle) One key = one shard. Adding more shards doesn't help. Mitigations: 1. Read replicas per shard Spread reads across replicas 2. Key splitting product:viral:{1..10} 3. Local in-process cache LRU + 5s TTL per app server Each trades complexity for load distribution Mitigations for hot keys: Read replicas per shard. Each shard has one or more read replicas. Hot-key reads spread across the primary and its replicas. This helps but adds complexity and memory cost. Key splitting. Append a random suffix to the hot key (e.g., product:viral:{1..10}). The client reads from a random variant. Ten variants can spread the load across up to 10 shards, depending on how slots are distributed. The write path must update all variants, adding write amplification. Local caching. Cache the hot key in application-level memory (an in-process LRU cache with short TTL). This reduces Redis traffic for hot keys significantly, depending on the local cache's hit rate and TTL. This reduces Redis load but introduces a second consistency layer. From there, the focus shifts to keeping the distributed cache available when individual nodes fail—replication, failover, and the tradeoffs involved. 5/5

Scaling Services › Caching

Cache High Availability

Open PDF

Cache High Availability A sharded cache spreads keys across multiple nodes. But if one node crashes, all keys on that node become misses simultaneously. If that node held popular keys, the database absorbs a sudden spike. High availability keeps the cache serving even when individual nodes fail. Replication: Primary-Replica Each cache node (primary) has one or more replicas that mirror its data. The primary handles all writes and replicates them asynchronously to replicas. Reads can be served by either the primary or its replicas. Primary-Replica: Failover on Crash Normal Operation App writes Primary Replica 1 Replica 2 async replication reads After Primary Fails App Primary ✗ Replica 1 → NEW Primary Replica 2 redirected Sentinel detects failure, promotes replica in ~2-5 seconds (automatic) 1/4

When the primary fails, a replica contains a nearly-complete copy of the data. Promotion to primary is fast—the replica already holds the data in memory. The only data lost is writes that arrived at the primary after the last replication batch (typically milliseconds of data). Read scaling. Replicas also serve as read capacity. A primary with 2 replicas handles roughly 3x the read throughput of a single node. The application reads from replicas for eventually- consistent data and from the primary when strong consistency is required. Replication lag. Because replication is asynchronous, replicas can be slightly behind the primary. A write to the primary may not appear on the replica for milliseconds to seconds depending on load and network conditions. For cache use cases, this lag is typically acceptable—the cache already operates on eventual consistency assumptions. Failover: Sentinel When a primary fails, something must detect the failure and promote a replica. Redis Sentinel provides this automation. Sentinel is a separate process (typically 3+ instances for quorum) that monitors primary and replica nodes. When a majority of Sentinels agree that a primary is unreachable, they trigger an automated failover. Sentinel Failover: Detect → Promote → Reconfigure Sentinel Quorum (3 instances) S1 S2 S3 1. Monitor Primary ✗ down no heartbeat 2. Quorum vote Majority agrees: "Primary is unreachable" 3. Select replica Replica 1 most up-to-date Replica 2 4. Promote Replica 1 → NEW Primary 5. Reconfigure Replica 2 now follows new primary 6. Notify clients Client libraries reconnect to new primary address Total failover time: ~2-5 seconds (writes unavailable during transition) The steps: 1. Select the replica with the most up-to-date data 2. Promote it to primary 3. Reconfigure the other replicas to follow the new primary 4. Notify client libraries of the topology change 2/4

The failover takes a few seconds—during which writes to the failed primary are lost and reads may fail. After promotion, the cluster resumes normal operation. Client libraries that support Sentinel reconnect automatically to the new primary. Split-brain risk. If the old primary is not actually dead (network partition), both the old and new primary accept writes. When the partition heals, the old primary discovers it's been demoted and discards its writes. Data written to the old primary during the partition is lost. For cache workloads, this is acceptable—the data can be re-fetched from the database. Redis Cluster: Built-In HA Redis Cluster combines sharding and replication in one system. Each shard (set of hash slots) has a primary and one or more replicas. When a shard's primary fails, the cluster automatically promotes a replica—no separate Sentinel process needed. Redis Cluster: Sharding + Replication Shard 1 (slots 0–5460) Primary A Replica A1 Replica A2 Shard 2 (slots 5461–10922) Primary B Replica B1 Replica B2 Shard 3 (slots 10923–16383) Primary C ✗ (failed) Replica C1 → Primary Replica C2 Shards 1 & 2: unaffected by Shard 3 failure Continue serving their keys normally Shard 3: automatic failover Replica promoted, cluster recovers in seconds The cluster remains available as long as each shard has at least one surviving node (primary or replica). If an entire shard (primary + all replicas) goes down, only the keys in that shard's slots become unavailable. Other shards continue serving normally. Configuration NodesTolerates Total Memory 3 primaries, 1 replica each6 1 failure per shard3x shard size 3 primaries, 2 replicas each9 2 failures per shard3x shard size 6 primaries, 1 replica each12 1 failure per shard6x shard size The cluster-require-full-coverage configuration controls behavior when a shard is completely down. Set to yes (default), the cluster stops serving key operations (both reads and writes) and reports CLUSTERDOWN. Set to no, the surviving shards continue serving their keys while the failed shard's keys return errors. 3/4

Cache as a Non-Critical Layer Caches differ from databases in one important way: the database is the source of truth. If the cache fails entirely, the application can fall back to direct database queries. It will be slower, but no data is lost. This changes the HA calculus. For a database, losing a node risks data loss—stronger durability guarantees (sometimes synchronous replication) may be required depending on RPO. For a cache, losing a node risks only performance degradation. Asynchronous replication (faster, simpler) is acceptable because the worst case is re-fetching from the database. Graceful degradation. When a cache node fails, the application should handle the resulting misses without cascading failures. Circuit breakers prevent the database from being overwhelmed by the sudden miss storm. Rate limiting on cache-miss paths ensures the database handles the spike without crashing. The cache re-warms naturally as requests flow through. From here, the cold start problem appears when a cache node starts empty, and warming strategies prevent the database from getting overwhelmed. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

Warming Strategies and Database Protection

Open PDF

Warming Strategies and Database Protection Cold Start Replication and failover keep the cache serving during node failures. But when a replacement node comes online—or a new node joins the cluster—its memory is empty. Every request to that node misses. The database absorbs load it hasn't seen since the cache was first deployed. The Problem A system handling 50,000 reads per second with a 95% hit rate sends roughly 2,500 queries per second to the database. The database is provisioned for this load. When the cache is cold, all 50,000 requests become misses. The database sees a 20x spike instantly. 1/4

Cold Start: Database Load Impact Warm Cache (Steady State) 50,000 req/s 95% hit Cache 2,500 req/s Database Cold Cache (After Restart) 50,000 req/s 100% miss Cache (empty) 50,000 req/s Database (20x load) Line thickness represents relative traffic volume This isn't just a performance issue—it can cascade. The database saturates, query latency spikes, application threads block waiting for responses, connection pools exhaust, and the system returns errors to users. The cache was protecting the database. Removing that protection, even temporarily, can trigger an outage. When Cold Starts Happen Fresh deployments are the obvious case, but cold starts occur in several scenarios: Node replacement after failure. The HA system promotes a replica or spins up a fresh node. If the replacement has no data (fresh instance rather than promoted replica), it starts cold. Scaling out. Adding a new shard to a Redis Cluster redistributes hash slots. Keys migrated to the new shard must be fetched from the database on first access—the new node doesn't have them in memory yet. Cache flush after a bug. A code bug writes corrupt data to cache. The fix involves flushing all affected keys (or the entire cache). The system returns to cold-start conditions. Mass TTL expiration. If many keys share the same TTL and were written at the same time (a batch import, for example), they expire simultaneously. The cache effectively goes cold for that key range. Adding jitter to TTLs (covered in the invalidation article) prevents this. Warm-Up Strategies Three approaches, from simplest to most proactive: 2/4

Warm-Up Strategies: Hit Rate Recovery Over Time 95% 50% 0% Time pre-load completes Gradual warm-up Controlled ramp-up Pre-loading Hit Rate Gradual warm-up. Let natural traffic populate the cache through misses. The first request for each key hits the database; subsequent requests hit cache. The hit rate climbs from 0% toward steady-state over minutes as popular keys accumulate. This works when the database has headroom to absorb the temporary spike. For the example above, the database must handle 50,000 QPS during warm-up instead of its usual 2,500. If the database can sustain this for several minutes, gradual warm-up is the simplest approach. The risk: if the database cannot handle the spike, the system degrades before the cache has time to warm. Controlled ramp-up. Route traffic to the new cache node gradually. Start at 10% of traffic, let it warm for a few minutes, then increase to 25%, 50%, and finally 100%. During the ramp, the old cache (or database) still handles the remaining traffic. This limits the cold-start spike to a fraction of total load. Load balancers or service meshes can implement this with weighted routing. The tradeoff is operational complexity—you need tooling to manage the ramp and monitoring to decide when to increase the weight. Pre-loading (cache priming). Before accepting traffic, load the most popular keys into the new cache node. This requires knowing which keys are hot. Sources include: Access logs from the last hour (extract the most-requested keys) A popularity index maintained in the database (a counter table tracking access frequency) A snapshot from an existing replica (copy the key-value pairs directly) Pre-loading gets the hit rate to a reasonable level (often 70–80% of steady-state) before the node serves real traffic. The remaining keys populate through natural misses. This approach is common in large-scale systems where a full cold start would overwhelm the database. 3/4

Protecting the Database During Warm-Up Even with warm-up strategies, some period of elevated database load is unavoidable. Two mechanisms prevent the spike from cascading: Rate limiting on the miss path. Cap the number of concurrent database queries triggered by cache misses. If the limit is 5,000 QPS and 50,000 misses arrive, 45,000 requests wait or receive a degraded response (stale data from a secondary source, or a brief error). The database stays within its capacity. Request coalescing. When multiple requests miss on the same key simultaneously, only one query goes to the database. The others wait for the result. This is the same singleflight pattern used to prevent stampedes (covered in the failure modes article). During cold start, it's especially effective because many users request the same popular keys. Monitoring Cold Starts The hit rate is the primary indicator. A sudden drop from steady-state (say, 95%) toward 0% signals a cold start event. Correlate with deployment events, node replacements, or cache flush operations to identify the cause. Track the warm-up curve: how quickly the hit rate recovers. A healthy warm-up reaches 80% of steady-state within minutes for most workloads. If recovery is slow, the working set may be too large for natural traffic to cover—pre-loading would help. From here, specific failure patterns—cache penetration, avalanche, and stampede—show what can go wrong and how the defenses prevent them from taking down the system. 4/4

Scaling Services › Caching

Failure Modes

Open PDF

Failure Modes A well-configured cache typically absorbs 90–99% of read traffic, depending on access patterns and TTL strategy. The database handles the remainder. But specific failure patterns bypass the cache entirely, routing full traffic to the database. A database provisioned for that small fraction of load cannot absorb 100%. Three patterns cause this. Cache Penetration Penetration occurs when requests query keys that do not exist in the cache or the database. Every request is a cache miss. Every miss queries the database. The database returns empty. The cache stores nothing (there's no data to cache). The next identical request repeats the cycle. Cache Penetration: Non-Existent Keys Bypass Cache Requests user:9999999 product:-1 item:abc!@# Cache (miss — key absent) 100% miss Database (returns empty) nothing to store → cycle repeats Defense: Bloom filter blocks non-existent keys before cache lookup Every request hits the database — cache provides zero protection 1/4

An attacker or a buggy client can exploit this by requesting random non-existent IDs (user:99999999, product:-1). The cache provides no protection because these keys are never populated. Defense 1: Cache empty results. When the database returns no data for a key, cache a sentinel value (e.g., NULL or an empty object) with a short TTL (30–60 seconds). Subsequent requests for the same non-existent key hit the cache and return the sentinel without touching the database. The limitation: if the attacker uses random keys (each request a different non-existent ID), the cache fills with millions of sentinel entries. Memory is wasted on entries that are never re- requested. Defense 2: Bloom filter. A Bloom filter is a probabilistic data structure that answers "might exist" or "definitely does not exist" using minimal memory. Place a Bloom filter in front of the cache. Before checking the cache, check the Bloom filter. If the filter says "does not exist," reject the request without querying cache or database. The filter must be updated on the write path when new keys are created—otherwise legitimate new keys are incorrectly rejected. A Bloom filter holding 100 million keys uses roughly 120 MB of memory (at 1% false positive rate). The remaining 1% of non-existent keys pass through as false positives and still hit the database, but the vast majority are blocked. Cache Avalanche Avalanche occurs when many cache entries expire simultaneously, causing a wave of misses that flood the database. This happens when a large batch of data is cached at the same time with the same TTL—all entries expire at the same instant. Cache Avalanche: Mass Synchronized Expiration Cache entries loaded at midnight with TTL=3600s: p:1 p:2 p:3 p:4... ×1000 1 hour p:1 p:2 p:3 p:4 ALL expire at 1:00 AM! Database load: Normal: 50 queries/sec (cache absorbs 95%) SPIKE: 1000 q/s 20× normal load Gradual recovery as cache re-warms 12:00–1:00 AM 1:00 AM 1:00–1:15 AM Defense: TTL jitter (±10%) Entries expire between 54–66 min → spike spreads into gentle slope A product catalog loaded into cache at midnight with a 1-hour TTL creates an avalanche at 1:00 AM. Every product page becomes a miss. The database receives the full catalog's read traffic at once. 2/4

Defense 1: TTL jitter. Add a random offset to each entry's TTL. Instead of all entries expiring at 3600 seconds, each gets a TTL between 3240 and 3960 seconds (3600 ± 10%). Expirations spread across 12 minutes instead of clustering at one instant. Defense 2: Staggered warm-up. After a cache restart or cold deploy, don't load all entries at once. Load entries gradually as they're requested (lazy loading). The database handles elevated but distributed load rather than a synchronized spike. Defense 3: Multi-level caching. Use a local in-process cache (small, fast) in front of the distributed cache. Even if the distributed cache experiences an avalanche, the local cache absorbs some of the repeated hits. The database sees a reduced spike. Cache Stampede (Thundering Herd) Stampede occurs when a single popular key expires and many concurrent requests all experience a cache miss simultaneously. All of them query the database for the same key at the same instant. A product page cached with a 60-second TTL expires. Within the next millisecond, 5,000 users request the same page. All 5,000 see a cache miss. All 5,000 query the database for the same row. The database executes 5,000 identical queries instead of 1. Cache Stampede: Hot Key Expires Without lock: product:viral TTL=0 5,000 users all miss DB: 5,000 queries! DB overloaded With request coalescing (lock): product:viral TTL=0 1st request lock + fetch DB: 1 query 4,999 wait... cache populated → all 4,999 read from cache DB protected Defense 1: Locking (request coalescing). The first request that discovers the miss acquires a lock on the key. Subsequent requests see the lock and wait (or return a stale value) instead of querying the database. Once the first request populates the cache, it releases the lock. All waiting requests read from the freshly-populated cache. This reduces 5,000 database queries to 1. The tradeoff is added complexity: the lock must have a timeout (to prevent deadlocks if the first request fails), and waiting requests need a fallback if the lock expires. 3/4

Defense 2: Stale-while-revalidate. Return the expired (stale) value to all requests while a single background task refreshes the cache. Users get slightly stale data but never wait. This still requires coordination (a lock or singleflight mechanism) to ensure only one task triggers the refresh—without it, multiple workers may revalidate simultaneously. Defense 3: Preemptive refresh. Refresh the cache before the TTL expires. A background job monitors popular keys and refreshes them at 80% of their TTL (e.g., refresh at 48 seconds for a 60-second TTL). Under normal operation the key is always fresh when requests arrive, so no miss occurs. If the refresh job fails or lags, the key can still expire—combine with stale-while- revalidate as a fallback. Choosing Defenses Failure Mode Root Cause Primary Defense Fallback Penetration Non-existent keys Bloom filter Cache nulls with short TTL Avalanche Synchronized expiration TTL jitter Staggered warm-up Stampede Hot key expires Request coalescing (lock) Stale-while-revalidate In practice, production systems deploy all three defenses simultaneously. TTL jitter is nearly free (add one random number). Bloom filters require a setup step but prevent an entire class of attacks. Request coalescing for hot keys prevents the most damaging failure mode—a single expired key taking down the database. A hands-on lab simulates these failure modes so you can observe the impact and verify that the defenses work. 4/4
