Simulating Failure Modes and Defenses

Open PDF

Simulating Failure Modes and Defenses Lab: The Disaster Drill The previous article described three failure modes in theory. This lab simulates each one so you can observe the database impact and verify that defenses work. Exercise 1: Cache Stampede and Request Coalescing Simulate a hot key expiring while 100 concurrent readers try to access it. First without protection (all hit the database), then with a lock that coalesces requests. Stampede: Without vs With Locking Loading Python environment... This may take a few seconds on first load. Without the lock, all 100 threads race to the database simultaneously. With request coalescing, only the first thread queries the database. The other 99 wait for the result and read from the freshly-populated cache. Exercise 2: Cache Penetration and Bloom Filter Simulate an attacker requesting random non-existent keys. First without protection (all requests hit the database), then with a Bloom filter that blocks impossible keys. 1/2

Penetration: Bloom Filter Defense Loading Python environment... This may take a few seconds on first load. The Bloom filter catches most non-existent keys without any database interaction. A small percentage may pass through as false positives (the Bloom filter says "might exist" when it doesn't), but the database load drops from 500 queries to near zero. Exercise 3: Cache Avalanche and TTL Jitter Simulate 1000 entries all expiring at the same time versus staggered expiration with jitter. Avalanche: Synchronized vs Jittered Expiration Loading Python environment... This may take a few seconds on first load. Without jitter, all 1000 entries expire in the same instant—the database receives a burst of 1000 queries at once. With jitter, expirations spread across a 12-second window. The peak load drops from 1000 to roughly 80–100 queries per second—an order of magnitude reduction that the database can handle comfortably. These three exercises demonstrate the failure modes and their defenses. In a system design interview, being able to name the failure mode, explain why it happens, and describe the fix shows depth beyond basic cache usage. 2/2

Scaling Services › Caching

Security Observability

Open PDF

Security & Observability The caching section has covered patterns, consistency, scaling, and failure modes. This final article addresses two often-overlooked concerns—security and observability—then provides a checklist for discussing caching in system design interviews. Security: What Not to Cache Caching is fundamentally about sharing data across requests. This creates risk when the cached data should not be shared. Auth leakage. A cache key like user:profile:123 stores one user's profile. If the application accidentally uses a key without the user identifier (e.g., profile:current), one user's data can be served to another user. This is a serious authorization breach. The fix: always include the identity scope in the key. For per-user data, the key must contain the user ID. For per-tenant data, include the tenant ID. Never cache personalized data under a generic key. Cached responses with credentials. An API response containing an access token or session cookie gets cached. The next user hitting the same cache entry receives someone else's credentials. CDNs are especially vulnerable—a Cache-Control: public header on an authenticated endpoint leaks tokens to all subsequent users. The fix: never cache responses that contain tokens, cookies, or credentials. Set Cache- Control: private, no-store on authenticated API endpoints. Review cache headers in deployment checklists. 1/2

PII in cache. Cached user profiles containing email addresses, phone numbers, or payment data persist in memory. If the cache node's memory is accessible (debug tools, memory dumps, side-channel attacks), PII is exposed. Cached data also bypasses database-level encryption and access controls. Mitigations: Cache only the fields needed for display (name, avatar), not sensitive fields (SSN, payment info) If sensitive fields must be cached, encrypt values at rest in the cache Set short TTLs on PII-containing entries to minimize exposure window Include cache stores in your data retention and GDPR deletion procedures Observability: Four Key Metrics A cache without monitoring is a black box. These four metrics reveal whether the cache is healthy, correctly sized, and protecting the database. Hit rate. cache_hits / (cache_hits + cache_misses). The most important metric. Many read-heavy workloads aim for 90%+ hit rates, though acceptable targets vary by system. A drop signals that the working set exceeds cache capacity, TTLs are too short, or a failure mode (penetration, avalanche) is occurring. Latency (p50, p95, p99). The time to complete a cache operation. On a low-latency network, Redis reads are often sub-millisecond to a few milliseconds depending on payload size and load. A spike in p99 latency indicates network congestion, oversized values, or the cache node approaching CPU saturation. Eviction rate. How many entries Redis evicts per second due to memory pressure. Some evictions at max memory are expected, though sustained high rates may indicate undersizing. A sudden spike means the working set just grew (new feature launch, traffic increase) and the cache needs more memory. Memory usage. Current memory consumption vs maximum configured memory. When memory reaches maxmemory, Redis evicts keys if an eviction policy is configured (otherwise writes are rejected with OOM errors). Track the ratio over time to forecast when capacity needs to increase. Example starting thresholds (tune based on your baseline and SLOs): Hit rate drops below 85% for 5 minutes p99 latency exceeds 5ms Eviction rate increases 10x from baseline Memory usage exceeds 90% of configured maximum This sets up a caching interview checklist—a structured framework with detailed examples for each dimension. 2/2

Scaling Services › Caching

How to Discuss Caching in System Design

Open PDF

How to Discuss Caching in System Design Interview Walkthrough Candidates often mention caching as a one-liner: "We'll add Redis here for performance." This fails to demonstrate understanding. Interviewers want to see reasoning—why cache this data, which pattern, what happens on failure. This article walks through eight dimensions with a running example to show how to discuss caching with depth. The Running Example Throughout this walkthrough, we'll use an e-commerce product page as the scenario. The page shows product details, pricing, reviews summary, and seller information. The system handles 50,000 page views per second at peak, with product data updating a few times per day. 1. What to Cache Start by identifying which data is worth caching. Not everything benefits equally from a cache— the best candidates are read frequently and change infrequently, though caches also help with expensive computations and session state. The thought process. Look at the read-to-write ratio. Product details are read 50,000 times per second but updated perhaps a few times per day—an astronomically skewed read-to-write ratio and an ideal caching candidate. Compare this to a user's shopping cart, which mutates on every click. A cache-aside approach for the cart has limited benefit because the cached entry invalidates on every mutation (though Redis is often used as the cart's primary store). 1/6

What to say. "The product detail query runs on every page view—50,000 QPS at peak. The data changes a few times per day. This extreme read-to-write ratio makes it a strong caching candidate. The shopping cart, by contrast, changes on every interaction, so caching it provides minimal benefit." Common mistake. Saying "cache everything" without reasoning. The interviewer wants to see you distinguish hot data from cold data. Another mistake: caching only at one layer. Consider whether both the database query result (app cache) and the rendered response (CDN) deserve caching. What the interviewer is looking for. Can you identify the specific queries or data paths that create database load? Can you quantify the benefit (hit rate, load reduction)? 2. Cache Key Design After deciding what to cache, explain how keys are structured. This shows attention to correctness and future-proofing. The thought process. A product page cache key needs to uniquely identify the content. The key product:v1:detail:12345 encodes: the entity type (product), a schema version (v1), the data scope (detail), and the identifier (12345). If the product detail schema changes (a new field is added), bumping to v2 avoids serving old-format data from cache. What to say. "I'd use keys like product:v1:detail:{id}. The version segment lets us deploy schema changes safely—new code writes to v2 keys, old v1 entries expire naturally via TTL. For the reviews summary, a separate key: product:v1:reviews:{id} with a shorter TTL since reviews change more frequently." Common mistake. Using overly generic keys (product:12345) that collide when different views of the same product are cached (detail view vs list view vs admin view). Another mistake: forgetting versioning, which forces cache flushes on every deploy. What the interviewer is looking for. Do you think about key collisions, versioning, and how different data types have different freshness requirements? 3. Read and Write Patterns State which caching pattern you're using and why. Default to cache-aside unless there's a specific reason for another pattern. The thought process. Product pages are read-heavy with infrequent writes. Cache-aside (read: check cache → miss → query DB → store in cache; write: update DB → delete cache key) is the natural fit. The application controls all cache interactions, and the cache layer stays simple (just Redis as a key-value store). 2/6

If this were an inventory count updated on every purchase (write-heavy), write-behind might apply—buffer frequent updates in cache and flush to the database periodically. But the durability risk (lost writes on crash) makes write-behind inappropriate for inventory where accuracy matters. What to say. "For product details, I'd use cache-aside. Reads check Redis first, fall back to PostgreSQL on miss, and store the result. On product update, we delete the cache key—the next read repopulates with fresh data. Write-through would add unnecessary latency since product reads far exceed writes, and most writes won't be read again immediately." Common mistake. Choosing write-through "for consistency" without considering that write- around (delete on write) achieves the same consistency with less cache pollution. Another mistake: not addressing the write path at all—only discussing reads. What the interviewer is looking for. Can you reason about the tradeoff between consistency and performance? Do you understand when each pattern applies? 4. Invalidation Strategy Explain how stale data is prevented. This is where candidates often lack depth. The thought process. Two mechanisms work together. Explicit invalidation (delete the key when the product is updated) handles the normal case—on the next read, the cache repopulates with fresh data. TTL acts as a safety net—if the invalidation message is lost or the delete fails, the stale entry self-destructs after a bounded time. For product details that change a few times per day, a 15-minute TTL means the worst-case staleness is 15 minutes (if invalidation fails). For pricing that must be accurate, a 60-second TTL tightens the window. For reviews summaries (where slight staleness is invisible to users), 5–10 minutes is acceptable. What to say. "Each data type gets a TTL matching its staleness tolerance: product details at 15 minutes, pricing at 60 seconds, reviews at 5 minutes. On any product write, we explicitly delete the cache key—so the normal-case staleness is near zero. The TTL is a safety net for when invalidation fails. I'd also add jitter (±10%) to prevent batch-loaded products from expiring simultaneously." Common mistake. Setting one TTL for all data types. Another: relying only on TTL without explicit invalidation (accepting up to 15 minutes of staleness on every write). The worst mistake: no invalidation strategy at all—"the cache will eventually expire." What the interviewer is looking for. Do you understand that different data types need different freshness guarantees? Can you layer explicit + TTL-based invalidation? 5. Eviction Policy When the cache fills, something must be removed. State the policy and why. 3/6

The thought process. LRU (least recently used) is the default for most web workloads because recent access predicts future access—users browse a category and revisit the same products within minutes. If the system runs nightly batch jobs that scan the entire product catalog (for indexing or analytics), LFU (least frequently used) resists the scan pollution that would push hot products out of cache. What to say. "LRU as the default. If we run catalog scans that pollute the cache, I'd switch to LFU—it protects frequently-accessed products from being evicted by one-time batch reads. For sizing, the hot 20% of products (roughly 200K entries at 2KB each) needs about 400MB—well within a single Redis node." Common mistake. Ignoring eviction entirely. Another: over-engineering by proposing custom eviction policies when LRU handles most cases. What the interviewer is looking for. Do you understand the memory tradeoff? Can you size the cache with rough math? 6. Scaling When one node isn't enough, explain how to distribute the cache. The thought process. A single Redis node handles ~100K–200K operations per second. Our 50,000 product page views generate 50K cache reads per second—one node suffices for now. But if traffic doubles or the working set exceeds one node's memory, sharding across multiple nodes becomes necessary. Redis Cluster splits the key space into 16,384 hash slots distributed across nodes. Adding a node only remaps keys in the transferred slots (~25% for 3→4 nodes). For read scaling, each shard can have replicas that serve read traffic. What to say. "At 50K QPS with typical payload sizes, a single Redis node likely handles the load. If we grow beyond a single node's throughput or memory capacity, I'd shard using Redis Cluster. Hash slots give us incremental scaling—adding a node only remaps ~25% of keys. For the viral-product hot-key problem, I'd use local in-process caching with a 5-second TTL to absorb repeated reads before they hit Redis." Common mistake. Proposing sharding for a system that doesn't need it (over-engineering). Another: ignoring the hot key problem—sharding distributes keys evenly by hash, not by access frequency. What the interviewer is looking for. Can you identify when scaling is actually needed? Do you understand the hot key problem that sharding doesn't solve? 7. Failure Modes Name the relevant failure mode for the scenario and its defense. This shows senior-level awareness. 4/6

The thought process. For a product page system, the primary risk is stampede (thundering herd). A popular product's cache entry expires, and thousands of concurrent requests all miss simultaneously and flood the database with identical queries. Cache penetration is relevant if the system allows user-generated product searches—users can query non-existent product IDs. Avalanche applies if products are batch-loaded with identical TTLs. What to say. "The main risk is stampede on popular products. When a viral item's cache expires, 5,000 concurrent requests all miss and hit the database. I'd use request coalescing— the first miss acquires a lock, fetches from DB, and populates the cache. The other 4,999 requests wait for the lock and read from cache. For user-facing search by ID, I'd add a Bloom filter to reject non-existent products before they reach the database." Common mistake. Not knowing any failure modes—just saying "the cache could go down." Another: naming all three modes without identifying which is most relevant to the specific scenario. What the interviewer is looking for. Can you identify the specific risk for this system? Can you name a concrete defense and explain the mechanism? 8. Consistency Tradeoff Acknowledge that caching trades consistency for performance. Frame the acceptable staleness per data type. The thought process. Different fields on the same product page have different staleness tolerances. The product name and images can be stale for hours (they rarely change). The price must be current (a stale price leads to customer complaints or financial loss). The review count can be stale for minutes (users don't notice if it's 4,201 vs 4,203). What to say. "Caching trades consistency for performance, and the acceptable tradeoff varies by data type. Pricing gets a 60-second TTL with synchronous invalidation on update—the worst case is 60 seconds of stale pricing. Product descriptions use 15-minute TTL—staleness here is invisible to users. Inventory count bypasses the cache entirely and queries the database directly, because overselling is worse than a few milliseconds of added latency." Common mistake. Treating all data the same ("everything gets a 5-minute TTL"). Another: claiming the cache provides "strong consistency" when it fundamentally does not. The worst mistake: never mentioning the consistency tradeoff at all. What the interviewer is looking for. Do you understand that caching is a tradeoff, not free performance? Can you articulate what level of staleness is acceptable for different data types? 5/6

Matching Depth to the Problem Not every interview requires all eight points. Focus on what matters for the specific system being designed. System Type Emphasize De-emphasize Social media feedRead patterns, stampede, hot keysWrite patterns, security Payment system Consistency, what not to cache, securityEviction, scaling E-commerce catalogAll eight dimensions apply — Real-time dashboardWrite-behind, observability, staleness Eviction, penetration Chat application Session caching, HA, scaling Write patterns, avalanche The goal is demonstrating reasoning, not reciting a list. Pick the 3–4 dimensions most relevant to the problem and discuss them with depth rather than covering all eight superficially. 6/6

Scaling Services › Dataflow

3 Efficient Ways to Enable Inter-Process Communication

Open PDF

3 Efficient Ways to Enable Inter-Process Communication Dataflow Overview A user service runs on server A. An order service runs on server B. The order service needs user data to process a purchase. These processes cannot share memory across different servers. How does the order service get user information from the user service? The Inter-Process Communication Problem Processes on the same machine share memory. Read a variable. Write to an array. Direct memory access is simple and fast. But distributed systems run processes on different machines. No shared memory exists. Data must move over the network. The order service needs to encode user data, send it over TCP, and decode it on the other end. This data movement between separate processes is dataflow. Three patterns handle this: shared databases, synchronous service calls, and message queues. Database as Communication Channel One process writes to a database. Another process reads from it. The database acts as an intermediary. Both processes connect to the same PostgreSQL instance. The user service inserts a row into the users table. The order service queries that table when processing an order. 1/4

This pattern centralizes data. Multiple services read the same source of truth. Schema changes affect all readers, but at least everyone sees consistent structure. The database handles concurrency and locking. Two services updating the same row use transactions to prevent conflicts. The database becomes a bottleneck under heavy load. Every service queries the same instance. Read replicas help but add complexity. Latency increases with each database round trip. A service chain that queries the database five times adds five network hops. Use database dataflow when data consistency matters more than latency. Configuration data, user profiles, and inventory counts work well. Real-time chat messages do not. Synchronous Service Calls The order service calls the user service directly via HTTP or gRPC. Send a GET request. Wait for a response. Block until data arrives. This is request-response communication. 2/4

The client controls when to fetch data. Services expose APIs. Other services call those APIs on demand. The order service requests user data only when needed for an active order. No polling. No background updates. Just direct requests. Each service encapsulates its data. The user service owns user data. The order service cannot query the user database directly. It must go through the user service API. This isolation allows independent evolution. The user service can change its database schema without affecting callers as long as the API contract remains stable. The caller blocks waiting for a response. If the user service takes 200ms to respond, the order service waits 200ms. Chain multiple calls and latency compounds. Service A calls B. B calls C. C calls D. Total latency is the sum of all calls plus network overhead. One service down breaks the chain. If the user service crashes, the order service cannot fetch user data. The order fails even though the order service itself works fine. Timeouts, retries, and circuit breakers mitigate these failures but add complexity. Use synchronous calls when you need immediate data to proceed. Payment processing needs confirmation now. User authentication blocks until credentials are verified. Real-time queries require current data. Message Queues for Asynchronous Flow The order service publishes an event: "OrderCreated." It does not wait for a response. The message goes to a broker like Kafka or RabbitMQ. The broker stores the message. The inventory service subscribes to OrderCreated events. When ready, it processes the message and reserves stock. The sender and receiver decouple. The order service does not know who consumes its events. Multiple services can subscribe. The inventory service reserves stock. The notification service sends an email. The analytics service logs the event. One message serves many consumers. The broker buffers messages. If the inventory service is slow, messages queue up. The order service continues publishing. Temporary slowdowns do not block senders. The broker provides fault tolerance. If a consumer crashes, messages wait until it recovers. Messages flow one way. The sender publishes and forgets. It does not get a response. For two-way communication, the receiver must publish a separate response message. This eventual consistency works when immediate confirmation is not required. 3/4

Use message queues when services operate at different speeds or when one event triggers multiple actions. Background jobs, notifications, and data pipeline events fit this pattern. Do not use message queues when you need an immediate response or strong consistency. Comparing Patterns Pattern RecipientsSync/AsyncResponseUse When Database Many Async None Centralized data, multiple readers Service CallsOne Sync ImmediateNeed data now to proceed Message Queue Many Async None Decouple producers and consumers Database dataflow suits read-heavy workloads with consistent schemas. Service calls work for interactive requests needing real-time responses. Message queues excel at asynchronous processing with variable consumer speeds. Real systems combine patterns. An e-commerce platform uses service calls for checkout, message queues for order processing, and databases for product catalogs. Choose based on latency requirements, consistency needs, and coupling tolerance. From here, push versus pull models in dataflow lead into how Twitter implements both patterns at scale. 4/4

Scaling Services › Dataflow

Push vs Pull in System Design

Open PDF

Push vs Pull in System Design Push Model: Server Initiates Data Transfer Server New data available Push immediately! Client 1 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 2 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 3 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 4 Receives update ✓ Real-time Push Push Push Push Push vs Pull We explored three dataflow patterns: databases, service calls, and message queues. But within these patterns, data moves in two ways. Either the producer pushes data to consumers, or consumers pull data from the producer. This choice affects latency, scalability, and resource usage. Push Model The server initiates data transfer. When new data appears, the server sends it to clients immediately without waiting for a request. A chat application demonstrates this. When Alice sends a message, the server pushes it to Bob's device. Bob does not poll for new messages. The server maintains connections to all online clients and pushes updates as they occur. WebSockets enable push in web applications. The client opens a persistent connection. The server keeps it alive and sends data whenever events happen. Stock trading platforms push price updates. News apps push breaking stories. IoT sensors push measurements to central systems. 1/4

Push delivers data with minimal latency. Updates arrive as soon as they are produced. Users see changes in real time. No polling delay exists between when data becomes available and when clients receive it. But push consumes server resources. The server tracks all connected clients. It maintains open connections and manages state for each one. A million concurrent users mean a million connections to maintain. Memory usage and network overhead scale with client count. Push works best when updates are frequent and unpredictable. Live sports scores change constantly. Financial data updates every second. Chat messages arrive randomly. If clients need immediate notification, push avoids polling waste. Push Model: Server Initiates Data Transfer Server New data available Push immediately! Client 1 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 2 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 3 Receives update ✓ Real-time Client 4 Receives update ✓ Real-time Push Push Push Push Pull Model The client initiates data requests. Clients ask for updates at intervals or on demand. The client polls the server: "Any new data?" The server responds with whatever is available. Web browsers work this way. Click a link. The browser requests a page. The server responds. API calls use pull. A mobile app calls GET /notifications every 30 seconds. The server returns new notifications if any exist. If nothing changed, it returns an empty list. The client controls request frequency. 2/4

Pull gives clients control over timing and rate. A client processing data slowly can reduce polling frequency. A client catching up can increase it. No server-side tracking of client state is needed. The server simply responds to incoming requests. Pull adds latency. New data sits on the server until the next poll. If a client polls every minute, updates lag by up to a minute. Reducing the polling interval increases freshness but wastes resources. Polling every second when updates come every hour generates 3,600 unnecessary requests. Empty polls waste bandwidth and compute. The client asks for new data. The server checks. Nothing new exists. Send an empty response. Both sides burned resources for no value. Under heavy polling, most requests return no data. Pull scales better on the producer side. The server does not track clients. It just answers requests as they arrive. No persistent connections to maintain. A stateless server handles millions of sporadic requests more easily than millions of open connections. Use pull when updates are infrequent or when clients have varying consumption rates. Configuration data changes rarely. An app can poll for config updates every hour. Background sync jobs run on their own schedule. Pull lets them fetch data when ready. Pull Model: Client Initiates Data Requests Server Responds when requested Client 1 Polls every 30s Client 2 Polls every 1m Client 3 On-demand Client 4 Polls every 5m Request Request Request Request Response Response Response Response 3/4

Comparing Push and Pull Aspect Push Pull Latency Immediate Delayed by polling interval Server ResourcesHigh (maintain connections)Low (stateless responses) Client Control Limited Full control over timing Network EfficiencyHigh (no empty polls) Low (many empty polls) Scalability Challenging with many clientsEasier to scale horizontally Best For Real-time updates Infrequent or scheduled updates Real systems blend both models. Email uses pull. Clients check for new messages periodically. But mobile push notifications alert users immediately. The combination gives real-time awareness without constant polling. Long polling bridges push and pull. The client sends a request. The server holds it open until new data arrives. When data becomes available or a timeout expires, the server responds. The client immediately sends another request. This provides near-real-time updates without WebSocket complexity. Server-sent events (SSE) are another hybrid. The client opens a connection with a standard HTTP request. The server keeps it open and streams updates. Unlike WebSockets, SSE is unidirectional. The server pushes. The client cannot send messages back over the same connection. Choose based on update frequency, latency requirements, and scale. High-frequency real-time data favors push. Sporadic or scheduled data favors pull. That leads into how Twitter combines both models to deliver timeline updates efficiently at massive scale. 4/4

Scaling Services › Dataflow

Push vs Pull in Twitters Timeline System Design

Open PDF

Push vs Pull in Twitter's Timeline System Design Fan-Out on Write (Push): 1 Write → Many Copies Bob Posts tweet Write Amplification 1 tweet → 1,000 timeline writes Alice's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed Carol's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed Dave's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed...997 more timelines ✓ Pre-computed Write Write Write Write Later: Alice Opens Twitter Fast Read! Just read pre-computed timeline Push vs Pull in Twitter Timeline Push delivers real-time updates but struggles with scale. Pull handles millions of clients but adds latency. Twitter timelines need both: immediate updates for active users and efficient resource usage for 300 million users. How does Twitter decide when to push and when to pull? The Timeline Problem A user opens Twitter. Their timeline shows tweets from everyone they follow. Alice follows 500 accounts. Some post frequently. Others post once a week. When Alice loads her timeline, the system must show recent tweets from all 500 accounts, sorted by time. The simple approach queries on demand. Fetch tweets from each followed account. Merge and sort them. Return the results. This is pull. It works for small follower counts but breaks at scale. Querying 500 accounts, sorting thousands of tweets, and merging results takes time. Every timeline load repeats this work. 1/5

Fan-Out on Write (Push) When Bob tweets, the system immediately writes that tweet to the timelines of all his followers. Bob has 1,000 followers. The system inserts Bob's tweet into 1,000 pre-computed timelines. When Alice opens Twitter, her timeline is already prepared. Just read and display. No merging. No sorting. Instant load. This is fan-out on write. One write creates many copies. The timeline becomes a cache. Reading is fast because the work happened during the write. Alice sees Bob's tweet immediately without waiting for queries. But fan-out on write amplifies writes. A celebrity with 50 million followers posts a tweet. The system must write to 50 million timelines. That single tweet generates 50 million database operations. Popular accounts create massive write spikes. Systems struggle under this amplification. Storage explodes. Every tweet appears in multiple timelines. A tweet seen by 10,000 users stores 10,000 copies. Disk usage scales with follower count times tweet volume. For accounts with millions of followers, storage costs become prohibitive. Fan-Out on Write (Push): 1 Write → Many Copies Bob Posts tweet Write Amplification 1 tweet → 1,000 timeline writes Alice's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed Carol's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed Dave's Timeline + Bob's tweet ✓ Pre-computed...997 more timelines ✓ Pre-computed Write Write Write Write Later: Alice Opens Twitter Fast Read! Just read pre-computed timeline 2/5

Fan-Out on Read (Pull) Instead of pre-computing timelines, query on demand. When Alice loads her timeline, fetch recent tweets from all 500 accounts she follows. Merge them. Sort by timestamp. Return the top 50. This is fan-out on read. The work happens during the read instead of the write. No write amplification exists. Bob tweets once. The system stores one copy. Regardless of follower count, it is one write operation. Celebrities and regular users cost the same on the write path. Storage stays efficient. No duplicate tweets across millions of timelines. But reads become expensive. Alice loads her timeline. Query 500 accounts. If each account has recent tweets, scan thousands of rows. Merge and sort. This computation happens every time Alice refreshes. Popular users like Alice refresh frequently. Multiply computation across millions of active users. Latency increases. Querying hundreds of accounts takes time. Merging and sorting adds more. Alice waits several hundred milliseconds for her timeline to load. The UX suffers. Fan-Out on Read (Pull): Query Many → Build Timeline Bob Posts tweet Efficient Write! Just 1 write to Bob's tweets Alice Loads timeline Follows 500 users Query User 1 Query User 2 Query User 3... 497 more Heavy Read Processing Merge 500 sources + sort by time Timeline Ready (after 200-500ms) Hybrid Approach Twitter uses both strategies based on user profiles. Most accounts have modest follower counts. Twitter uses fan-out on write for them. When Bob posts and has 1,000 followers, write to 1,000 timelines. The write amplification is manageable. Alice's timeline loads instantly. 3/5

Celebrities have millions of followers. Fan-out on write breaks. Instead, Twitter uses fan-out on read. When a celebrity tweets, store it once. When Alice loads her timeline, include a query for celebrities she follows. Merge celebrity tweets with the pre-computed timeline from regular accounts. Alice follows 490 regular users and 10 celebrities. The system pre-computes a timeline from the 490 regular users using fan-out on write. When Alice loads her timeline, it queries the 10 celebrity accounts separately and merges those tweets with her pre-computed feed. This hybrid gives Alice fast loads while avoiding write amplification for celebrities. Twitter's Hybrid Approach: Fan-Out on Write + Fan-Out on Read Regular Users (< 1M followers) Fan-Out on Write (Push) Bob 1,000 followers Posts tweet ↓ Write to 1,000 timelines Alice's timeline ✓ 997 more timelines ✓ Celebrities (> 1M followers) Fan-Out on Read (Pull) Celebrity 50M followers Posts tweet ↓ Store once only 1 copy ✓ When Alice Loads Timeline: Hybrid Query 1. Read pre-computed timeline (490 regular users) 2. Query 10 celebrity accounts separately 3. Merge results Alice's Complete Timeline ✓ Fast (mostly pre-computed) The threshold for switching strategies varies. Twitter might use 1 million followers as the cutoff. Accounts below use fan-out on write. Accounts above use fan-out on read. The exact threshold balances write cost against read complexity. Implementation Details Twitter tracks follower counts. When an account crosses the threshold, switch strategies. New tweets from that account stop writing to follower timelines. Instead, queries fetch those tweets on read. The transition is gradual. Existing timeline entries remain. New tweets follow the new pattern. 4/5

During read, the system runs two queries. One fetches the pre-computed timeline. Another queries high-follower accounts. Merge results in memory and return the top items. Caching helps. Cache celebrity tweets for a few seconds. Multiple users requesting timelines share the cached query results. For inactive users who rarely open Twitter, skip fan-out on write entirely. When they eventually load their timeline, compute it on demand. No point maintaining pre-computed timelines for users who have not logged in for months. Detect activity patterns and adapt. Trade-Offs Fan-out on write optimizes reads at the cost of writes. Use it when read frequency exceeds write frequency. Most Twitter users read more than they write. Pre-computing timelines makes sense for the majority. Fan-out on read optimizes writes at the cost of reads. Use it when writes would amplify excessively. Celebrities post tweets that reach millions. Avoiding that write amplification justifies slower timeline construction. The hybrid approach combines both. Optimize for the common case (regular users) while handling edge cases (celebrities) differently. This pattern applies beyond Twitter. Any system with highly skewed distribution benefits. E-commerce recommendations. Social networks. Content feeds. During system design interviews, discuss fan-out strategies. Explain write amplification. Show how follower count affects the decision. Demonstrate awareness of scale implications. This hybrid thinking separates strong candidates from those who only know one pattern. References Original twitter architecture talk by Twitter engineers in 2016 Design Twitter 5/5

Data Storage › Data Structures Behind Databases

Database Storage Engines System Design Primer

Open PDF

Database Storage Engines | System Design Primer HDD vs SSD Write Mechanics Hard Disk Drive (HDD) 7200 RPM Sequential: Head follows track Random: Head seeks between tracks Solid State Drive (SSD) Sequential Pattern Random Pattern Controller Sequential: Batch operations Random: Individual cell management Performance Characteristics HDD Performance Sequential Read: 150+ MB/s Random Read: 1-2 MB/s Seek Time: 8-15ms Rotational Latency: 4-8ms Why slow: Physical head movement SSD Performance Sequential Write: 500+ MB/s Random Write: 200-300 MB/s Access Time: 0.1ms Why sequential wins: • Batch erase/write operations Data Structures Behind Databases Note that this section is optional and provides an in-depth exploration of the data structures utilized in the construction of modern databases. It is not mandatory to possess this level of knowledge in order to solve system design questions. However, acquiring an understanding of these concepts will enhance your appreciation of various technologies and facilitate your comprehension of their documentations and learning new technologies. Understanding the storage engine, the underlying technology that powers databases, may seem like an endeavor meant only for database developers. However, as a software engineer or a system designer, knowing these intricacies has significant benefits. It helps with database selection - understanding why PostgreSQL excels at reads while Cassandra handles massive writes enables informed choices. It also aids performance tuning by revealing how configuration changes affect underlying data structures. Every database uses a storage engine that manages how data gets written to and read from disk. MySQL offers multiple storage engines (InnoDB, MyISAM, Memory) with different trade- offs. InnoDB uses B-trees for fast range queries, while RocksDB uses LSM trees for high write throughput. Sequential writes to disk are dramatically faster than random writes - often 10-100x on traditional drives. Even modern SSDs benefit from sequential access patterns through controller optimizations and wear leveling, though the gap is much smaller. This fundamental constraint drives database design decisions throughout the entire system. 1/7

Sequential Writes vs Random Writes Physical storage hardware heavily favors sequential operations over random access. Traditional hard drives have physical read/write heads that must seek to different locations. Sequential writes keep the head moving in one direction, while random writes require expensive seek operations. SSDs eliminate mechanical movement but still benefit from sequential writes through better wear leveling and internal optimizations. HDD vs SSD Write Mechanics Hard Disk Drive (HDD) 7200 RPM Sequential: Head follows track Random: Head seeks between tracks Solid State Drive (SSD) Sequential Pattern Random Pattern Controller Sequential: Batch operations Random: Individual cell management Performance Characteristics HDD Performance Sequential Read: 150+ MB/s Random Read: 1-2 MB/s Seek Time: 8-15ms Rotational Latency: 4-8ms Why slow: Physical head movement SSD Performance Sequential Write: 500+ MB/s Random Write: 200-300 MB/s Access Time: 0.1ms Why sequential wins: • Batch erase/write operations Kafka demonstrates this advantage in practice. Sequential writes to Kafka logs achieve 600 MB/sec on commodity hardware, while random database updates might only reach 60 MB/sec. This 10x difference drives architectural decisions across the entire system. Operating systems optimize for sequential access through larger buffer pools and predictive read-ahead. Database designers leverage these optimizations by structuring write patterns to align with filesystem behavior rather than fighting against it. 2/7

Sequential vs Random Write Performance Sequential Writes Storage Block Contiguous allocation HDD: 150+ MB/sSSD: 500+ MB/s • No seek time • Optimal for logs Random Writes Storage Block Fragmented allocation HDD: 5-10 MB/sSSD: 100-200 MB/s • High seek overhead • CPU/memory intensive Real-World Impact Kafka Logs 600 MB/s sequential writes Append-only design LSM Trees Sequential writes + merging Cassandra, RocksDB B-Tree Updates Random page modifications Traditional RDBMS Building a database from scratch At its core, a database stores and retrieves data. Building a simple key-value store reveals the fundamental challenges that sophisticated data structures solve. Simple File-Based Key-Value Store from typing import Optional, Dict, Any def __init__(self, db_dir: str = "simple_db"): self.db_dir = db_dir self.log_file = os.path.join(db_dir, "operations.log") self.index: Dict[str, int] = {} # Create database directory os.makedirs(db_dir, exist_ok=True) # Rebuild index from log file self._rebuild_index() def _rebuild_index(self): """Rebuild in-memory index from operation log""" if not os.path.exists(self.log_file): with open(self.log_file, 'r') as f: for line in f: operation = json.loads(line.strip()) if operation['type'] == 'set': self.index[operation['key']] = offset elif operation['type'] == 'delete': self.index.pop(operation['key'], None) offset = f.tell() 3/7

def set(self, key: str, value: Any) -> None: """Store a key-value pair""" 'type': 'set', Loading Python environment... This may take a few seconds on first load. This simple implementation reveals fundamental database challenges. Sequential writes to the log file are fast, but the log grows indefinitely. The in-memory index enables quick lookups but must be rebuilt on restart. These constraints drive the need for more sophisticated data structures in production databases. Real databases solve these problems with techniques like log compaction, persistent indexes, and write-ahead logs. Understanding these basic trade-offs helps explain why different databases choose different approaches for their storage engines. Hash Indexes Hash indexes store key-value pairs using a hash table structure. Each key gets hashed to determine its storage location, enabling O(1) average-case lookups. The database maintains an in-memory hash table that maps keys to file positions on disk. When storing data, the system appends records sequentially to data files and updates the hash table with the new record's location. Lookups hash the key, find the disk offset, and read the record directly. Bitcask (used by Riak) implements this approach effectively. All writes append to log files while an in-memory hash table tracks key locations. This design provides extremely fast writes and reads for workloads with many random key lookups. However, the entire key space must fit in memory, limiting scalability. Hash indexes excel at point lookups but cannot answer range queries like "find all users with IDs between 1000 and 2000." The sequential write pattern keeps write performance high, but periodic log compaction is needed to reclaim space from deleted records. 4/7

Hash Index Structure In-Memory Hash Table user:123 → offset 0 order:456→ offset 64 product:789→ offset 128 session:abc→ offset 192 O(1) key lookup Must fit in memory Data File (Append-Only) offset 0:user:123 → {"name": "Alice", "email": "alice@..."} offset 64:order:456 → {"total": 99.99, "items": [...]} offset 128:product:789 → {"name": "Widget", "price": 25.00} offset 192:session:abc → {"user_id": 123, "expires":...} Sequential writes only Needs periodic compaction Direct offset lookup WRITE 1. Append to file2. Update hash table READ 1. Hash table lookup2. Seek to offset Limitations • No range queries • Key space limited by memory • Requires periodic compaction B-Trees B-trees organize data in sorted order across multiple disk pages, enabling both fast point lookups and efficient range scans. Most traditional relational databases use B-tree indexes as their primary storage structure. B-trees maintain balance automatically as data grows, ensuring O(log n) performance for reads, writes, and range queries. Each internal node contains multiple keys and pointers to child nodes, while leaf nodes store the actual data. This structure minimizes disk seeks by keeping frequently accessed nodes in memory. MySQL's InnoDB storage engine clusters table data using a B-tree where the primary key determines row placement. Secondary indexes point back to primary key values rather than row locations. This design optimizes for primary key lookups while supporting efficient secondary index operations. However, B-tree updates can trigger cascading changes up the tree structure. Inserting a single record might require updating multiple disk pages to maintain balance. This write amplification becomes problematic for write-heavy workloads, especially with traditional spinning disks. 5/7

B-Tree Structure Root Node [50, 100] [20, 35] [70, 85] [120, 150] 5, 10, 15 data... 22, 25, 30 data... 40, 45, 48 data... 55, 60, 65 data... 75, 80, 82 data... 90, 95, 98 data... Range Query: 25 ≤ key ≤ 65 1. Navigate tree to find start (25) 2. Scan leaf nodes sequentially until 65 B-Tree Advantages • O(log n) point lookups • Efficient range scans • Self-balancing structure • Cache-friendly (few disk seeks) • Write amplification on updates Internal Nodes Leaf Nodes (sorted data) LSM Trees Log-Structured Merge trees optimize for write throughput by accepting temporary read penalties. Instead of updating data in place, LSM trees append writes to memory structures and periodically merge them to disk in sorted runs. LSM trees maintain data across multiple levels. New writes go to in-memory structures called memtables. When memtables fill up, they get flushed to disk as Level 0 sorted runs (SSTables). Background processes merge smaller runs into larger, sorted runs at higher levels. This structure keeps writes fast while eventually organizing data for efficient reads. Apache Cassandra uses LSM trees to handle massive write workloads across distributed clusters. Writes first go to commit logs and memtables, providing immediate durability and fast acknowledgment. Background compaction processes merge SSTables (Sorted String Tables) to optimize read performance and reclaim space. LSM trees can require checking multiple sorted runs to find a record, creating read amplification. Bloom filters help skip files that definitely don't contain a key, while careful tuning of compaction strategies balances write performance against read latency. 6/7

Level 2 (Larger SSTables) SST-A (10MB) SST-B (10MB) SST-C (10MB) Level 3+ (Largest SSTables) SST-X (100MB) SST-Y (100MB) Flush Compact Compact Write Process 1. Write to WAL 2. Insert into MemTable 3. Return success Read Process 1. Check MemTable 2. Check Level 1, 2, 3... 3. Use Bloom filters Compaction 1. Merge sorted runs 2. Remove duplicates 3. Understanding these trade-offs helps select appropriate databases for specific workloads. RocksDB (an LSM tree implementation) powers various systems from MySQL storage engines to blockchain databases, proving the versatility of well-designed storage abstractions. 7/7

Understand Its Structure Operations and Use-Cases Now

Open PDF

Understand Its Structure, Operations, and Use-Cases Now! B-Tree Structure Root Node [50, 100] [20, 35] [70, 85] [120, 150] 5, 10, 15 data... 22, 25, 30 data... 40, 45, 48 data... 55, 60, 65 data... 75, 80, 82 data... 90, 95, 98 data... Range Query: 25 ≤ key ≤ 65 1. Navigate tree to find start (25) 2. Scan leaf nodes sequentially until 65 B-Tree Advantages • O(log n) point lookups • Efficient range scans • Self-balancing structure • Cache-friendly (few disk seeks) • Write amplification on updates Internal Nodes Leaf Nodes (sorted data) B-tree B-tree: Disk-Optimized Tree Structure Binary search trees work well in memory but fail catastrophically on disk. Each node lookup requires a separate disk read. Searching through a million-record database means potentially thousands of disk operations, each taking 5-10 milliseconds. What should be a microsecond operation becomes seconds of waiting. B-trees solve the disk access problem by packing hundreds of keys into each node. Instead of storing one key per node like binary trees, B-tree nodes contain 50-500 keys. A single disk read fetches an entire node, making each I/O operation maximally useful. This architectural change transforms database performance. MySQL's InnoDB engine uses B+ trees (a B-tree variant) as its primary storage structure. PostgreSQL similarly relies on B-tree indexes. Without B-trees, relational databases would be unusably slow on traditional storage. 1/5

B-trees maintain three critical properties: they stay balanced (all leaf nodes at the same depth), sorted (keys within nodes are ordered), and wide (nodes contain many keys). These properties ensure consistent performance regardless of data insertion patterns. B-Tree Structure Root Node [50, 100] [20, 35] [70, 85] [120, 150] 5, 10, 15 data... 22, 25, 30 data... 40, 45, 48 data... 55, 60, 65 data... 75, 80, 82 data... 90, 95, 98 data... Range Query: 25 ≤ key ≤ 65 1. Navigate tree to find start (25) 2. Scan leaf nodes sequentially until 65 B-Tree Advantages • O(log n) point lookups • Efficient range scans • Self-balancing structure • Cache-friendly (few disk seeks) • Write amplification on updates Internal Nodes Leaf Nodes (sorted data) Node Structure and Constraints B-trees enforce strict structural rules that enable their disk optimization. Each order m B-tree has nodes containing between ⌈ m/2 ⌉ -1 and m-1 keys, except the root which can have as few as one key. This constraint ensures nodes stay between half-full and completely full, preventing degenerate cases that would hurt performance. The root node sits at the top and contains up to m-1 keys with m child pointers. Internal nodes contain keys plus pointers to child nodes. Keys within each node are sorted, allowing binary search within the node. Leaf nodes contain the actual data records along with their keys, and crucially, all leaves exist at the same tree depth. For database storage, typical B-tree orders range from 50 to 500, meaning each node contains dozens to hundreds of keys. This matches disk page sizes (4KB-16KB), maximizing the data retrieved per disk read. Search Operations Finding a record in a B-tree requires walking from root to leaf, using binary search within each node. For a tree with one million records and order 100, this means just 3-4 disk reads maximum. Each node access narrows the search space dramatically. 2/5

B-tree Search: Finding Key 65 25 | 50 | 75Step 1: Root 65 > 50, 65 < 75 → 60 | 70Step 2: Internal 65 > 60, 65 < 70 → 62 | 65Step 3: Leaf Found 65! 10 | 20 30 | 40 80 | 90 5 | 8 12 | 15 22 | 24 32 | 35 72 | 74 82 | 85 Search Performance • 3 disk reads to find any record (root → internal → leaf) • Each node contains 50-500 keys, maximizing data per disk access 1 2 3 The search algorithm compares the target key against keys in the current node. If found, the search succeeds. Otherwise, the algorithm follows the appropriate child pointer based on where the target key would fall in the sorted order. Range queries become especially efficient. After locating the starting key, the database can scan leaf nodes sequentially since they're sorted and often linked. This enables fast SELECT * FROM users WHERE age BETWEEN 25 AND 35 queries without additional tree traversals. Insertion and Node Splitting B-tree insertions always happen at leaf nodes to maintain sorted order and consistent depth. When inserting into a non-full leaf, the operation simply places the new key in sorted position. However, when a leaf node becomes full (contains m-1 keys), node splitting occurs. 3/5

B-tree Insertion & Node Splitting Process Step 1: Insert 37 into full leaf node 25 | 50 15 | 20 30 | 35 | 40 FULL (order 4) 37 Cannot insert! Step 2: Split leaf node 30 | 35 37 | 40 36 promotes up Step 3: Final tree after split propagation 25 | 36 | 50Root now has 3 keys 15 | 20 30 | 35 37 | 40 55 | 60 Node Splitting Algorithm When inserting into a full leaf node (m-1 keys): 1. Insert new key into sorted position (temporarily creating m keys) 2. Split node: left gets ⌊ m/2 ⌋ keys, right gets ⌊ m/2 ⌋ keys 3. Promote middle key to parent node 4. If parent is full, repeat splitting process recursively 5. May create new root if splitting reaches the top Performance Impact • Splits are rare: only when nodes full • Most insertions: O(log n) + 1 write • Split insertions: O(log n) + up to log(n) writes (cascade up tree) • Keeps nodes 50-100% full 1 2 3 During splits, the node divides into two nodes, each containing ⌈ m/2 ⌉ -1 keys. The middle key promotes to the parent node. If the parent is also full, splitting cascades upward, potentially creating a new root and increasing tree height by one level. This splitting mechanism maintains the half-full guarantee while keeping the tree balanced. Modern databases optimize splits by maintaining pages that are 2/3 full rather than completely full, reducing split frequency during bulk insertions. Production Database Usage MySQL's InnoDB engine implements B+ trees (a B-tree variant where data resides only in leaves) for both primary and secondary indexes. The clustered index organizes table data in primary key order, while secondary indexes contain pointers back to the clustered index. PostgreSQL uses B-trees for most index types, with typical page sizes of 8KB containing 100- 200 index entries. PostgreSQL's implementation includes optimizations like right-link pointers between sibling pages to improve concurrent access patterns. File systems also rely on B-trees. ext4 uses extent trees (a B-tree variant) to track file block allocations efficiently. NTFS organizes directory entries and file allocation tables using B+ tree structures. 4/5

B-tree Search Operation def __init__(self, keys=None, children=None, is_leaf=False): self.keys = keys or [] self.children = children or [] self.is_leaf = is_leaf def btree_search(node, target_key, path=None): """Search for a key in B-tree, tracking the path taken""" path.append(f"Visiting node with keys: {node.keys}") # Binary search within the node for i, key in enumerate(node.keys): if target_key == key: path.append(f"Found key {target_key} at position {i}") return target_key, path elif target_key < key: if node.is_leaf: path.append(f"Key {target_key} not found - reached leaf") return None, path path.append(f"Key {target_key} < {key}, following left child") return btree_search(node.children[i], target_key, path) # Key is greater than all keys in node path.append(f"Key {target_key} not found - reached leaf") return None, path path.append(f"Key {target_key} > all keys, following rightmost child") return btree_search(node.children[-1], target_key, path) # Build a sample B-tree (order 4) 5/5

Sorted String Tables System Design Primer

Open PDF

Sorted String Tables | System Design Primer SSTable File Format SSTable File (Immutable) Data Section (Sorted Key-Value Pairs) alice → {"name": "Alice", "age": 30, "city": "SF"} bob → {"name": "Bob", "age": 25, "city": "NYC"} charlie → {"name": "Charlie", "age": 35, "city": "LA"} david → {"name": "David", "age": 28, "city": "Austin"}... (sorted lexicographically) Sparse Index (Every Nth key) alice → 0 charlie → 256 frank → 512 john → 768 Points to byte offsets Metadata Section • Min/Max keys: "alice" / "zoe" • Record count: 1,048,576 • Compression: Snappy • Bloom filter data • Checksums • Creation timestamp • File size: 64MB Points to Key Properties: Immutable • Sorted • Binary Searchable • Compressed • Self-Describing SSTable Sorted String Tables are immutable file formats that store key-value pairs in lexicographic order on disk. SSTables aren't complete database systems - they're the storage format that enables efficient binary search over large datasets. Modern distributed databases like Cassandra, RocksDB, and LevelDB use SSTables as their fundamental building blocks within larger storage engines. The File Format Problem Database storage engines need efficient file formats for persisting sorted data to disk. Simple approaches like CSV files require sequential scanning, making lookups O(n) for large datasets. Random access file formats can provide fast lookups but struggle with range queries and compression. SSTables solve this by combining sorted order with binary searchable indexes. The idea behind SSTables came from Google's BigTable project: store data in immutable, sorted files with sparse indexes. Once written, an SSTable never changes - updates and deletes are handled by creating new SSTables at the system level. This immutability enables aggressive caching, compression, and concurrent access without complex locking. SSTables provide the foundation for LSM trees, which handle the complete write path through memory buffers and multi-level compaction. Understanding SSTables as a file format helps explain why LSM-based systems achieve such high performance for both reads and writes. 1/4

SSTable File Format An SSTable file contains three main components: the sorted data section, a sparse index, and optional metadata. The data section stores key-value pairs in lexicographic order, enabling binary search algorithms. The sparse index contains every Nth key and its file offset, trading memory usage for lookup performance. Metadata includes compression algorithms, bloom filter data, and file statistics. SSTable File Format SSTable File (Immutable) Data Section (Sorted Key-Value Pairs) alice → {"name": "Alice", "age": 30, "city": "SF"} bob → {"name": "Bob", "age": 25, "city": "NYC"} charlie → {"name": "Charlie", "age": 35, "city": "LA"} david → {"name": "David", "age": 28, "city": "Austin"}... (sorted lexicographically) Sparse Index (Every Nth key) alice → 0 charlie → 256 frank → 512 john → 768 Points to byte offsets Metadata Section • Min/Max keys: "alice" / "zoe" • Record count: 1,048,576 • Compression: Snappy • Bloom filter data • Checksums • Creation timestamp • File size: 64MB Points to Key Properties: Immutable • Sorted • Binary Searchable • Compressed • Self-Describing The sparse index is crucial for performance. Instead of indexing every key (which would consume enormous memory), SSTables index every 16KB or 64KB block. This reduces memory overhead while still enabling fast seeks to the approximate location of any key. Reading from an SSTable File Format from typing import Optional, List, Tuple, Dict """Read-only SSTable implementation demonstrating the file format""" def __init__(self, data_entries: List[Tuple[str, str]], block_size: int = 3): # Simulate an already-written SSTable file self.data = sorted(data_entries) # Data section (sorted) self.block_size = block_size # Build sparse index (every Nth entry) self.sparse_index: List[Tuple[str, int]] = [] for i in range(0, len(self.data), block_size): key, _ = self.data[i] self.sparse_index.append((key, i)) print(f"SSTable created with {len(self.data)} entries") print(f"Sparse index built with {len(self.sparse_index)} entries (every {block_size} keys)") 2/4

def get(self, search_key: str) -> Optional[str]: """Binary search lookup using sparse index + linear scan""" print(f" --- Looking up '{search_key}' ---") if not self.data: # Step 1: Binary search in sparse index to find block index_keys = [key for key, _ in self.sparse_index] block_idx = bisect.bisect_right(index_keys, search_key) - 1 block_idx = max(0, block_idx) # Handle edge case start_key, start_pos = self.sparse_index[block_idx] end_pos = len(self.data) if block_idx + 1 < len(self.sparse_index): _, end_pos = self.sparse_index[block_idx + 1] Reading from SSTables Reading from SSTables leverages the sorted structure and sparse index for efficient lookups. Point queries use a two-step process: binary search the sparse index to find the approximate block, then binary search within that block to find the exact key. This approach provides O(log n) performance while using minimal memory for the index. SSTable Read Process Query GET("charlie") Step 1 Binary Search Sparse Index alice → offset 0 charlie → offset 256 frank → offset 512 Found block at offset 256 Step 2 Binary Search in Block bob → data2 charlie → data3 david → data4 Found exact match! Return Result "data3" Performance Characteristics Time Complexity: • Sparse index: O(log B) where B = blocks • Block search: O(log K) where K = keys/block • Total: O(log N) for N total keys Memory Usage: • Only sparse index loaded in RAM • Data blocks read on demand Range Queries: Sequential scan from start position (leverages sorted order) Find start with sparse index, then scan until end key reached Range queries are particularly efficient because of the sorted layout. Once you find the start position using the sparse index, you can sequentially scan until you reach the end of the range. This makes SSTables excellent for analytics workloads that need to process ranges of data. 3/4

Write Limitations and LSM Integration SSTables are immutable by design - they provide no write operations after creation. Adding new keys or updating existing ones requires creating entirely new SSTable files. This limitation is exactly why LSM trees exist: they provide the write path through memory buffers (memtables) and handle the complexity of managing multiple SSTable files. The immutable nature makes SSTables ideal building blocks for distributed systems. Files can be replicated, cached aggressively, and accessed concurrently without coordination. Multiple readers can access the same SSTable simultaneously, and the files can be safely copied or moved without affecting ongoing operations. When systems need to "update" data, they create new SSTables with newer values and rely on the read path to find the most recent version. Space reclamation and file management are handled by the storage engine's compaction processes, which merge multiple SSTables and delete obsolete files atomically. 4/4
