LSM Trees System Design Primer

Open PDF

LSM Trees | System Design Primer LSM Tree Structure Memory (Level 0) MemTable (sorted)key1, key2, key3... Write-Ahead Log Fast writes, volatile Level 1 (Recent SSTables) SST-1 SST-2 SST-3 SST-4... Level 2 (Larger SSTables) SST-A (10MB) SST-B (10MB) SST-C (10MB) Level 3+ (Largest SSTables) SST-X (100MB) SST-Y (100MB) Flush Compact Compact Write Process 1. Write to WAL 2. Insert into MemTable 3. Return success Read Process 1. Check MemTable 2. Check Level 1, 2, 3... 3. Use Bloom filters Compaction 1. Merge sorted runs 2. Remove duplicates 3. Reclaim space LSM Tree Trade-offs Advantages • Fast sequential writes• High write throughput Considerations • Read amplification• Background compaction Use Cases • Time-series data• Write-heavy workloads LSM Tree SSTables provide sorted, immutable storage files but create a new problem: how do you efficiently handle continuous writes without creating a lot of small files? Log-structured merge (LSM) trees solve this by organizing SSTables into a hierarchy that balances write throughput with read performance. Traditional B-tree engines suffer from write amplification because each small change requires reading pages, modifying them, and writing them back to random disk locations. LSM trees eliminate this bottleneck by batching writes in memory and converting them to efficient sequential disk operations using SSTables as the storage format. Core Architecture Memory-first design 1/9

The core insight is deferring disk work until it's efficient. Instead of immediately updating disk structures, LSM trees buffer writes in a memory table (memtable) and periodically flush sorted batches to disk as immutable SSTable files. Background compaction merges overlapping files to maintain read performance. This architecture transforms random write patterns into predictable sequential operations that modern storage handles efficiently. Why batching works Sequential writes dramatically outperform random access. Creating a new SSTable for every write would be catastrophic - adding one key-value pair to an existing 1GB SSTable requires reading the entire file, merging the new entry, and writing over 1GB back to disk. Patrick O'Neil's 1996 paper introduced the solution: buffer writes in memory, flush them as sorted runs, and merge those runs when the system has spare I/O capacity. Leveled Organization File hierarchy Without structure, frequent flushing creates thousands of tiny files that destroy read performance. LSM trees use a pyramid hierarchy where fresh data starts at Level 0 and older data migrates to deeper levels through background compaction. Consider this progression: Memtable fills up → flushes to create a new Level 0 file (newest data) After several flushes → Level 0 accumulates 4-8 separate files Background compaction → merges Level 0 files into different Level 1 files Level 1 grows large → compacts into separate Level 2 files (typically when 10x larger) Pattern continues → each level contains different files, with Level 0 always holding the newest data Bounded lookup cost This hierarchy solves the file proliferation problem. Without levels, a single lookup might check hundreds of files. With levels, reads check at most 10 files in Level 0, plus one file per deeper level. The bounded lookup cost maintains query latency even as the database grows to terabytes. Production adoption Bigtable popularized the pattern in production, and today Cassandra, DynamoDB, and RocksDB rely on LSM variants for sustained write throughput. The trade-off is increased complexity - reads must check multiple locations and background compaction consumes significant I/O bandwidth. An LSM tree consists of a memtable for recent writes, immutable SSTables on disk, and a write-ahead log for crash recovery. The write-ahead log (WAL) records each operation before acknowledging it, ensuring durability while the memtable optimizes read-after-write latency. 2/9

LSM Tree Structure Memory (Level 0) MemTable (sorted)key1, key2, key3... Write-Ahead Log Fast writes, volatile Level 1 (Recent SSTables) SST-1 SST-2 SST-3 SST-4... Level 2 (Larger SSTables) SST-A (10MB) SST-B (10MB) SST-C (10MB) Level 3+ (Largest SSTables) SST-X (100MB) SST-Y (100MB) Flush Compact Compact Write Process 1. Write to WAL 2. Insert into MemTable 3. Return success Read Process 1. Check MemTable 2. Check Level 1, 2, 3... 3. Use Bloom filters Compaction 1. Merge sorted runs 2. Remove duplicates 3. Reclaim space LSM Tree Trade-offs Advantages • Fast sequential writes• High write throughput Considerations • Read amplification• Background compaction Use Cases • Time-series data• Write-heavy workloads Write operations Writes prioritize immediate acknowledgment over disk efficiency. Each write operation appends to the WAL, updates the memtable, and returns success immediately. This approach minimizes write latency while preserving durability. When the memtable reaches a configured size threshold (typically 64MB to 256MB), the engine flushes its contents to disk as a new Level 0 SSTable. Since SSTables are immutable, the flush process requires no coordination with concurrent readers. 3/9

LSM Tree Write Flow 1. Write Request PUT(key, value) Client request Write-Ahead Log Durability first SET key value MemTable Fast in-memory insert key → value other entries... When MemTable Gets Full MemTable Full Size threshold reached • 64MB typical limit • Trigger flush process Flush Level 0 SSTable Sorted data on disk alice → data1 bob → data2 Create new New MemTable Ready for new writes (empty) Background Compaction Level 0 4 SSTables Level 0 4 SSTables Compact Level 1 SSTable Merged & sorted Write Performance • O(1) memtable insert • Sequential WAL append • Immediate acknowledgment • Background compaction • No blocking on flush Write-Ahead Log (WAL) The write-ahead log solves the durability problem inherent in memory-based structures. Memtables disappear during crashes, potentially losing all writes since the last flush. The WAL prevents this by recording every operation in an append-only disk file before acknowledging the write. For example, a WAL entry may look like this: 2024-01-15 14:30:25.123 PUT user:123 "alice" 2024-01-15 14:30:25.456 DELETE user:456 2024-01-15 14:30:25.789 PUT session:abc "active" Recovery simply replays all WAL entries since the last successful flush, reconstructing the memtable state. WAL entries become redundant once their corresponding data reaches persistent storage as SSTables, allowing periodic truncation. Updates and deletes LSM trees maintain immutability by treating updates and deletes as new entries rather than modifications. An update writes a newer value for the same key. A delete writes a tombstone marker indicating the key should be considered removed. During reads, the engine searches from newest to oldest data, so recent entries automatically shadow older ones. 4/9

Tombstones and Read Correctness Write Sequence T1: Write PUT key1 → "v1" → SSTable-1 T2: Update PUT key1 → "v2" → SSTable-2 T3: Delete DELETE key1 → Tombstone Storage Layout (Newest → Oldest) SSTable-3 Level 0 (Newest) key1 → <TOMBSTONE> SSTable-2 Level 0 key1 → "v2" SSTable-1 Level 1 (Oldest) key1 → "v1" Read Process GET(key1) Check newest firstTombstone found!Return: key deleted After Compaction Merge & clean Merged SSTable Level 1 (key1 removed entirely) Benefits • No in-place edits • Readers see consistent snapshots • Tombstones eventually removed • Space reclaimed during compaction • Newer values shadow older ones Immutable SSTables use logical deletion: newer records shadow older ones without modification This approach preserves the immutable nature of SSTables while supporting the full spectrum of database operations. The trade-off is that obsolete data continues consuming disk space until background compaction removes it. Read operations Reads must check multiple data locations. The read path starts with the memtable for the most recent writes, then searches SSTables from newest to oldest until finding the key or exhausting all possibilities. Bloom filters eliminate most unnecessary disk access by quickly determining which SSTables definitely don't contain a key. Sparse indexes and block caches further optimize the lookup process. 5/9

LSM Tree Read Flow Read Request GET("alice") 1 MemTable Check first (newest) "alice" not found Continue to Level 0 2 Level 0 SSTables SSTable-003 Bloom Filter Negative SSTable-002 Bloom Filter Positive SSTable-001 Bloom Filter Negative 3. Check data SSTable-002 Data alice → {"v": 2} bob → {"v": 1}Found "alice"! If not found Level 1 SSTables Check bloom filters Binary search if positive Continue to Level 2... Return Result Read Optimizations Bloom Filters: • Skip SSTables that definitely don't contain the key Search Order: • MemTable → Level 0 → Level 1... • Stop at first match (newest) Performance: • Best case: O(1) from MemTable • Worst case: Check all levels Read Amplification • May need to check multiple SSTables to find a key• Compaction reduces number of levels over time Range scans exploit the sorted layout by streaming across files once compaction has merged overlapping ranges. The read path complexity increases with the number of levels and files, but bounded file counts per level keep worst-case latency predictable. Compaction Background compaction prevents file proliferation and maintains read performance. Without maintenance, writes create hundreds of small SSTable files that destroy lookup performance. Compaction periodically merges overlapping files into larger, consolidated SSTables. The process follows a predictable pattern: identify overlapping files, stream them in sorted key order, and emit a new SSTable containing the most recent version of each key. When multiple files contain the same key, sequence numbers determine precedence. Tombstones override older live values. The merge creates a new SSTable with fresh metadata, bloom filters, and checksums. Only after the new file reaches disk does the storage engine update its manifest to replace the input files. 6/9

LSM Compaction Flow 1. Select Overlapping Runs SSTable-A alice→v1, bob→v2 SSTable-B alice→v3, charlie→v1 SSTable-C bob→<TOMB>, dave→v1 Merge 2. Priority Queue Merge Priority Queue (Key Order) Step 1: alice (A:v1, B:v3) → emit alice:v3 (newest) Step 2: bob (A:v2, C:<TOMB>) → emit bob:<TOMB> (newest) Step 3: charlie (B:v1) → emit charlie:v1 Write 3. New Merged SSTable Compacted SSTable alice→v3, charlie→v1, dave→v1 (bob removed by tombstone) Consistency Guarantees During Compaction: • Input SSTables remain untouched • Readers see consistent catalog • New file built completely first Merge Rules: • Newest value wins for same key • Tombstones remove older versions • Output maintains sorted order After Completion: • Manifest updated atomically • Input files marked for deletion • Readers switch to new file Benefits: • Fewer files to check on reads • Space reclaimed from deletes • Improved sequential locality 4. Cleanup Delete input files Remove aftersync complete Write amplification trade-offs Write amplification is the fundamental trade-off. Each byte gets rewritten multiple times as it moves through the level hierarchy. RocksDB typically sees 10-15x write amplification on modern NVMe storage. This cost is amortized across many operations and happens in background threads without blocking user requests. Compaction strategies Compaction strategies depend on workload patterns. Leveled compaction maintains one sorted run per level, minimizing read amplification but increasing write amplification. Tiered compaction allows multiple runs per level, reducing write costs but requiring more file checks during reads. Production systems tune level sizes, fanout ratios, and compaction concurrency to balance these trade-offs. Production considerations Operational challenges Heavy write bursts, node repairs, or failed compactions create many overlapping Level 0 files. Reads continue working but traverse more bloom filters and disk blocks, increasing tail latency. Operators respond by throttling writes or adding compute resources to let compaction catch up. Monitoring Level 0 file counts and pending compaction work provides early warning before performance degrades. 7/9

A single partition key receiving most writes can saturate one shard's WAL and memtable capacity. Production clusters distribute hot keys across multiple partitions or implement adaptive load shedding to prevent compaction debt accumulation. Tuning and monitoring Level size ratios, target file sizes, and compaction strategies significantly impact performance. Files that are too small trigger constant rewrites. Files that are too large slow recovery and increase read latency. Write amplification monitoring, combined with read latency percentiles, guides operational decisions. When to use LSM trees Time-series ingestion, logging pipelines, and message queues benefit from sustained write throughput and predictable latency. Read-heavy workloads with strong locality often perform better with B-trees, which avoid multi-level lookups and compaction overhead. The choice depends on whether your bottleneck is write throughput or read efficiency. LSM Tree Implementation with Compaction from collections import OrderedDict from typing import Dict, List, Optional, Tuple def __init__(self, entries: List[Tuple[str, Optional[str]]], level: int): self.entries: Dict[str, Optional[str]] = dict(entries) self.level = level def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[str]: return self.entries.get(key) def __init__(self, memtable_limit: int = 4, fanout: int = 2): self.memtable: "OrderedDict[str, Optional[str]]" = OrderedDict() self.wal: List[Tuple[str, Optional[str]]] = [] self.levels: List[List[Run]] = [] self.memtable_limit = memtable_limit self.fanout = fanout def put(self, key: str, value: str): self._write(key, value) def delete(self, key: str): self._write(key, None) def _write(self, key: str, value: Optional[str]): self.wal.append((key, value)) self.memtable[key] = value print(f"memtable[{key}] = {value}") if len(self.memtable) >= self.memtable_limit: self._flush() def get(self, key: str) -> Optional[str]: # Check memtable first 8/9

if key in self.memtable: return self.memtable[key] The implementation demonstrates the core mechanics: writes batch in memory, flushes create sorted runs, compaction merges overlapping data, and tombstones handle deletes without mutating existing SSTables. 9/9

Data Storage › Storage

How to Choose the Right Storage Solution

Open PDF

How to Choose the Right Storage Solution Introduction to Storage Storage is the backbone for designing scalable systems. It's not just about having a place to put data; it's about how quickly you can access it, how securely it's stored, and how efficiently it can scale as your needs grow. Let's dive into the role of storage in system architecture and why choosing the right type of storage is crucial for meeting specific system requirements. The Role of Storage Imagine a library filled with an immense number of books. This library is meticulously organized, allowing you to find any book you need quickly. In this analogy, the library represents your storage system, and the books represent the data. Just as the organization of the library impacts how quickly you can find a book, the structure and type of your storage system affect how swiftly and efficiently you can access and manage your data. Storage in system architecture serves several key purposes: Persistence: Data needs to outlive the process that created it. Whether it's user profiles, transaction records, or session data, storage ensures that this information is preserved for future access. Performance: The speed at which data can be read or written directly impacts the performance of applications. Efficient storage solutions enhance application responsiveness and user satisfaction. Scalability: As an application grows, so does its data. Storage solutions must be able to scale, accommodating increasing amounts of data without compromising performance. 1/4

Reliability and Availability: Data must be reliably stored and readily available whenever needed. This involves protecting against data loss and ensuring that the system can withstand various failure scenarios. Types of Storage Systems The world of data storage can largely be categorized into three primary types: SQL, NoSQL, and Blob/Object Storage. Each serves different data storage needs and comes with its unique strengths and weaknesses. SQL (Structured Query Language) Databases SQL databases, also known as relational databases, are the traditional choice for storing structured data. They organize data into tables, which can relate to one another through primary and foreign keys, facilitating complex queries and transactions. When to Use: Complex transactions requiring atomicity, consistency, isolation, and durability (ACID). Systems where data integrity and relationships are crucial. Use cases that benefit from structured schema and SQL's powerful querying capabilities. NoSQL Databases NoSQL databases are designed to store, retrieve, and manage semi-structured and unstructured data. They come in various forms, including key-value stores, document databases, column-family stores, and graph databases. NoSQL is known for its flexibility, scalability, and performance with large volumes of data. Document Database Example: 2/4

{ "userID": "2", "name": "Bob", "orders": [{ "orderID": "102", "details": "Order details here..." }] } When to Use: Applications requiring high scalability and flexibility with data models. Projects where rapid development and iteration over data structures are necessary. Systems that handle large volumes of unstructured or semi-structured data. Blob/Object Storage Blob (Binary Large Object) or Object Storage is used to store unstructured data such as multimedia files, backups, and logs. This type of storage manages data as objects, each containing the data itself, metadata, and a unique identifier. Blob/Object Storage is highly scalable and accessible over HTTP/HTTPS, making it ideal for storing large amounts of unstructured data. When to Use: Storing large multimedia files (images, videos, audio). Data backups and disaster recovery solutions. Serving content directly to web browsers. Comparative Analysis Feature SQL Databases NoSQL Databases Blob/Object Storage Data Structure Structured Semi- Structured/Unstructured Unstructured Scalability Vertical Horizontal Horizontal TransactionsACID Varies Not applicable Querying Complex Queries Flexible, schema-less queries Metadata-based 3/4

Feature SQL Databases NoSQL Databases Blob/Object Storage Use Case Traditional web apps, ERP systems Big Data, Real-time analytics Multimedia storage, backups Importance of Choosing the Right Type of Storage Selecting the appropriate storage solution is akin to choosing the right tool for a job. Just as you wouldn't use a hammer to screw in a bolt, you wouldn't use a key-value database to store relational data that requires complex joins. The choice of storage impacts not only the performance and scalability of your system but also its complexity and cost. Considerations for choosing the right type of storage include: Data Model: What kind of data are you storing? Is it structured, semi-structured, or unstructured? Does it fit neatly into tables, or is it more free-form? Access Patterns: How will the data be accessed and manipulated? Will there be more reads than writes, or vice versa? Do you need fast reads, fast writes, or both? Scalability Requirements: How much data do you expect to store now and in the future? Does your storage need to scale horizontally (across more machines) or vertically (adding more resources to a single machine)? Consistency Requirements: How important is data consistency? Do you need strong consistency, or is eventual consistency acceptable? Cost: What are your budget constraints? Different storage solutions come with different cost profiles, influenced by factors such as capacity, transaction rates, and operational overhead. Choosing the right storage solution is critical for building efficient, reliable, and scalable applications. It requires a deep understanding of both your current needs and anticipated future requirements. In the following sections, we'll explore the different types of storage systems — SQL, NoSQL, and Blob/Object storage — and provide guidance on selecting the best option for your specific use case. 4/4

Data Storage › Storage

SQL Database

Open PDF

SQL Database We explored three storage categories: SQL, NoSQL, and Blob storage. SQL databases enforce structure and relationships. This article explains why that structure matters and when to use it. The Problem Without Structure A bank processes account transfers. Customer A transfers $100 to Customer B. The operation requires two steps: deduct $100 from A, add $100 to B. A network error occurs after the deduction but before the addition. Account A loses $100. Account B gains nothing. The money vanishes. File-based storage can't prevent this. Neither can most key-value stores. You need coordinated operations that either fully succeed or fully fail. This guarantee is the foundation of SQL databases. 1/7

Partial Failure Without Transactions Step 1: Initial State Account A Balance: $500 Account B Balance: $200 Step 2: Deduct $100 from A ✓ Account A Balance: $400 Account B Balance: $200 Network Error! Step 3: Add $100 to B ✗ (Failed) Account A Balance: $400 Account B Balance: $200 Inconsistent State! • Total before: $700 • Total after: $600 • Missing: $100 Without transactions, partial failures corrupt data permanently Why SQL Exists Production systems face three related problems that unstructured storage cannot solve. First, race conditions corrupt data. Two ticket buyers read seats_available=1 simultaneously. Both proceed to purchase. The second write overwrites the first. Two customers hold tickets for the same seat. File systems and simple databases let both writes succeed. Second, partial failures create inconsistent state. An e-commerce order involves inventory decrement, payment processing, and order creation. If payment succeeds but inventory update fails, the store oversells. Manual reconciliation takes hours or days. Third, unstructured data drifts over time. One engineer stores phone numbers as integers. Another uses strings with dashes. A third includes country codes. Queries break. Reports fail. Schema enforcement prevents this drift. SQL databases solve these problems through structured schemas and transactional guarantees. How SQL Databases Work SQL organizes data into tables. Each table represents an entity type: customers, orders, products. Rows represent individual instances. Columns define attributes with specific data types. CREATE TABLE Customers ( CustomerID int PRIMARY KEY, Name varchar(255) NOT NULL, Email varchar(255) UNIQUE, Balance decimal(10,2) DEFAULT 0.00 ); 2/7

This schema enforces rules before data enters the database. CustomerID must be an integer and unique. Name cannot be null. Email must be unique across all rows. Balance must be a decimal with exactly two places. Attempting to insert "invalid" into CustomerID fails immediately. Primary Keys. Every table needs a unique identifier for each row. CustomerID serves this role. The database creates an index on this column, enabling fast lookups. Without a primary key, you cannot reliably update or delete specific rows. Tables and Relationships Tables connect through foreign keys. An Orders table references Customers through CustomerID: CREATE TABLE Orders ( OrderID int PRIMARY KEY, CustomerID int NOT NULL, Amount decimal(10,2), OrderDate timestamp, FOREIGN KEY (CustomerID) REFERENCES Customers(CustomerID) ); The foreign key constraint enforces referential integrity. You cannot create an order for a non- existent customer. Attempting INSERT INTO Orders (CustomerID) VALUES (999) fails if customer 999 doesn't exist. This prevents orphaned records. Relationships enable complex queries across multiple tables. Find all orders for a specific customer: SELECT o.OrderID, o.Amount, o.OrderDate FROM Orders o JOIN Customers c ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID WHERE c.Email = 'user@example.com'; The database uses indexes to execute this join efficiently, avoiding full table scans. 3/7

Tables and Foreign Key Relationships Customers Column Type CustomerID int PK Name varchar(255) Email varchar(255) Country varchar(255) Sample Row 42 Acme Corp | acme@ex.com | USA Orders Column Type OrderID int PK CustomerID int FK Amount decimal(10,2) OrderDate timestamp Sample Row 101 42 | $99.99 | 2024-10-08 FOREIGN KEY Orders.CustomerID must reference an existing Customers.CustomerID ACID Transactions ACID properties guarantee reliable transaction processing. Atomicity treats multiple operations as a single unit. Consistency enforces schema rules and constraints. Isolation prevents concurrent transactions from interfering. Durability ensures committed data survives crashes. Consider the bank transfer example. Wrap both operations in a transaction: BEGIN TRANSACTION; UPDATE Accounts SET Balance = Balance - 100 WHERE AccountID = 'A' AND Balance >= 100; UPDATE Accounts SET Balance = Balance + 100 WHERE AccountID = 'B'; COMMIT; Atomicity ensures both updates execute or neither does. If the second update fails, the database rolls back the first. The network error scenario becomes impossible. Consistency enforces the balance check; accounts can't go negative. Isolation prevents other transactions from reading intermediate state. Different isolation levels trade off correctness for performance. Durability writes committed changes to disk before acknowledging success. Write-Ahead Log (WAL). Databases write changes to a sequential log before modifying data files. Each log entry contains the old value and new value. On crash, the database replays the log to restore consistent state. This enables durability without sacrificing write performance. 4/7

ACID Transaction: Atomicity Guarantees Success Path: All Operations Complete 1. BEGIN TRANSACTION Start transaction scope 2. UPDATE Account A Balance: $500 → $400 3. UPDATE Account B Balance: $200 → $300 4. COMMIT All changes permanent ✓ Failure Path: Automatic Rollback 1. BEGIN TRANSACTION Start transaction scope 2. UPDATE Account A Balance: $500 → $400 Error! 3. UPDATE Account B Network failure ✗ 4. ROLLBACK Undo all changes ↶ ACID Properties in Action Atomicity: Both updates succeed or both roll back Consistency: Balance checks enforced (no negative values) Isolation: Other transactions don't see intermediate state Database State Initial A: $500 | B: $200 Total: $700 After Success ✓ A: $400 | B: $300 Total: $700 ✓ After Rollback ↶ A: $500 | B: $200 Total: $700 ✓ Basic SQL Operations SQL provides four fundamental operations. SELECT retrieves data. INSERT adds rows. UPDATE modifies existing data. DELETE removes rows. Retrieve customers in the USA: SELECT Name, Email FROM Customers WHERE Country = 'USA'; Add a new customer: INSERT INTO Customers (Name, Email, Country) VALUES ('Acme Corp', 'contact@acme.com', 'USA'); Update contact information: UPDATE Customers SET Email = 'new@acme.com' WHERE CustomerID = 42; Delete a customer: DELETE FROM Customers WHERE CustomerID = 42; These operations combine with transactions to enable complex workflows while maintaining consistency. When to Use SQL Databases SQL databases excel when data relationships and integrity matter more than flexibility. 5/7

Financial systems require atomic multi-account updates. A payment processing system debits one account and credits another. These operations must succeed together or fail together. SQL transactions provide this guarantee. Stripe's payment infrastructure uses PostgreSQL to ensure payment consistency. E-commerce platforms manage inventory, orders, and customer data with strict referential integrity. An order must reference an existing customer and product. Inventory decrements must match order creation. Foreign key constraints enforce these relationships. Attempting to delete a product with pending orders fails automatically. Healthcare systems store patient records, appointments, and prescriptions. Privacy regulations demand audit trails showing who accessed which records when. SQL databases provide row- level permissions and transaction logs. Regulatory compliance often requires this level of control. Reporting systems execute complex analytical queries. Calculate revenue by product category and region for the last quarter: SELECT p.Category, c.Region, SUM(o.Amount) as Revenue FROM Orders o JOIN Customers c ON o.CustomerID = c.CustomerID JOIN Products p ON o.ProductID = p.ProductID WHERE o.OrderDate >= '2024-10-01' GROUP BY p.Category, c.Region; SQL's query optimizer handles joins and aggregations efficiently. Building this query in a key- value store requires application-level processing. Popular SQL Databases PostgreSQL offers advanced features including custom data types, full-text search, and JSON support. It handles both transactional workloads and complex analytics. Instacart uses PostgreSQL for order processing at scale. MySQL prioritizes simplicity and read performance. Many web applications use MySQL for user data and content management. Its replication features support read-heavy workloads. Oracle Database provides enterprise features like advanced partitioning and multi-version concurrency control. Large corporations use Oracle for mission-critical applications requiring high availability. Challenges with SQL Databases Scaling SQL databases requires careful planning. A single server handles limited throughput. Adding capacity means choosing between vertical and horizontal scaling. Vertical scaling adds CPU, RAM, and faster disks to existing servers. This approach is simple but expensive. Hardware costs grow exponentially. A server with 512GB RAM costs more than twice a 256GB server. Eventually you hit physical limits. 6/7

Horizontal scaling distributes data across multiple servers through sharding. This requires partitioning data by some key. User data might partition by user ID ranges. Orders might partition by creation date. Queries spanning partitions become expensive. Joins across servers require network round trips and application-level coordination. Database Sharding. Partition data across multiple independent databases. Each shard handles a subset of data. User IDs 1-1000000 go to shard A, 1000001-2000000 to shard B. Queries for a single user remain fast. Cross-shard aggregations require querying all shards and merging results. Choose partition keys carefully to minimize cross-shard operations. We cover sharding strategies in detail in the database partitioning section. Database Sharding: Horizontal Partitioning Application Routes by UserID shard = UserID % 3 UserID % 3 = 0 UserID % 3 = 1 UserID % 3 = 2 Shard A (Server 1) UserID | Name 3 | Alice 6 | David 9 | Grace 12 | Jack... Shard B (Server 2) UserID | Name 1 | Bob 4 | Emma 7 | Frank 10 | Henry... Shard C (Server 3) UserID | Name 2 | Carol 5 | Eve 8 | Iris 11 | Kim... Fast single-user queries | Slow cross-shard aggregations | Choose partition key carefully Replication helps with read scaling. Write to a primary server. Replicas copy changes and serve reads. This works when reads vastly outnumber writes. Write-heavy workloads still bottleneck on the primary. Schema changes become complex at scale. Adding a column to a billion-row table locks the table for hours. Production systems use online migration tools that copy data incrementally while serving traffic. GitHub's gh-ost demonstrates this approach. Microservices architectures complicate SQL usage. Traditional monoliths use a single shared database. Microservices favor a database per service. This improves independence but loses transactional guarantees across services. Implementing distributed transactions or sagas adds complexity. 7/7

Flexibility Scalability and Modern Applications

Open PDF

Flexibility, Scalability, and Modern Applications Introduction to NoSQL Databases We explored SQL databases and their ACID guarantees. They excel at structured data with complex relationships. But they struggle with two modern problems: flexible schemas and horizontal scaling. The Schema Rigidity Problem A social media startup stores user profiles in PostgreSQL. Each user has a name, email, and bio. The schema looks clean: CREATE TABLE users ( user_id int PRIMARY KEY, name varchar(255), email varchar(255), bio text ); Three months later, the product team wants verified badges for some users. Add a column. Six months later, premium users get custom themes. Add another column. A year in, influencers need follower counts, average engagement rates, and top hashtags. Every new feature requires a schema migration on a table with 10 million rows. Each migration locks the table. Deployments take hours. Meanwhile, a competitor using MongoDB adds new fields instantly. No migration. No downtime. They ship features weekly while the SQL-based startup ships monthly. The Horizontal Scaling Problem An e-commerce site stores product catalogs in MySQL. Traffic grows. The database hits CPU limits. Vertical scaling helps temporarily: upgrade from 8 cores to 16, then 32, then 64. Eventually hardware limits appear. A 128-core server costs exponentially more than two 64- 1/4

core servers. SQL databases struggle with horizontal scaling. Sharding requires manual partition key selection and application-level routing. Joins across shards become expensive. Foreign key constraints break. The team spends months building sharding logic instead of features. NoSQL databases solve both problems. They embrace flexible schemas and distribute data automatically. How NoSQL Works NoSQL databases store data without fixed schemas. Different records can have different fields. Adding new fields requires no migration. The term "NoSQL" means "Not Only SQL." These databases sacrifice some SQL guarantees for flexibility and scalability. They typically trade strong consistency for availability and partition tolerance. Schema Flexibility A document database stores user profiles as JSON: { "user_id": 123, "name": "Alice", "email": "alice@example.com", "bio": "Software engineer" } Add a badge field for one user: { "user_id": 456, "name": "Bob", "email": "bob@example.com", "verified": true, "badge_color": "gold" } No migration needed. The database accepts both documents. Application code handles missing fields. This flexibility enables rapid iteration. Horizontal Scaling NoSQL databases partition data automatically. DynamoDB distributes records across multiple servers based on partition keys. Add more servers to increase capacity. The database rebalances data automatically. Cassandra replicates data across nodes with configurable consistency. Write to three replicas for durability. Read from one for speed. The system remains available even when nodes fail. Types of NoSQL Databases NoSQL databases optimize for different access patterns. Four main categories exist. Key-Value Stores map keys to values. Redis stores session data. DynamoDB handles high- throughput lookups. These databases excel at simple get/put operations with predictable performance. 2/4

Document Databases store JSON-like documents. MongoDB manages product catalogs. CouchDB handles content management. Documents can have nested fields and arrays, matching application object models. Column-Family Stores organize data by columns instead of rows. Cassandra handles time- series data. HBase processes analytics workloads. Scanning specific columns becomes efficient while full-row scans remain expensive. Graph Databases model relationships explicitly. Neo4j powers social networks. ArangoDB supports recommendation engines. Traversing relationships (friend-of-friend queries) becomes fast while aggregations remain slow. Each type trades off different characteristics. Choose based on access patterns, not just data structure. When to Use NoSQL NoSQL databases fit specific scenarios where SQL struggles. Unpredictable schemas benefit from NoSQL flexibility. Instagram's user profiles evolved rapidly during growth. Adding profile fields weekly would cripple MySQL migrations. Cassandra absorbed changes without downtime. High write throughput demands horizontal scaling. Discord stores trillions of messages. A single database can't handle millions of writes per second. Cassandra partitions writes across hundreds of nodes. Simple access patterns don't need SQL complexity. Shopping carts require key-value lookups by session ID. Session data needs no joins or transactions. Redis provides microsecond latency without SQL overhead. Global distribution benefits from eventual consistency. Social media feeds can tolerate stale data for seconds. Users don't notice if a like count updates with slight delay. DynamoDB replicates globally with configurable consistency. Large unstructured datasets exceed SQL capabilities. Log aggregation systems collect gigabytes per second. Elasticsearch indexes and searches text without schemas. SQL full-text search becomes impractical at this scale. Challenges with NoSQL NoSQL databases sacrifice guarantees that SQL provides. Eventual consistency complicates application logic. Two users editing the same document might see different versions. Application code must handle conflicts. Dynamo's shopping cart merges conflicting updates by keeping all items ever added. Users occasionally see deleted items reappear. 3/4

No ACID transactions across records forces workarounds. Transferring money between accounts requires two separate updates. If the system fails between updates, money disappears or duplicates. Applications implement saga patterns or accept inconsistency. Limited query capabilities require denormalization. SQL joins multiple tables efficiently. NoSQL databases store redundant data to avoid joins. A user's posts include their name in each document. Name changes require updating thousands of posts. Operational complexity increases. SQL databases mature tooling exists for backup, monitoring, and debugging. NoSQL databases require learning new tools. Cassandra's eventual consistency makes debugging difficult. DynamoDB's pricing model requires careful capacity planning. Choosing Between SQL and NoSQL Most applications can use either database type. Start with the simpler choice. PostgreSQL handles 99% of use cases including JSON fields and horizontal scaling extensions. Use NoSQL when specific requirements demand it. Need millions of writes per second? Use Cassandra. Schema changes deploy daily? Use MongoDB. Simple key-value lookups only? Use Redis. Many systems use both. E-commerce sites store orders in PostgreSQL for ACID guarantees, cache sessions in Redis for speed, and index products in Elasticsearch for search. Choose the right tool for each component. We cover specific NoSQL types in detail next: key-value databases, document databases, and full-text search. 4/4

Data Storage › Storage

Key-value Database

Open PDF

Key-value Database NoSQL databases optimize for different access patterns. The simplest pattern is direct key- value lookup. Many applications need only this operation: session storage, caching, user preferences. Why Key-Value Databases Exist In the original Dynamo paper, Amazon engineers identified a pattern: Many services on Amazon's platform only need primary-key access to a data store. Services providing best seller lists, shopping carts, customer preferences, session management, sales rank, and product catalog use relational databases inefficiently. This pattern limits scale and availability. Dynamo provides a simple primary-key only interface to meet these requirements. 1/3

Key-value databases are the simplest NoSQL type. The interface resembles a hashmap. Store values by key. Retrieve values by key. That's it. How Key-Value Databases Work The data model couldn't be simpler. Each record has two parts: a unique key and a value. The database provides three operations: get(key), put(key, value), and delete(key). No schemas. No relationships. No joins. This simplicity enables performance. Primary key lookups are fast. The database can hash the key to find the exact location. No table scans. No index traversals. Typical latency measures in microseconds. Horizontal scaling is straightforward. Partition data by key ranges or hash values. Add more servers to increase capacity. Each server handles a subset of keys. The partitioning logic remains simple because queries never span partitions. The API matches programming language dictionaries. Developers already understand get and put. No SQL to learn. No ORM complexity. Application code and database model align naturally. When to Use Key-Value Databases High-performance reads and writes benefit from key-value simplicity. Reading and writing with a key is a fast operation. Applications demanding high-speed operations choose key-value stores. Simple data structures fit naturally. Customer details, webpages keyed by URL, shopping-cart contents, product categories, and e-commerce product details work well. Each entity needs independent access without relationships. Basic query requirements match key-value capabilities. Applications that don't need complex queries or frequent updates across multiple records work well. Simple data storage and retrieval operations are ideal. Popular Key-Value Databases Because of their performance and simplicity, key-value databases rank highly in popularity according to db engine ranking. 2/3

Redis and DynamoDB lead the category. Redis specializes in in-memory caching with microsecond latency. DynamoDB provides durable, distributed storage with single-digit millisecond latency. Real-World Examples Dropbox stores file metadata in DynamoDB through their Alki system. Each file's metadata maps to a unique file ID. No relationships between files matter. Simple key-value lookups provide the speed needed. This saved Dropbox millions in infrastructure costs compared to their previous on-premises storage. Snapchat stores Stories in DynamoDB. Story ID maps to content metadata. Stories don't relate to each other. Ephemeral nature matches key-value simplicity. Snap handles over 10 million queries per second and reduced median latency by 20%. Disney+ uses DynamoDB for content metadata and user interactions. Video ID maps to title, description, and thumbnail URL. Each video loads independently. They ingest billions of customer actions daily for features like watchlists and personalized recommendations. 3/3

Data Storage › Storage

Document Database

Open PDF

Document Database Key-value databases provide simple lookups. But many applications need nested data structures: user profiles with addresses, blog posts with comments, products with specifications. Document databases solve this problem. The ORM Problem Douglas Crockford published the JSON specification in 2001. JSON became the standard for web APIs. Applications communicate using JSON. Browsers send JSON. Servers respond with JSON. But databases store tables and rows. Storing JSON in SQL databases creates friction. A user profile contains nested addresses and multiple phone numbers. SQL requires separate tables: users, addresses, phone_numbers. Insert one profile? Execute three INSERT statements. Read one profile? JOIN three tables. Object-relational mappers translate between JSON and SQL. ORMs work until they don't. Nested arrays require complex mapping. Schema migrations break existing code. Performance suffers from N+1 query problems. 1/3

How Document Databases Work Document databases store JSON directly. No translation layer. No ORM complexity. The application sends JSON. The database stores JSON. Reading returns JSON. Each document is self-contained. A user profile includes all related data in one document. Addresses nest inside. Phone numbers nest inside. One read operation retrieves everything. Documents can have different fields. One user has verified badges. Another doesn't. The database accepts both. No schema migration needed. Add fields to new documents. Old documents remain unchanged. When to Use Document Databases Document databases work best for self-contained content without strong relationships. User profiles fit perfectly. A Reddit profile displays when someone visits your page. No complex queries needed. New features require new fields. Add rewards: [] without migration. The same applies to merchant profiles in Yelp, venue profiles in TicketMaster, and e-commerce listings. Content management systems benefit from document databases. WordPress stores blog posts. Each post contains title, body text, and tags array. Posts are mostly independent. Retrieving one post shouldn't require joins. Flexible schemas allow custom fields per post. Product catalogs match document patterns. An electronics product has different specifications than clothing. Electronics need screen size and battery life. Clothing needs size and material. Document databases store both in the same collection with different fields. When Not to Use Document Databases Avoid document databases when data needs strong consistency, complex relationships, and strict normalization. Banking applications managing account transfers require ACID transactions. Use relational databases instead. 2/3

Interactive Example Try running MongoDB commands to see document databases in action: // create a new collection db.createCollection("users"); // insert one document into the collection db.users.insert({ name: "John Doe", age: 30, email: "john.doe@example.com" }); // insert two documents into the collection db.users.insertMany([ { name: "Jane Smith", age: 28, email: "jane.smith@example.com" }, { name: "Bob Johnson", age: 45, email: "bob.johnson@example.com" }, ]); // find all documents db.users.find(); // find a specific document db.users.findOne({ name: "John Doe" }); // update a document db.users.updateOne({ name: "John Doe" }, { $set: { age: 31 } }); // delete a document db.users.deleteOne({ name: "John Doe" }); // add a new field db.users.updateOne( { name: "John Doe" }, { $set: { address: "123 Main St, Springfield" } } ); 3/3

Data Storage › Storage

Going Beyond SQLs LIKE for Lightning-Fast Results

Open PDF

Going Beyond SQL's "LIKE" for Lightning-Fast Results Full-text Search Database Document databases store JSON efficiently. But searching text within documents requires different technology. Product descriptions, blog posts, and customer reviews need full-text search. The LIKE Keyword Problem SQL databases provide the LIKE keyword for text search. Search for products containing "wireless headphones": SELECT * FROM products WHERE description LIKE '%wireless%headphones%'; SQL scans every row. Check the description column. Does it match the pattern? Include it in results. This full table scan examines millions of rows. A products table with 10 million entries takes seconds to search. The wildcards make indexing impossible. B-tree indexes work for prefix matches. LIKE 'apple%' uses an index. But LIKE '%apple%' cannot. The database must read every row. Large tables make this impractical. E-commerce sites need sub-second search. SQL LIKE can't deliver. Full-text search databases solve this problem. 1/5

Inverted Index Inverted indexes map terms to documents. Unlike a book's table of contents mapping pages to topics, an inverted index maps topics to pages. This reversal enables fast lookups. Consider a collection of documents with their respective document IDs: Document IDDocument 1 The cat is black 2 The dog is brown 3 The mouse is grey 4 The cat and the dog are friends To create an inverted index, we first tokenize the documents by splitting them into individual terms and then associate each term with the corresponding document IDs where it appears. Here's how the inverted index would look for the given example: Term Document IDs and 4 are 4 black 1 brown 2 2/5

Term Document IDs cat 1, 4 dog 2, 4 friends4 grey 3 is 1, 2, 3 mouse3 the 1, 2, 3, 4 Find documents containing "cat"? Look up "cat" in the index. Return documents 1 and 4. No table scan needed. The lookup takes constant time. The index can store additional metadata. Term frequencies enable ranking. Term positions support phrase queries. Timestamps enable recency sorting. This metadata powers advanced search features. Popular Full-Text Search Databases There are several full-text search databases available today, each with its unique features and optimizations. Some popular choices include: Database Name Cloud Provider Distinct Feature Elasticsearch AWS, GCP, Azure, Open Source Probably the most popular full-text search database out there. Real-time analytics, extensive query capabilities, and horizontal scaling for large data volumes. Amazon CloudSearch AWS Seamless integration with other AWS services, automatic scaling, and language-specific text processing. Azure Cognitive Search Azure AI-powered features like semantic search, computer vision, and natural language processing capabilities. Google Cloud Search GCP Machine learning-based search quality improvement and integration with G Suite applications. Algolia Other (SaaS)Comprehensive typo-tolerance, and customizable ranking rules. Apache Solr Open SourceAdvanced faceted search, distributed searching, and extensible plugin architecture. MeiliSearch Open SourceLow memory footprint, instant search-as-you-type experience, and straightforward API integration. 3/5

Demo Here is an example of a more realistic e-commerce application using Elasticsearch and Python. We would first create an Elasticsearch index with a custom mapping that defines the fields and their types for a product document. We would also have more fields, such as price, category, and description, and handle features like pagination, sorting, and filtering. from elasticsearch import Elasticsearch # Initialize Elasticsearch client es = Elasticsearch(" # Define the index mapping for products mapping = { "mappings": { "properties": { "name": {"type": "text"}, "description": {"type": "text"}, "price": {"type": "double"}, "category": {"type": "keyword"}, } } } # Create the index with the mapping es.indices.create(index="products", body=mapping) # Index sample products products = [ {"id": 1, "name": "Apple iPhone 13", "description": "Latest iPhone model with a 120Hz display.", "price": 999.0, "category": "smartphones"}, {"id": 2, "name": "Samsung Galaxy S22", "description": "Flagship Samsung smartphone with a versatile camera system.", "price": 899.0, "category": "smartphones"}, {"id": 3, "name": "Google Pixel 6", "description": "Google's newest smartphone with stock Android experience.", "price": 799.0, "category": "smartphones"}, {"id": 4, "name": "Apple MacBook Pro", "description": "High-performance laptop with Apple's M1 Pro chip.", "price": 2499.0, "category": "laptops"}, ] for product in products: es.index(index="products", id=product["id"], body=product) # Refresh the index to make the newly added documents searchable es.indices.refresh(index="products") # Simulate user input user_input = "smartphone" # Define the search query with pagination, sorting, and filtering query = { "from": 0, # Starting index for pagination "size": 10, # Number of results per page "query": { "multi_match": { "query": user_input, "fields": ["name", "description"], } }, "sort": [ {"price": {"order": "asc"}} ], "filter": { "term": {"category": "smartphones"} } } response = es.search(index="products", body=query) # Print search results for hit in response["hits"]["hits"]: print(hit["_source"]) If you would like to try it on your local computer, install elasticsearch on your local system and the python package using pip install elasticsearch. If you would like to see a more complete demo, check out ElasticSearch’s official movie demo. Use Cases Any search box likely uses full-text search. Several common patterns appear across industries. E-commerce platforms like Amazon and eBay rely on full-text search. Users search for products using keywords. The search engine ranks results by relevance, price, and reviews. Typo tolerance helps users find "wireles headphones" even with misspellings. Social media platforms including Twitter and Facebook enable content discovery. Users search for posts, profiles, and hashtags. Results rank by recency, relevance, and engagement. Real-time indexing shows new content immediately. 4/5

Document management systems like Dropbox and Google Drive search file names and contents. Users find documents by searching text within PDFs and documents. Metadata filters narrow results by file type and modification date. Forums and Q&A platforms such as Stack Overflow and Reddit help users find discussions. Search keywords, phrases, or tags. Results rank by recency, relevance, and community votes. Answers to common questions surface first. For deeper coverage of search engine internals, see the Search Engine Mechanics section. It covers inverted index fundamentals, TF-IDF scoring, BM25 ranking, and Elasticsearch architecture. 5/5
