Tiny blocks would mean constant task scheduling overhead without enough work per task. So far we've covered the traditional Hadoop stack: MapReduce for computation, HDFS for storage. But MapReduce has limitations—particularly for iterative algorithms and multi-stage pipelines that repeatedly read and write intermediate results. That leads into Spark, which addresses these limitations by caching data in memory across stages. 4/4

Big Data › Batch Processing

Modern Batch Processing Beyond Map Reduce

Open PDF

Modern Batch Processing Beyond MapReduce MapReduce Input Map Disk Reduce Disk Map Output Spark DAG Input Filter Map Join Output Disk I/O between stages In-memory, flexible graph Modern Batch: Spark MapReduce established distributed batch processing, but working with it felt like writing assembly code. Every computation must fit the rigid map-shuffle-reduce pattern. Intermediate results write to disk between jobs. Iterative algorithms crawl. Apache Spark addressed these limitations while preserving MapReduce's core insights about distributed data and fault tolerance. Today, Spark handles most batch workloads that once ran on MapReduce. Why MapReduce Became Limiting Consider what MapReduce requires for a 10-stage data pipeline. Each stage is a separate job. Between jobs, you write intermediate results to HDFS and read them back. That's 10 writes and 10 reads to distributed storage, even if the entire dataset fits in the cluster's aggregate memory. Rigid structure. Every MapReduce job follows the same pattern: map phase, shuffle, reduce phase. Some algorithms need different access patterns. A graph algorithm that iteratively updates node values requires a full MapReduce job per iteration—the framework can't express "iterate until converged" as a single job. Disk overhead between stages. MapReduce writes map outputs to local disk, shuffles them, then writes reduce outputs to HDFS. A multi-stage pipeline pays this disk I/O tax repeatedly. For small intermediate datasets that fit in memory, this overhead dominates runtime. 1/4

Poor iteration support. Machine learning algorithms iterate until convergence. Gradient descent might need hundreds of passes over the training data. Each MapReduce iteration reads from HDFS, computes gradients, writes updated parameters to HDFS. The disk overhead per iteration makes iterative ML impractical. How Spark Changed the Model Spark introduced three key improvements that address MapReduce's limitations. Flexible DAG execution. Instead of forcing everything into map-shuffle-reduce, Spark represents computations as a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of operations. You compose transformations (filter, map, join, aggregate) and Spark's optimizer arranges them efficiently— combining stages where possible, pushing filters early, choosing join strategies. MapReduce Input Map Disk Reduce Disk Map Output Spark DAG Input Filter Map Join Output Disk I/O between stages In-memory, flexible graph In-memory caching. Spark can keep intermediate data in memory across stages. A 10-stage pipeline reads input once, processes through all stages in memory, and writes output once. When data doesn't fit in memory, Spark spills to disk—graceful degradation rather than failure. Richer operation vocabulary. Beyond map and reduce, Spark provides joins, aggregations, window functions, and SQL queries out of the box. The same engine handles batch ETL, interactive SQL queries, machine learning, and graph processing. Concrete Comparison: Log Analysis Let's trace the same log analysis job through both frameworks. MapReduce approach: 1. Job 1: Read logs from HDFS → Map (extract fields) → Shuffle → Reduce (count by URL) → Write to HDFS 2. Job 2: Read counts from HDFS → Map (identity) → Shuffle (sort by count) → Reduce (top-N) → Write to HDFS 2/4

Each job involves disk writes for intermediate data. Two jobs means four HDFS operations plus local disk for shuffle. Spark approach: 1. Read logs from HDFS 2. Transform in memory: extract fields → group by URL → count → sort → take top N 3. Write results to HDFS Spark reads once, transforms through a single DAG (potentially with one shuffle for the groupBy), and writes once. For datasets that fit in memory, the difference is substantial. When the Difference Is Dramatic: Iteration The gap widens for iterative algorithms. Consider PageRank, which iteratively updates page scores based on link structure. MapReduce: Each iteration is a separate job. Read graph + current scores from HDFS → compute new scores → write to HDFS. Repeat until convergence. If PageRank needs 20 iterations, that's 40 HDFS read/write operations. Spark: Load graph into memory once, cache it. Each iteration updates scores in memory. Write final results to storage. Total: 1 read + 1 write, regardless of iteration count. This is why Spark's original paper emphasized iterative machine learning—the performance difference can be 10-100x for algorithms that iterate over cached data. When to Use Spark Spark handles most workloads that historically ran on MapReduce: ETL pipelines with multiple transformation stages benefit from DAG optimization and reduced disk I/O Machine learning on historical data leverages in-memory iteration Interactive queries get faster turnaround than batch-scheduled MapReduce jobs SQL analytics through Spark SQL provides a familiar interface with distributed execution Spark still reads from and writes to distributed storage (HDFS, S3, cloud object storage). The improvement is in how computation executes between storage operations—not eliminating storage, but reducing redundant trips. Limitations and Trade-offs Spark isn't free of trade-offs. Memory caching requires sufficient cluster memory; datasets larger than aggregate memory spill to disk, reducing the advantage. Spark's query optimizer occasionally makes poor choices that require manual tuning. And for simple one-pass transformations, the difference versus MapReduce may be negligible. 3/4

What's Next So far we've covered batch processing: scheduled jobs that process bounded datasets. But some applications can't wait hours for results. Fraud detection needs to flag suspicious transactions in seconds. Dashboards need live updates. Sensors generate continuous data streams. The next section introduces stream processing—handling unbounded data continuously as it arrives rather than in scheduled batches. 4/4

Big Data › Stream Processing

Unbounded Data and Message Brokers

Open PDF

Unbounded Data and Message Brokers What is Stream Processing? We've covered batch processing—scheduled jobs that process bounded datasets. But some applications can't wait. A fraud detection system that runs nightly analyzes yesterday's transactions and flags suspicious accounts by morning. But a fraudster who steals credit card data at 9 AM has all day to drain accounts. By the time the batch job runs, the money is gone. Stream processing takes a different approach: handle data continuously as it arrives, rather than waiting for it to accumulate. Bounded vs Unbounded Data The fundamental distinction between batch and stream processing is the nature of the input. Batch processing works with bounded data—a finite dataset with a defined beginning and end. You might process "yesterday's log files" or "all transactions from Q3." The batch job reads all input, processes it, and terminates. Stream processing works with unbounded data—a continuous flow of events with no defined end. Events keep arriving: user clicks, sensor readings, financial transactions. The processing system runs indefinitely, handling each event as it arrives. 1/4

Bounded (Batch) Finite dataset start end Job reads all, processes, terminates Unbounded (Stream) Continuous events start no end Process each event as it arrives Think of it like the difference between counting fish in a bucket versus counting fish swimming past a point in a river. The bucket has a fixed number; you count them all and you're done. The river never stops—you count continuously and report rolling totals. Message Brokers: The Input Layer Where does stream data come from? The standard architecture uses a message broker (sometimes called an event bus or streaming platform). Apache Kafka is the most widely used, though alternatives include AWS Kinesis, Apache Pulsar, and others. Producers (your application services) write events to topics—named categories like "user- signups," "order-placed," or "sensor-readings." Consumers (stream processing applications) subscribe to topics and receive events in order. Producer Producer Message Broker Topic: orders Topic: payments Consumer Consumer write read Events retained for replay (hours to weeks) The broker provides key capabilities: Decoupling. Producers don't need to know which consumers exist. They write events; consumers independently subscribe to the topics they care about. 2/4

Durability. Events persist in the broker for a configurable retention period—hours, days, or indefinitely. If a consumer crashes, it can resume from where it left off. If you deploy a new consumer, it can replay historical events. Ordering. Within a partition (a subset of a topic), events maintain their order. A consumer sees events in the sequence they were produced. Scalability. Topics partition across multiple brokers. Consumers parallelize by assigning different partitions to different instances. The Stream Processing Pattern A stream processing application follows a simple loop: 1. Read events from input topics 2. Process each event—filter, transform, enrich, aggregate 3. Produce output—write to output topics, update databases, trigger alerts The application runs continuously. Unlike batch jobs that start, process, and terminate, a stream processor is always running, waiting for the next event. State is the tricky part. Many stream operations need to remember information across events. Counting page views requires remembering the running count. Detecting "three failed logins in 5 minutes" requires remembering recent login attempts. Stream processors maintain this state, often backed by embedded databases or external stores for durability. Key Differences from Batch Aspect Batch Stream Input Finite dataset Continuous events Execution Start, process, terminateRuns indefinitely Result latencyMinutes to hours Seconds to sub-second (typically) State Often rebuilt each runMaintained continuously Debugging Examine frozen inputData keeps flowing When Stream Processing Fits Real-time decisions. Fraud scoring must happen before a transaction completes. Recommendation engines should respond while the user is browsing. Waiting for a batch job means missing the window. Live dashboards and monitoring. Operations teams need current metrics: requests per second, error rates, active users. Hourly batch updates aren't actionable for incident response. 3/4

Event-driven architectures. An order placement triggers inventory reservation, triggers payment processing, triggers shipping notification. Each step produces events that the next step consumes. The system reacts immediately rather than waiting for scheduled jobs. Alerting on thresholds. When error rates exceed 5%, alert the on-call engineer now—not when the nightly batch job discovers it. What's Ahead Stream processing introduces challenges that batch processing doesn't face. When data arrives continuously, how do you aggregate "per hour" when the stream never ends? That leads into windowing patterns—tumbling, sliding, and session windows that let you compute bounded aggregates over unbounded data. After that, we'll tackle event time versus processing time (what happens when events arrive late?), and then delivery guarantees (how do you ensure every event is processed exactly once?). 4/4

Big Data › Stream Processing

Windowing Patterns

Open PDF

Windowing Patterns 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 Window 1 Window 2 Window 3 Window 4 Tumbling Windows (1 minute, non-overlapping) Each event belongs to exactly one window The previous article introduced stream processing as handling unbounded data. But many useful computations need aggregation: count page views per minute, sum sales per hour, detect three failed logins within 5 minutes. You can't aggregate an infinite stream—you need boundaries. Windows solve this by dividing the continuous stream into finite chunks. Each window collects events over some interval, aggregates them, and emits a result. There are three main patterns. Tumbling Windows: Fixed, Non-Overlapping Tumbling windows divide time into fixed intervals that don't overlap. Each event belongs to exactly one window. 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 Window 1 Window 2 Window 3 Window 4 Tumbling Windows (1 minute, non-overlapping) Each event belongs to exactly one window Consider counting page views per minute. A 1-minute tumbling window groups all events from 10:00:00–10:00:59, then 10:01:00–10:01:59, and so on. When the clock crosses a minute boundary, you emit the count for the completed window and start a new one. Concrete example: An analytics dashboard shows "requests per minute" as a time-series chart. Each data point is the count from one tumbling window. 1/4

Strengths: Simple to implement and reason about Memory-efficient—you only store one window's state at a time Each event is counted exactly once Limitation: Events near boundaries may split unnaturally. A user session spanning 10:59 to 11:01 contributes to two separate windows. Sliding Windows: Overlapping for Smoothness Sliding windows (also called hopping windows) overlap. You specify both a window size and a slide interval. 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 10:00 - 10:01:30 10:00:30 - 10:02 10:01 - 10:02:30 Sliding Windows (1.5 min size, 30 sec slide) Events near boundaries appear in multiple windows Consider a 1-minute window that slides every 30 seconds: Window 1: 10:00:00 – 10:00:59 Window 2: 10:00:30 – 10:01:29 Window 3: 10:01:00 – 10:01:59 Events between 10:00:30 and 10:00:59 appear in both Window 1 and Window 2. Concrete example: A rate limiter checks "requests in the last 60 seconds." Using a tumbling window, a burst at 10:00:59 followed by another burst at 10:01:01 might slip through (different windows). A sliding window catches both bursts because they appear in the same overlapping window. Strengths: Smoother metrics without boundary artifacts Better for detecting patterns that might straddle tumbling boundaries Good for moving averages 2/4

Limitation: Higher memory usage because you maintain multiple overlapping windows simultaneously. With a 1-minute window sliding every 10 seconds, you maintain 6 active windows. Session Windows: Activity-Based Session windows don't use fixed time intervals. They group events based on activity gaps. Session 1 Session 2 Session 3 gap > timeout gap > timeout Session Windows (30 min gap timeout) Windows sized by activity, not fixed time Define a gap timeout (say, 30 minutes of inactivity). Events arriving within the timeout extend the current session. When no events arrive for 30 minutes, the session closes and emits. Concrete example: Analyzing user browsing sessions on an e-commerce site. User A browses for 5 minutes, then leaves. User B browses for 2 hours. Fixed windows would chop these sessions arbitrarily. Session windows capture each user's natural activity pattern. Strengths: Captures natural activity patterns Window size adapts to actual user behavior Ideal for per-user or per-entity analysis Limitations: Variable output latency—you wait for the gap before emitting More complex state management (per-key sessions) Must handle very long sessions that accumulate unbounded state Choosing a Window Type Pattern Use When Trade-off TumblingPeriodic reports, dashboards, simple counts Events at boundaries may split Sliding Rate limiting, anomaly detection, moving averages Higher memory for overlapping windows 3/4

Pattern Use When Trade-off Session User journeys, activity analysis, per-entity patterns Variable latency, must wait for gap In practice, the choice often follows from the question you're answering: "How many requests per minute?" → Tumbling "Did we exceed 100 requests in any 60-second period?" → Sliding "How long was this user's session?" → Session The Incomplete Window Problem There's a catch. When should a window close and emit its result? For tumbling windows, you might think: "when the clock shows 10:01:00, emit the 10:00 window." But what if an event from 10:00:45 arrives at 10:01:02 due to network delay? The window already closed. This event is late. Do you: Drop it? (lose accuracy) Reopen the window and re-emit? (update previous results) Include it in the current window? (wrong time attribution) That leads into event time (when the event actually happened) versus processing time (when your system received it), and watermarks (signals that tell the system when it's safe to close a window). 4/4

Big Data › Stream Processing

Event Time Processing Time and Watermarks in Stream Processing

Open PDF

Event Time, Processing Time, and Watermarks in Stream Processing Event Time (when it happened) 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 Processing Time (when processed) 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 A B A B 30s delay 60s delay Event B happened before A, but processed after. Event-time windows preserve correct ordering. Event Time & Watermarks The previous article showed how windows aggregate unbounded streams. But windows raise a question: when should they close? That depends on understanding two different notions of "when" an event happens. Event Time vs Processing Time Event time is when the event actually occurred—the timestamp recorded at the source. A user clicked a button at 10:00:00 according to their device. That timestamp is embedded in the event. Processing time is when your system processes the event. Network delays, queue backlogs, or consumer restarts mean processing might happen at 10:00:05—or 10:05:00 if the system fell behind during an outage. 1/4

Event Time (when it happened) 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 Processing Time (when processed) 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 A B A B 30s delay 60s delay Event B happened before A, but processed after. Event-time windows preserve correct ordering. These two times can differ significantly. A mobile app might buffer events during spotty connectivity and send them in a batch when the connection recovers. Events from IoT devices in remote locations might take minutes to reach your datacenter. Why the Difference Matters Consider counting clicks per minute for a real-time dashboard. With processing-time windows: You group clicks by when your system processes them. If a traffic spike creates a 5-minute backlog, clicks that happened at 10:00 get processed at 10:05 and count toward the 10:05 window. If a consumer crashes and restarts, replayed events might get double-counted (depending on your checkpointing). The same underlying data produces different results depending on system timing. With event-time windows: Clicks count based on when users clicked—the timestamp in the event—regardless of processing delays. The same data always produces the same counts. This is called deterministic reprocessing: you can replay the input and get identical output. For analytics that need to be reproducible, event-time windowing is essential. The Out-of-Order Problem Event-time processing introduces a challenge: events don't arrive in timestamp order. Suppose events from two different mobile users arrive at your system. User A's event from 10:00:55 travels through a slow cell tower. User B's event from 10:01:02 travels through fast wifi. You see 10:01:02 first, then 10:00:55. If you close the 10:00–10:01 window as soon as you see a 10:01 event, you'll miss the 10:00:55 event. But you can't wait forever either—you need to emit results eventually. 2/4

Watermarks: Tracking Progress Watermarks solve this by tracking the system's estimate of event-time progress. A watermark at time T means: "We believe all events with timestamps before T have arrived." When the watermark advances past a window's end time, the system can close that window and emit results. 10:00 10:01 10:02 10:03 Window 10:00-10:01 Watermark at 10:01:20 late! event time: 10:00:55 On-time events Late event Watermark passed 10:01, window closed. Event at 10:00:55 is late. Watermarks are estimates, not guarantees. Late-arriving events can still appear after the watermark has passed their timestamp. The system must have a policy for handling them. How Watermarks Are Generated Different approaches suit different scenarios: Perfect watermarks are possible when you control the source and know all events have been seen. For example, if you're reading from a bounded log file in order, the watermark can track exactly which timestamps have been fully consumed. Heuristic watermarks estimate based on observed patterns. If you observe that 99% of events arrive within 30 seconds of their event time, you might set the watermark to lag 30 seconds behind the most recent event timestamp. This balances completeness against latency. Source-based watermarks track progress from upstream systems. If the input is partitioned (like Kafka partitions), you track the minimum event-time progress across all partitions—the slowest partition bounds the watermark. Handling Late Events Events arriving after their window's watermark has passed are called late events. Common strategies: Drop late events. Simplest implementation. Accept some data loss in exchange for finalized windows that never change. Works when approximate counts are acceptable. 3/4

Allowed lateness. Keep windows open for a grace period after the watermark passes. Events within the grace period update the window; events beyond it get dropped. Balances correctness against state retention. Emit updates. When late events arrive, recompute and emit an updated result for the affected window. Downstream consumers must handle updates (retractions and new values). This is most correct but adds complexity. Side output. Route late events to a separate stream for special handling—perhaps manual investigation or delayed reconciliation. The Latency vs Completeness Trade-off This is a fundamental tension in stream processing: Aggressive watermarks (advancing quickly, short allowed lateness) emit results sooner but may miss late events. Conservative watermarks (lagging further behind, longer allowed lateness) produce more complete results but add latency and increase state. The right choice depends on your use case: A real-time dashboard showing active users can tolerate missing a few late events. Fast watermarks keep the display current. A billing system calculating charges needs completeness—it's better to wait and get the right answer than to undercharge and reconcile later. A fraud detection system needs speed for blocking transactions, so it might use aggressive watermarks for real-time scoring while running a separate batch reconciliation for accuracy. Summary Event time gives you deterministic, reproducible results regardless of processing delays. Watermarks let you make progress on event time even when events arrive out of order. The trade-off between latency and completeness is unavoidable—but at least you can make an explicit choice rather than having processing delays randomly distort your results. That connects to delivery guarantees—what happens to events when processing nodes fail, and how to ensure events are processed exactly once (or at least understand when that's not achievable). 4/4

Big Data › Stream Processing

Delivery Guarantees

Open PDF

Delivery Guarantees The previous articles covered how stream processing handles unbounded data, windows, and event time. But there's a critical question we haven't addressed: when processing fails partway through, what happens to in-flight events? A consumer reads an event, starts processing, then crashes. Is the event lost? Will it be reprocessed? Stream systems offer different guarantees about loss and duplication. At-Most-Once: Fire and Forget Guarantee: Each event is processed zero or one time. Events may be lost on failure. At-Most-Once Broker Consumer Output 1. get 2. ack immediately 3. process If crash here → event lost Simplest. No retries. Possible data loss. How it works: The consumer acknowledges receipt (commits its offset) immediately, before processing completes. If processing then crashes, the event is gone—the broker already marked it consumed and won't redeliver. 1/4

Example scenario: A logging pipeline processes events to count errors. The consumer reads event X, commits its offset, then crashes while writing to the database. When it restarts, it resumes from after event X. Event X is lost. Use when: Occasional data loss is acceptable and speed matters most. Metrics pipelines where a few missing data points don't affect overall trends. Log aggregation where some gaps are tolerable. Trade-offs: Simplest implementation Lowest latency (no retry overhead) Data loss on failures At-Least-Once: Never Lose, May Duplicate Guarantee: Each event is processed one or more times. No events are lost, but duplicates are possible. At-Least-Once Broker Consumer Output 1. get 2. process 3. ack after process If crash before ack → redelivery → duplicate No data loss. Duplicates possible. Use idempotent writes. How it works: The consumer processes the event fully, then acknowledges. If it crashes before acknowledging, the broker redelivers the event when the consumer restarts. The consumer processes it again—a duplicate. Example scenario: Same logging pipeline. The consumer reads event X, writes to the database, then crashes before committing its offset. When it restarts, the broker redelivers event X. The database now has two copies of the same error count. Making it work: Downstream systems must handle duplicates. Strategies include: Idempotent operations: An upsert (INSERT or UPDATE by key) produces the same result whether executed once or twice. A plain INSERT creates duplicates. Deduplication by event ID: Store a unique event ID with each record. Before processing, check if you've seen this ID before. Natural idempotency: "Set balance to $100" is idempotent. "Add $10 to balance" is not. 2/4

Use when: Data loss is unacceptable and you can design downstream operations to be idempotent. Most production stream processing uses this approach. Trade-offs: No data loss Must handle duplicates in downstream logic Slightly higher latency than at-most-once Exactly-Once: Neither Loss Nor Duplication Guarantee: Each event is processed exactly once, even across failures. No loss, no duplicates. Exactly-Once Broker Consumer Output Atomic Transaction get process + commit Checkpoint (offset + state) Output + offset commit atomically. No loss, no duplicates. How it works: The system bundles the processing result and the input acknowledgment into a single atomic transaction. Either both commit (result is written AND event is marked consumed) or neither does. On failure, the system rolls back to a consistent state. Modern stream processors like Flink and Kafka Streams achieve this through distributed snapshots (checkpoints). The framework periodically captures the entire pipeline state: what's been consumed, what's in-flight, what's been output. On failure, restore to the last checkpoint and resume. Events between the checkpoint and the failure get reprocessed, but the outputs are either suppressed (if already committed) or written exactly once. The catch: "Exactly-once" typically applies within the stream processing system. If your pipeline writes to external systems (a database, an API), achieving exactly-once end-to-end requires either: The sink supporting transactions that coordinate with the checkpoint, or Idempotent writes to the sink, or The ability to roll back external writes on failure Without this, you might have exactly-once processing but at-least-once delivery to the sink. 3/4

Use when: Correctness is critical—financial transactions, billing calculations, inventory updates where duplicates or gaps cause real problems. Trade-offs: Strongest guarantee Higher latency (checkpointing overhead) More complex implementation Requires coordination with external systems for true end-to-end guarantees Practical Guidance Guarantee Data LossDuplicatesComplexityLatency At-most-oncePossible No Low Lowest At-least-onceNo Possible Medium Medium Exactly-onceNo No High Highest Many production systems use at-least-once with idempotent operations. This achieves "effectively-once" semantics with lower complexity than true exactly-once checkpointing: Use upserts instead of inserts Include unique event IDs and deduplicate at the sink Design operations to be naturally idempotent where possible This approach works well because idempotency pushes the complexity to application logic (which you control) rather than distributed transaction protocols (which are hard to get right). What's Next We've covered the fundamentals of batch and stream processing: the models, windowing, time semantics, and delivery guarantees. The next section explores hybrid architectures— Lambda and Kappa patterns that combine batch and stream processing for systems that need both historical completeness and real-time updates. 4/4

Big Data › Stream Processing

Modern Stream Processing Flink and Kafka Streams

Open PDF

Modern Stream Processing: Flink and Kafka Streams Flink: Distributed Stream Processing Kafka TaskManager 1 State Operator TaskManager 2 State Operator JobManager Checkpoint Storage Sink Sink Processing nodes State snapshots Coordination Modern Stream: Flink & Kafka Streams The previous articles covered stream processing concepts: windowing, event time, and delivery guarantees. Implementing these patterns with raw Kafka consumers is possible but requires substantial work. You'd build your own state management, handle consumer rebalancing, and coordinate exactly-once semantics manually. Modern stream processing frameworks handle this complexity for you. Apache Flink and Kafka Streams are two widely adopted options, each with a different deployment model. What These Frameworks Provide Both Flink and Kafka Streams give you: Managed state. The framework tracks per-key state (running counts, window contents) and handles redistribution when workers scale up or down. Fault tolerance. Automatic checkpointing and recovery. When a worker fails, processing resumes from the last checkpoint without data loss. Windowing and event time. Built-in support for tumbling, sliding, and session windows with watermarks for handling late-arriving events. Exactly-once semantics. Coordinated commits ensure events aren't lost or double- counted, without you building that coordination yourself. 1/3

Flink vs Kafka Streams: Deployment Model The key difference is how you run them. Flink: Distributed Stream Processing Kafka TaskManager 1 State Operator TaskManager 2 State Operator JobManager Checkpoint Storage Sink Sink Processing nodes State snapshots Coordination Apache Flink is a distributed cluster. You submit jobs to a JobManager, which schedules work across TaskManager workers. Flink handles diverse sources—Kafka, databases via CDC, files, and more. The trade-off is operational overhead: you run and monitor a separate cluster. Kafka Streams: Embedded Library Kafka Cluster Input Topics Changelogs Output Topics App Instance 1 Kafka Streams Local State App Instance 2 Kafka Streams Local State App Instance 3 Kafka Streams Local State Your application instances State backed by changelogs No separate cluster needed Kafka Streams is a library embedded in your application. Your application instances are the processing cluster. Scale by running more instances. Kafka handles partition assignment automatically. The trade-off is that it's Kafka-centric—your inputs and outputs should be Kafka topics. 2/3

When to Use Which Choose Flink when: Sources include databases, files, or non-Kafka systems You need very low latency (sub-second) You're already operating distributed infrastructure Choose Kafka Streams when: Kafka is already your event backbone You want to avoid managing a separate cluster Simpler operational model matters more than connector flexibility For system design interviews, what matters is understanding why these frameworks exist: they abstract away the hard parts of stateful stream processing (state management, fault tolerance, exactly-once) so you can focus on business logic. What's Next Modern stream processors handle stateful, fault-tolerant processing. But many systems need both real-time updates and batch processing for historical completeness. The next section explores hybrid architectures—Lambda and Kappa patterns that combine both approaches. 3/3

Big Data › Hybrid Architectures

Lambda Architecture

Open PDF

Lambda Architecture We've covered batch processing (complete but slow) and stream processing (fast but handling late data is complex). Some systems need both properties simultaneously. Consider an e-commerce company. The finance team needs daily sales reports accurate to the penny—they reconcile against payment processors, and discrepancies create accounting headaches. The operations team needs a live dashboard showing orders per minute—they need to spot outages immediately, not hours later. Running everything through batch means the dashboard updates once per day—useless for incident response. Running everything through streaming risks missing late events or handling corrections incorrectly—problematic for financial reports. Lambda architecture addresses this by running both approaches in parallel. The Three Layers Lambda architecture, popularized by Nathan Marz, organizes computation into three layers with distinct responsibilities. 1/4

Data Source Batch Layer Complete, accurate (hours) S p a r k / H a d o o p Speed Layer Fast, approximate (seconds) F l i n k / S t o r m Serving Layer Merge results b a t c h + r e a l t i m e QueryTwo processing paths: same logic, different implementations Batch layer: Stores the complete, immutable master dataset (often in distributed storage like HDFS or S3) and periodically recomputes views from scratch. A nightly job reads all historical data, applies business logic, and writes results to batch views. This is slow—potentially hours— but produces complete, accurate results. Late-arriving events and corrections are naturally incorporated the next time the batch runs. Speed layer (real-time layer): Processes events as they arrive via stream processing. It computes incremental updates covering only the time since the last batch completed. This is fast—seconds to minutes—but only captures events that have arrived so far. Events still in flight or delayed will be missing. Serving layer: Merges results from both layers to answer queries. When a user requests "total sales today," the serving layer fetches the batch-computed result (sales through 2 AM when batch last ran) and the speed layer's delta (sales from 2 AM to now). The sum provides an up- to-date answer. When the next batch completes, its results replace the speed layer's estimates, and the speed layer resets. Walking Through an Example Let's trace how Lambda architecture computes total sales by product category. Batch processing (2 AM daily): 1. Spark job reads all order events from the past year in S3 2. Aggregates sales by category: Electronics = $45.2M, Clothing = $28.1M, etc. 3. Writes results to a batch view table with timestamp "2024-01-15 02:00:00" Stream processing (continuous): 2/4

1. Flink job consumes from Kafka order events topic 2. Maintains running totals since 2 AM in Redis: Electronics = $180K, Clothing = $95K 3. These totals only include events that arrived since 2 AM Query handling (anytime): 1. User asks: "What are Electronics sales today?" 2. Serving layer fetches batch result: $45.2M (as of 2 AM) 3. Serving layer fetches speed layer delta: $180K (since 2 AM) 4. Returns: $45.38M Next morning at 2 AM: 1. New batch job runs, computes Electronics = $45.4M including yesterday's full activity 2. Batch view updates with new timestamp 3. Speed layer resets counters to zero 4. Now queries combine $45.4M (batch) + new deltas (speed) The Maintenance Problem Lambda architecture achieves both speed and completeness, but at a cost. Duplicate logic. The same business rules must be implemented twice—once in the batch system (perhaps Spark/SQL) and once in the stream system (perhaps Flink/Java). Subtle differences in how the two implementations handle edge cases lead to discrepancies. Users see different numbers depending on when they query relative to the batch run. Dual infrastructure. You operate two separate data processing clusters with different technologies, deployment patterns, monitoring, and failure modes. When the batch layer has a bug, you fix it and rerun. When the speed layer has a bug, you fix it, redeploy, and may need to reconcile historical data. Synchronization complexity. The serving layer must track which batch run is current, which speed layer data corresponds to that batch, and handle the handoff when a new batch completes. This coordination logic is error-prone. When Lambda Makes Sense Despite the overhead, Lambda architecture persists in scenarios where: Compliance requires exact match with batch systems. If audit systems or payment reconciliation use batch processing, the real-time layer must be seen as an approximation that the batch layer periodically "corrects." Existing batch infrastructure is mature. Organizations with significant investment in batch pipelines (Hadoop, data warehouses) can add a streaming layer incrementally rather than replacing everything. 3/4

Batch and stream genuinely have different semantics. Some computations (like ML model training) inherently require full historical data and can't be incrementalized. Lambda lets you run batch-only logic alongside streaming. What's Next Lambda architecture's maintenance burden motivated the search for simpler alternatives. That leads into Kappa architecture—Jay Kreps' proposal to run everything through stream processing, using log replay to achieve batch-like capabilities without a separate batch layer. 4/4

Big Data › Hybrid Architectures

Kappa Architecture

Open PDF

Kappa Architecture Data Source Event Log (Kafka) R e t a i n w e e k s / m o n t h s R e p l a y f o r r e p r o c e s s Stream Processor Serving Store Replay for reprocessing Single path: stream processing handles both real-time and historical The previous article showed Lambda architecture's approach: run batch and stream in parallel, merge results. It works, but you maintain two implementations of the same logic. Jay Kreps, co- creator of Apache Kafka, proposed a simplification: if you have a replayable event log, batch processing is just stream processing replayed from the beginning. Instead of separate batch and stream pipelines, run everything through streaming. When you need to reprocess—for bug fixes, schema changes, or new features—replay the event log through the same streaming code. The Core Idea The event log (typically Kafka or a similar system) serves as the system of record. It retains all events for a configurable period—days, weeks, or longer depending on storage and retention policy. Stream processing applications consume from the log, apply transformations, and write results to serving stores. There's no separate batch layer. The "batch" capability comes from replaying the log. 1/4

Data Source Event Log (Kafka) R e t a i n w e e k s / m o n t h s R e p l a y f o r r e p r o c e s s Stream Processor Serving Store Replay for reprocessing Single path: stream processing handles both real-time and historical Reprocessing Through Replay Suppose you discover a bug in your revenue calculation logic. In Lambda architecture, you'd fix the bug in both the batch codebase and the stream codebase, then rerun the batch job. In Kappa architecture: 1. Fix the bug in your single stream processing application 2. Deploy a new instance that starts reading from the beginning of the log 3. The new instance replays all historical events, applying the corrected logic 4. Once it catches up to the present, swap it in as the active processor 5. Shut down the old instance This matters because reprocessing follows the exact same code path as live processing. One implementation, one test suite, one deployment pipeline. You don't have subtle differences between batch and stream versions causing result discrepancies. Why Modern Infrastructure Makes This Feasible Kappa architecture became practical when message brokers evolved beyond traditional queue semantics. Durable, long-term storage. Kafka retains events on disk with configurable retention. A log with weeks or months of retention is effectively an append-only database. You don't need to extract data to a separate batch store. Independent consumption. Multiple consumer groups read the same log independently. A reprocessing job can replay from offset 0 while the live job continues processing at the current offset. They don't interfere with each other. Scalable replay. Parallel consumers across partitions mean replay can run as fast as your cluster allows—limited by disk I/O and compute, not by the broker's design. 2/4

A Concrete Example Consider a real-time dashboard showing "orders per hour" by region. Initial deployment: Stream processor reads from Kafka, computes hourly aggregates, writes to a serving database. Dashboard queries the database. Bug discovered: Region codes were incorrectly mapped for Canadian orders. All "CA" orders were counted as California instead of Canada. Kappa reprocessing: 1. Fix the region mapping in the stream processor code 2. Deploy new instance: stream-processor-v2, configured to start from offset 0 3. v2 replays the entire log with correct logic, writing to a new table orders_by_region_v2 4. Once v2 catches up (lag near zero), update dashboard to query orders_by_region_v2 5. Decommission v1 and drop the old table No separate batch codebase. No batch cluster. The fix applies identically to historical and future data. Trade-offs and Limitations When Kappa works well: Events are the primary source of truth Reprocessing time is acceptable for your use case Business logic is identical for real-time and historical processing Your event log retention covers the history you need When Kappa struggles: Historical data predates the event log (you can't replay what was never captured) Reprocessing takes too long (petabytes of events might take days to replay) Some computations genuinely require batch semantics (certain ML training workflows, complex joins across large historical datasets) External systems aren't designed for reprocessing (sinks that can't handle replayed data cleanly) Lambda vs Kappa: Choosing Factor Lambda Kappa Codebases Two (batch + stream) One Infrastructure Two clusters One 3/4

Factor Lambda Kappa Reprocessing mechanism Run batch job Replay event log Operational complexity Higher Lower Historical data Can process data outside the logLimited to log retention Best for Compliance-critical systems, existing batch investments Greenfield projects, event- sourced architectures For new projects with modern infrastructure and event-sourcing principles, Kappa often wins on simplicity. For enterprises with significant batch investments, strict compliance requirements tied to existing batch systems, or historical data that was never event-captured, Lambda provides a path to add real-time without disrupting proven systems. What's Next Both Lambda and Kappa treat batch and stream as somewhat separate concerns. Unified batch-stream frameworks—modern systems like Apache Flink and Spark Structured Streaming—offer a single API for both bounded and unbounded data, blurring the distinction further. 4/4

Big Data › Hybrid Architectures

Unified Batch and Stream Processing Flink Spark Beam

Open PDF

Unified Batch and Stream Processing: Flink, Spark, Beam Unified API Same code for batch & stream Bounded Historical files Unbounded (Kafka) Flink Engine Optimizes for each input type Backfill results Real-time results Unified Processing Lambda and Kappa architectures treat batch and stream as separate concerns—run them separately (Lambda) or use stream processing for everything (Kappa). Modern frameworks take a different approach: treat batch and stream as the same computation over different data shapes. Write your pipeline once. Point it at historical files for a backfill. Point it at a live event stream for real-time results. Same code, same semantics, different input sources. The Unified Model The distinction: batch processing operates on bounded data (finite, with a defined end), while stream processing operates on unbounded data (continuous, with no end). But the transformations—filter, map, aggregate, join—are conceptually identical. A unified framework provides a single API for both. The execution engine handles the differences: For bounded data: optimize for throughput, read all data, produce complete results For unbounded data: optimize for latency, process continuously, handle late data Apache Flink: Stream-First Unification Flink treats batch as a special case of streaming—a bounded stream. The same DataStream or Table API works for both. 1/4

Unified API Same code for batch & stream Bounded Historical files Unbounded (Kafka) Flink Engine Optimizes for each input type Backfill results Real-time results Flink's execution engine adapts based on input: Streaming mode: Processes events as they arrive, maintains state continuously, handles watermarks and late data Batch mode (BATCH execution): Reads complete input, enables additional optimizations like sort-based joins, produces final results Strengths: Native event-time processing with watermarks built in Exactly-once processing via distributed snapshots (checkpoints) Sophisticated state management that survives failures True low-latency streaming (sub-second), not micro-batching Flink excels at complex stateful applications where event-time correctness matters—session analysis, complex event processing, real-time machine learning features. Apache Spark: Batch-First Unification Spark started as a batch engine and added streaming capabilities through Structured Streaming. The same DataFrame/Dataset API works for both modes. # Same transformation code for batch or stream df.groupBy("category").agg(sum("amount")) The difference is in how you create the DataFrame: Batch: spark.read.parquet("s3://data/orders/") Stream: spark.readStream.format("kafka").load() How Structured Streaming works: Rather than processing event-by-event, Spark collects events into micro-batches (typically every few hundred milliseconds to seconds) and processes each batch using the same engine as batch jobs. This provides streaming semantics while leveraging Spark's mature batch execution. 2/4

Trade-offs: Micro-batching adds latency—typically seconds, not milliseconds True event-time semantics require explicit watermark configuration But teams already using Spark for batch can add streaming with minimal new learning Spark is a good choice when you have significant Spark batch investments, need ML integration (MLlib), or when second-level latency is acceptable. Apache Beam: Portability Layer Beam provides a unified programming model that runs on multiple execution engines: Flink, Spark, Google Cloud Dataflow, and others. Write your pipeline in the Beam SDK. Deploy to whichever runner makes sense for your environment. The same pipeline definition can run on Dataflow in GCP, Flink on-premise, or Spark on EMR. The appeal: Avoid vendor lock-in. Change execution engines without rewriting pipelines. Teams can standardize on Beam while using different infrastructure across environments. The trade-off: Abstraction costs. Advanced features of specific engines (Flink's sophisticated state backends, Spark's ML integration) may not be fully exposed through Beam's unified API. You optimize for portability rather than maximizing any single engine's capabilities. Choosing a Framework FrameworkChoose When Flink You need sub-second latency, complex event-time processing, or sophisticated stateful operations Spark You have existing Spark batch pipelines, need ML integration, or second-level latency is acceptable Beam Multi-cloud portability matters, or you want to standardize on one API across diverse infrastructure For greenfield projects requiring low-latency streaming, Flink is often the strongest choice. For organizations with heavy Spark investments adding streaming capabilities, Spark Structured Streaming provides the gentlest migration path. For multi-cloud enterprises, Beam offers valuable flexibility. The Practical Impact Unified processing simplifies development and operations: One codebase for batch and stream logic—no divergence between implementations 3/4

One test suite that validates both modes Easier backfills—run the same code over historical data to rebuild outputs Simpler reasoning—same semantics whether data is bounded or unbounded The batch-stream distinction becomes an input configuration rather than an architectural decision. You choose based on latency requirements and data availability, not because you need different code. What's Next We've covered the core processing models: batch (MapReduce, Spark), stream (Flink, Kafka Streams), hybrid architectures (Lambda, Kappa), and unified frameworks. The following articles address practical patterns: ETL vs ELT, backfill strategies, error handling with dead-letter queues, and real-time analytics architectures. 4/4

Big Data › Pipeline Operations

ETL vs ELT Data Pipeline Transformation Patterns

Open PDF

ETL vs ELT: Data Pipeline Transformation Patterns ETL: Extract → Transform → Load Sources Transform Spark/ETL Warehouse Clean data raw clean Transform outside warehouse. Raw data not preserved. ETL vs ELT Data pipelines move data from source systems to analytical destinations. A fundamental question shapes pipeline architecture: where do you transform the data? The answer has shifted dramatically as storage costs dropped and cloud warehouse compute became elastic. Understanding both patterns helps you choose the right approach for your constraints. Traditional ETL: Transform Before Loading ETL (Extract-Transform-Load) processes data in a separate transformation layer before loading into the destination. ETL: Extract → Transform → Load Sources Transform Spark/ETL Warehouse Clean data raw clean Transform outside warehouse. Raw data not preserved. 1. Extract raw data from sources—databases, APIs, log files, third-party services 2. Transform in a processing system (Spark, custom scripts, ETL tools)—clean malformed records, deduplicate, normalize schemas, aggregate, join across sources 3. Load the refined, ready-to-use result into the data warehouse This pattern emerged from historical constraints. Data warehouse storage was expensive—you paid per terabyte stored. Warehouse compute was limited—running complex transformations in the warehouse was slow and costly. Transforming before loading reduced both storage and 1/4

query costs. Strengths: Warehouse stays clean with pre-processed, validated data Query performance is better because data arrives optimized for common access patterns Storage costs stay lower—you don't store raw data that may never be queried Sensitive fields can be filtered or masked before entering the warehouse Limitations: Raw data is transformed away—if you realize you need a field you didn't include, you must re-extract from the source Schema changes (new columns, format changes) require pipeline modifications before data can flow Transformation logic lives outside the warehouse, often in tools with different development workflows than SQL Modern ELT: Load First, Transform In Place ELT (Extract-Load-Transform) inverts the order. Load raw data into the warehouse, then transform using the warehouse's own compute. ELT: Extract → Load → Transform Sources Warehouse Raw data Views (dbt) raw Transform inside warehouse. Raw data preserved for flexibility. 1. Extract raw data from sources 2. Load directly into the warehouse, preserving original format (often as JSON or in staging tables) 3. Transform using warehouse-native tools—SQL queries, dbt models, stored procedures The economics flipped. Cloud storage became cheap (pennies per GB/month). Cloud warehouses like BigQuery and Snowflake provide elastic compute that scales instantly. The constraints that drove ETL largely disappeared. Strengths: Full flexibility—raw data is preserved, so new requirements don't need re-extraction Schema evolution is easier—new fields automatically appear in raw tables Multiple teams can derive different views from the same raw data 2/4

Transformations are SQL (which analysts already know) and version-controlled alongside documentation Limitations: Storage grows faster—you keep raw data plus transformed views Raw data may contain sensitive fields that shouldn't persist (PII, credentials) Query costs can increase if users accidentally scan large raw tables Concrete Comparison: Adding a New Metric Suppose marketing wants a new metric: "average order value by customer segment." The source data includes orders, customers, and segment assignments. ETL approach: 1. Modify the extraction job to include segment data (if not already extracted) 2. Update transformation scripts to calculate the new aggregation 3. Deploy, wait for the next pipeline run, validate results 4. If something's wrong with the calculation, re-run the entire pipeline ELT approach: 1. Raw order, customer, and segment data is already in the warehouse 2. Write a SQL transformation (or dbt model) that joins and aggregates 3. Run it—results available in minutes 4. If the calculation needs tweaking, just modify the SQL and re-run The ELT approach is faster to iterate because the data is already available. The transformation is just a SQL query that anyone with warehouse access can write and test. When to Use Each Scenario ETL ELT Strict compliance—raw data can't persist ETL Sensitive data requiring masking before storageETL Very cost-sensitive with large data volumes ETL Evolving requirements with exploratory analysis ELT Data science teams needing raw access ELT Modern cloud warehouse (Snowflake, BigQuery, Databricks) ELT Many organizations use hybrid approaches: ETL for sensitive data that requires transformation before storage, ELT for everything else. 3/4

The Modern Data Stack The "modern data stack" typically follows an ELT pattern: Extract & Load: Tools like Fivetran, Airbyte, or Stitch connect to sources (SaaS APIs, databases, event streams) and load raw data into the warehouse with minimal transformation. Transform: dbt (data build tool) runs SQL-based transformations inside the warehouse. Models are version-controlled, tested, and documented alongside business logic. Warehouse: Snowflake, BigQuery, Databricks, or Redshift store both raw and transformed data with elastic compute. Raw data persists as the "source of truth." Transformations are just SQL that can be re-run anytime. Adding a new metric or fixing a bug means modifying a dbt model, not rebuilding the entire pipeline from source extraction. What's Next Loading data is just the first step. When source data changes and downstream systems need updates, backfill and reprocessing handle historical corrections and schema evolution. 4/4

Big Data › Pipeline Operations

Backfill and Reprocessing Strategies for Data Pipelines

Open PDF

Backfill and Reprocessing Strategies for Data Pipelines Full Reload Source All data Pipeline Reprocess Destination Replace all Simple but slow. Downtime during reload. Backfill & Reprocessing Data pipelines need to rerun. A bug corrupted last month's data. A schema change needs historical records updated. The analytics team wants a new metric computed across all history. In each case, you need to reprocess data that the pipeline already handled. Reprocessing isn't exceptional—it's expected. Production pipelines regularly need to backfill historical data. The question isn't whether you'll need to do it, but how gracefully your pipeline handles it. Common Reprocessing Scenarios Bug fixes. A logic error in your transformation produced incorrect output for the past month. Once you fix the code, you need to reprocess affected data. Schema evolution. You've added a new field to your data model. Existing records need to be backfilled with values computed from raw data. New derived data. Analytics wants a metric that didn't exist when the pipeline was built. You need to compute it across historical data, not just new events. System migration. You're moving to a new storage system or data warehouse. Reprocessing populates the new destination with historical data. Compliance or audit. A data correction is required for regulatory reasons. You need to update specific historical records. Strategy 1: Full Reload The simplest approach: reprocess everything from scratch. Delete the existing output, run the pipeline from the beginning of time, replace all data. 1/4

Full Reload Source All data Pipeline Reprocess Destination Replace all Simple but slow. Downtime during reload. When it works well: Dataset is small enough to reprocess quickly Destination can handle complete replacement without breaking downstream consumers Simplicity is worth more than speed Trade-offs: Slow for large datasets—reprocessing years of data takes time Destination may be unavailable or inconsistent during the reload Expensive in terms of compute resources Strategy 2: Incremental Backfill Reprocess in bounded chunks—typically by time partition (day, week, month). Track which chunks have been completed. Resume from failures without starting over. Incremental Backfill Day 1 2 3... Process by chunk pending Process in chunks. Resume from failures. Lower peak load. Concrete example: Your pipeline partitions output by date. To backfill the last 90 days: 1. List partitions needing reprocessing: 2024-01-01 through 2024-03-31 2. Process each partition independently, writing to its output location 3. Track completed partitions in a checkpoint table 4. If the job fails on day 45, restart from day 45, not day 1 When it works well: 2/4

Large datasets where full reload would take too long Need to show progress and provide estimates Can't afford extended downtime—data becomes available incrementally Trade-offs: More complex coordination logic Must handle partial state—what happens if you crash mid-partition? Dependencies between partitions (e.g., slowly changing dimensions) require careful ordering Strategy 3: Parallel Versioning Write to a completely new output table while the old table continues serving traffic. Once the new version is complete and validated, atomically swap it in. Parallel Versioning Table v1 ← serving Table v2 building...Backfill to v2Source Old serves queries. New builds in parallel. Swap when ready. Concrete example: 1. Current production reads from sales_summary_v1 2. Backfill writes to sales_summary_v2 (new table, same schema) 3. Run validation queries comparing v1 and v2 4. Once confident, update the view/alias to point to v2 5. Keep v1 around briefly for rollback, then delete When it works well: Zero downtime required Need to validate output before exposing it to users Rollback capability is important Trade-offs: Double storage during the migration period Requires an atomic swap mechanism (view, symlink, table rename) Downstream systems must use the indirection layer 3/4

The Foundation: Idempotent Writes Regardless of strategy, the key to safe reprocessing is idempotent writes: processing the same input twice produces the same result without creating duplicates or inconsistencies. Non-idempotent (dangerous): INSERT INTO sales_summary (date, total) VALUES ('2024-01-15', 1500); -- Reprocessing creates duplicate rows Idempotent (safe): INSERT INTO sales_summary (date, total) VALUES ('2024-01-15', 1500) ON CONFLICT (date) DO UPDATE SET total = 1500; -- Reprocessing overwrites, doesn't duplicate For batch pipelines, a common pattern is to partition outputs by time and replace entire partitions atomically: /output/date=2024-01-15/ <- Replace this whole partition /output/date=2024-01-16/ Each partition is either fully present (old version) or fully present (new version), never a mix. Atomic partition replacement is idempotent—processing twice just overwrites with the same data. Designing for Reprocessability Build reprocessing into your pipeline design from the start: Retain source data. Don't transform away the original. If you only store transformed data, you can't recompute with new logic. The ELT pattern helps here—raw data persists in the warehouse. Version your transformations. Track which logic version produced each output (a version column, or metadata in the output path). When logic changes, you know which outputs are stale and need reprocessing. Partition by time. Time-based partitioning makes incremental backfill natural. You can replace one day's data without touching others. It also aligns with typical "reprocess the last N days" workflows. Test reprocessability. Periodically replay historical data through your pipeline as a test. Catch issues (non-idempotent writes, source data retention problems) before they become urgent production incidents. What's Next Reprocessing handles bulk corrections. For individual records that fail during normal pipeline operation, error handling adds dead-letter queues, retries, and monitoring strategies for production pipelines. 4/4

Big Data › Pipeline Operations

Error Handling

Open PDF

Error Handling Production data pipelines encounter failures. Network partitions. Schema mismatches between producers and consumers. Malformed records from buggy upstream systems. External services that rate-limit or timeout. These aren't exceptional—they're expected in production environments. A robust pipeline handles failures gracefully: don't lose data, don't block progress on unrelated records, surface problems for investigation. This article covers the patterns that make this possible. Dead Letter Queues: Don't Drop, Don't Block When a record can't be processed, you have three choices: 1. Drop it. Simple but dangerous—you lose data silently. 2. Block the pipeline. Process nothing until the problem is fixed. One bad record stops everything. 3. Sideline it. Set the bad record aside and continue processing valid records. The third option uses a dead letter queue (DLQ)—a separate storage location for records that failed processing. 1/4

Dead Letter Queue Pattern Input Process Output DLQ Investigate & fix success failed Failed records go to DLQ. Pipeline continues. Investigate later. The pipeline attempts to process each record. Successful records flow to the normal output. Failed records go to the DLQ with metadata about why they failed. The pipeline continues making progress on valid data. Later, operators investigate the DLQ: Some failures are transient (timeout, rate limit)—replay these and they may succeed Some failures are data bugs (malformed JSON)—fix upstream and discard Some failures reveal pipeline bugs—fix the code and reprocess Enriching DLQ entries: Include the original record, error message, timestamp, pipeline version, and which stage failed. This metadata speeds debugging. You might also categorize errors (validation failure, serialization error, downstream timeout) to help prioritize. Common Failure Categories Different failures need different handling: Schema validation failures: A record has a null field where the schema expects a value. Or a string where it expects a number. These typically indicate producer bugs or schema drift—fix the source. Business rule violations: A quantity is negative. A timestamp is in the future. A user ID doesn't exist. These may be valid data that your pipeline doesn't handle, or upstream bugs. Transient downstream failures: The database times out. An API rate-limits you. A service is briefly unavailable. These often succeed on retry. Serialization/parsing errors: Can't decode JSON. Can't deserialize Avro. Often indicates version mismatch between producer and consumer schemas. Retry Strategies Transient failures often succeed on retry. But how you retry matters. 2/4

Immediate retry (usually wrong): The downstream service timed out because it's overloaded. Retrying instantly adds more load. The service stays overloaded. You create a feedback loop of failure. Exponential backoff: Space retries out. Wait 1 second, then 2, then 4, then 8. This gives the downstream service time to recover while still making progress. Exponential Backoff with Jitter 1 fail 2 fail 3 fail 4 ok! 1s 2s 4s + jitter Wait 1s, 2s, 4s... with random jitter to prevent thundering herd Jitter: Add randomness to retry timing. Without jitter, if 100 workers all fail at the same time, they all retry at the same time (thundering herd). Each retry wave fails again. With jitter, retries spread out: worker 1 waits 1.3s, worker 2 waits 0.9s, worker 3 waits 1.7s. retry_delay = base_delay * (2 ^ attempt) + random(0, base_delay) Circuit breaker: If a downstream dependency is broken, stop calling it. After enough failures (e.g., 50% failure rate over 10 seconds), "open" the circuit—fail fast without actually calling the dependency. Periodically try again. If it succeeds, "close" the circuit and resume normal operation. This protects both you (don't waste resources on calls that will fail) and the dependency (don't pile more load on an overloaded service). Configuring Max Retries Set a limit on retry attempts. Without a limit, records can retry forever, consuming resources and hiding the underlying problem. A typical pattern: 1. Attempt processing 2. On failure, retry up to 3 times with exponential backoff and jitter 3. After exhausting retries, write to DLQ 4. Alert operators 5. Continue processing other records 3/4

The exact numbers depend on your use case. Short-lived transient failures (network blips) recover in 2-3 retries. Longer outages (service deployment, database failover) might need more retries or longer backoff. Monitoring: Making Failures Visible Pipelines run continuously without direct user interaction. Failures must surface through monitoring rather than user complaints. Lag monitoring: How far behind real-time is the pipeline? If events happened at 10:00 but the pipeline is only processing events from 9:45, lag is 15 minutes. Growing lag means processing isn't keeping up—either throughput dropped or input volume increased. Error rate: What fraction of records fail processing? A sudden spike indicates systemic problems: bad deployment, schema change, dependency outage. A slow increase might indicate data quality degradation upstream. DLQ depth: How many records are waiting in the dead letter queue? A non-zero DLQ is normal. Rapid growth indicates an ongoing problem needing investigation. Data quality checks: Output properties should match expectations. Record counts align with inputs (accounting for expected filtering). Values fall within valid ranges. Duplicate detection finds expected rates. These checks catch bugs that don't cause failures but produce wrong results. End-to-end latency: Time from when an event was created to when it's available in the serving layer. This is what users experience. Even if each pipeline stage is fast, the sum might violate SLAs. Treating Pipelines as Production Systems Data pipelines deserve the same operational rigor as user-facing applications: Dashboards showing health metrics at a glance Alerts on degradation before users notice On-call rotations with clear escalation paths Runbooks for common failures Capacity planning to handle growth A silent pipeline failure that corrupts analytics for a week is often worse than a user-facing outage that gets fixed in minutes. What's Next Error handling keeps pipelines running. But what about the serving layer—how do you make analytical data available with low latency? The next section covers real-time analytics: materialized views, time-series patterns, and streaming analytics architectures. 4/4

Big Data › Real-time & Analytics

Materialized Views

Open PDF

Materialized Views Materialized View Pattern Events (Kafka) Processor aggregate View (Redis) Dashboard stream write read Pre-compute aggregates as events arrive. Dashboard reads pre-computed values. A dashboard shows page views per minute. The user clicks refresh. The backend runs a query that scans millions of raw events, groups by minute, and returns counts. At low volume, this works. But a busy site generates millions of events daily. Every dashboard refresh scans all that data—and there might be hundreds of concurrent users. The solution: compute aggregates as events arrive, not when users query. Dashboard reads become key lookups instead of full table scans. The Problem: Queries That Don't Scale Consider the straightforward query behind a "views per minute" chart: SELECT minute, COUNT(*) FROM page_events WHERE timestamp > NOW() - INTERVAL '1 hour' GROUP BY minute; For a small site, this completes in milliseconds. But scale changes everything. At 10 million events per day, this query scans roughly 400,000 rows per hour. With 50 concurrent dashboard users refreshing every 10 seconds, the database handles 5 queries per second—each scanning 400,000 rows. That's 2 million row scans per second, just for one chart. You might think: "Add an index on timestamp." That helps filter to the right hour. But GROUP BY still requires scanning every matching row. The fundamental problem remains: query cost grows with data volume. Materialized Views: Compute on Write, Not Read A materialized view inverts the work. Instead of computing aggregates when users query, compute them as data arrives. Store the results. Queries become lookups. A regular database view is just a saved query—it computes on every read. A materialized view stores the computed result. The difference is when work happens: read time vs write time. 1/3

For streaming systems, this means the stream processor maintains running aggregates as events flow through. Each event updates the relevant counters. When a user queries, the answer is already waiting. Materialized View Pattern Events (Kafka) Processor aggregate View (Redis) Dashboard stream write read Pre-compute aggregates as events arrive. Dashboard reads pre-computed values. How It Works: Page Views Example Let's walk through a concrete implementation. We want instant answers to "page views per minute for the last hour." Step 1: Events flow from the source. Page view events arrive in Kafka. Each event contains a page ID, user ID, timestamp, and metadata. Step 2: The stream processor windows events. Using tumbling windows (covered in earlier articles), the processor groups events by (page_id, minute). Each minute boundary triggers output. Step 3: Window results write to a fast store. When a window closes, the processor writes to Redis: Key: page:{id}:minute:{timestamp} Value: count Step 4: Dashboard queries the store. To show the last hour, the dashboard fetches 60 keys —one per minute. Each Redis lookup returns in microseconds. Response time is independent of how many events occurred. The dashboard query transforms from "scan 400,000 rows" to "fetch 60 keys." That's the power of pre-aggregation. Checkpoint: So far we've moved computation from query time to ingest time. But this creates new challenges. What happens when we need different slices of data? What about late-arriving events? The Trade-offs Pre-aggregation isn't free. Understanding the costs helps you decide what to pre-compute. 2/3

Storage grows with dimension combinations. Aggregate by page and minute: manageable. Now add region. You need a key for every (page, region, minute) combination. Add device type, browser, experiment variant—keys multiply exponentially. Consider a site with 10,000 pages, 50 regions, 5 device types, 10 experiment variants. Minute- level aggregates for all combinations would need 25 million keys per minute. That's 1.5 billion keys per hour just for retention. Pre-aggregating every possible slice is impractical. Late events complicate updates. An event's timestamp says 10:05, but it arrives at 10:08. The 10:05 window closed three minutes ago. What do you do? Three options: 1. Ignore late events. Simple implementation, but counts will be systematically lower than reality. Works if you can tolerate slight inaccuracy. 2. Update the materialized view. Correct, but downstream consumers must handle changing values. The "10:05 count" they cached might be stale. 3. Use wider watermarks. Wait longer before closing windows. More accurate, but results appear later. There's no universally right answer—it depends on whether accuracy or timeliness matters more for your use case. Pre-aggregation locks in query patterns. You decided to aggregate by page and minute. A product manager asks for hourly views by device type. That requires a different aggregation, code changes, and waiting for data to accumulate. Pre-aggregation trades query flexibility for query speed. Practical Approach: The 80/20 Rule Pre-aggregate the queries that matter most. Typically, a small number of dashboards account for most of the query load—the operational metrics everyone checks constantly. For those high-traffic queries, materialized views are worth the complexity. Real-time dashboards showing request rates, error counts, and active users benefit most. For ad-hoc exploration—"What was the click rate for users in Germany on mobile last Tuesday?"—keep raw events accessible in a separate system. A data warehouse handles these queries with acceptable latency (seconds, not milliseconds). Users running ad-hoc analyses can tolerate that delay. This two-tier approach gives you the best of both worlds: instant results for the metrics everyone watches, and flexibility for the questions you haven't thought of yet. What's Next Materialized views solve the query-time problem by moving computation earlier. From here, time-series patterns cover bucketing, rollups, and retention policies for metrics data. 3/3

Big Data › Real-time & Analytics

Time-Series Patterns

Open PDF

Time-Series Patterns The previous article showed how materialized views pre-compute aggregates for instant queries. But those aggregates are themselves time-series data—metrics measured at regular intervals. As metrics accumulate, storage grows without bound. Querying months of history gets slower. This article covers patterns for efficient time-series storage: bucketing, rollups, and retention policies. Bucketing: Fixed Time Boundaries The simplest pattern stores aggregates in time-aligned buckets. The key includes the time boundary: metric:page_views:minute:2024-01-15T10:05:00 metric:page_views:minute:2024-01-15T10:06:00 metric:page_views:minute:2024-01-15T10:07:00 Why fixed boundaries? Consider querying "views in the last 10 minutes." With minute-aligned buckets, this fetches exactly 10 keys. No scanning. No filtering within buckets. The query planner knows exactly which keys to read before fetching any data. 1/4

Time Bucketing 10:05 1,234 10:06 1,456 10:07 1,089 10:08 1,567... page_views:minute:2024-01-15T10:05:00 → 1234 Fixed time boundaries. Query "last 10 min" = fetch exactly 10 keys. Without fixed boundaries—storing events at arbitrary timestamps—querying a time range requires scanning and filtering. Fixed boundaries convert range queries into point lookups. Choosing Bucket Granularity Match bucket size to how the data will be queried: GranularityUse Case 1 second Real-time monitoring dashboards, rapid feedback loops 1 minute Operational dashboards, alerting systems 1 hour Business metrics, daily pattern analysis 1 day Historical trends, weekly/monthly reports Finer granularity gives more detail but costs more storage. A metric with 1-second resolution produces 86,400 data points per day. At 1-minute resolution, that drops to 1,440. Checkpoint: Bucketing solves the query efficiency problem. But storing fine-grained data indefinitely isn't practical. A year of 1-second data is 31 million points per metric. How do we keep recent data detailed while compacting older data? Rollups: Coarser Granularity Over Time Users need second-level detail for live dashboards. But nobody zooms into a one-second window from six months ago. Match retention granularity to query patterns. A rollup aggregates fine-grained buckets into coarser ones. As data ages, detail decreases but storage stays bounded. 2/4

Rollup Pattern Last 24 hours 1-min granularity Last 30 days 1-hour granularity Last year 1-day granularity → → 60 one-minute buckets → 1 hourly bucket → 24 hourly → 1 daily Storage drops from 31M points/year to roughly 10K points/year A typical retention policy: Last 24 hours: 1-minute granularity (1,440 points per metric) Last 30 days: 1-hour granularity (720 points per metric) Last year: 1-day granularity (365 points per metric) Total storage: roughly 2,500 points per metric per year, regardless of how many years of data you keep. Without rollups, one year of 1-second data would be 31 million points. How Rollups Work A background job runs periodically to aggregate older data: 1. Read the 60 one-minute buckets for 10:00-10:59 2. Compute aggregates (sum, average, min, max, count) into one 1-hour bucket 3. Write the 1-hour bucket 4. Delete or expire the minute-level buckets The rollup job typically runs hourly for minute-to-hour aggregation, and daily for hour-to-day aggregation. Timing matters: run after all events for the period have arrived (accounting for late data). What aggregates to keep depends on your queries. For metrics you'll sum (total requests), keep the sum. For metrics you'll average (latency), keep both sum and count so you can compute accurate averages across rolled-up periods. Min and max are useful for alerting ("what was the peak?"). Retention Policies with TTL Many time-series stores support TTL (time-to-live)—keys automatically expire after a configured period. Set TTLs to match your retention tiers: Minute-level buckets: 24-hour TTL 3/4

Hour-level buckets: 30-day TTL Day-level buckets: 1-year TTL or indefinite TTL-based expiration handles cleanup without explicit deletion jobs. Redis, many time-series databases, and some NoSQL stores support this natively. Practical tip: Match retention to actual query patterns. If nobody queries minute-level data older than 2 hours, reduce the TTL. Storage costs drop with no impact on users. Analyze your query logs to find the right boundaries. Handling High-Cardinality Dimensions Rollups work well when the number of distinct keys is bounded. But what about high-cardinality dimensions? Consider tracking "page views by user." With millions of users, you'd need millions of keys per time bucket. Storage explodes. Most of those keys have counts of 1 or 2—not useful for dashboards. For high-cardinality cases, approximate algorithms help: HyperLogLog for distinct counts. "How many unique users visited today?" The answer might be 1,847,293 or 1,852,106—either is useful for dashboards. HyperLogLog provides estimates within a few percent accuracy using fixed memory (typically 12 KB), regardless of cardinality. Count-Min Sketch for frequency estimation. "How often did each URL appear?" A probabilistic data structure tracks approximate counts without storing every distinct value. Sampling for high-volume streams. A 1% sample of events provides statistically valid aggregates for large-scale patterns. Store 1% of events in full detail, use aggregates for the rest. These approximations trade precision for bounded storage. For most analytics use cases, "roughly 1.8 million users" is as useful as "exactly 1,847,293 users." What's Next Time-series patterns solve the storage efficiency problem.
