Rate Limiting Patterns

Open PDF

Rate Limiting Patterns Token Bucket Algorithm Capacity: 100 tokens 0 to e s Refill: 1 token / 600ms Request Pattern T=0s: 100 requests (burst) T=0.6s: 1 req T=1.2s: 1 req T=1.8s: 1 req Burst allowed, then wait for refill Allows bursts up to bucket capacity Enforces average rate via refill speed Matches real traffic patterns Used by most APIs (Twitter, GitHub, Stripe) An API without rate limiting faces abuse. A user script calls the endpoint 100,000 times per second. The database saturates. Response times spike for all users. Legitimate traffic suffers because one bad actor consumes all resources. Rate limiting prevents any single client from overwhelming the system. Why Rate Limit Rate limiting protects resources from overuse. APIs impose limits to maintain availability for all clients. Free tier users get 100 requests per minute. Paid tier users get 10,000. This prevents free users from consuming capacity meant for paying customers. Rate limiting also prevents abuse. Credential stuffing attacks try thousands of password combinations per second. Search crawlers scrape entire websites. Automated bots create fake accounts. Rate limits slow these attacks to manageable levels. Different resources need different limits. Write operations consume more resources than reads. Creating a post might have a limit of 10 per minute. Reading posts might allow 1,000 per minute. Complex search queries get 100 per minute. Simple lookups get 10,000. 1/6

Token Bucket Algorithm The token bucket holds a fixed number of tokens. Each request consumes one token. Tokens refill at a constant rate. When the bucket empties, requests wait or fail. A bucket configured for 100 requests per minute holds 100 tokens. Every 600 milliseconds, one token arrives. A client can burst 100 requests instantly, consuming all tokens. Then it must wait 600ms between requests for tokens to refill. Token Bucket Algorithm Capacity: 100 tokens 0 to e s Refill: 1 token / 600ms Request Pattern T=0s: 100 requests (burst) T=0.6s: 1 req T=1.2s: 1 req T=1.8s: 1 req Burst allowed, then wait for refill Allows bursts up to bucket capacity Enforces average rate via refill speed Matches real traffic patterns Used by most APIs (Twitter, GitHub, Stripe) The algorithm uses four parameters. Bucket capacity sets the maximum burst size. Refill rate determines sustained throughput. Current token count tracks available capacity. Last refill timestamp enables rate calculation. When a request arrives, calculate elapsed time since last refill. Add tokens based on elapsed time and refill rate, capped at bucket capacity. If tokens are available, allow the request and decrement the count. If no tokens remain, reject the request. The token bucket allows bursts while enforcing average rate. A user can send 100 requests immediately, then waits. This matches real usage patterns where traffic comes in bursts. A dashboard loading makes 20 API calls at once. A background job processes a batch of 50 items. Leaky Bucket Algorithm The leaky bucket processes requests at a fixed rate regardless of arrival pattern. Requests enter a queue. The bucket drains at constant speed. When the queue fills, new requests overflow and fail. 2/6

Configure a bucket for 10 requests per second with queue size 100. Requests arrive at any rate but process at exactly 10 per second. If 50 requests arrive simultaneously, they queue. The system processes them over 5 seconds at the fixed rate. Leaky Bucket Algorithm Bursty incoming Queue (max 100) 35 queued Fixed rate: 10 req/s ↓ Smooth outgoing Processing Timeline T=0.0s: 1 T=0.1s: 1 T=0.2s: 1 T=0.3s: 1 Always 10 req/s, no bursts Key Properties Smooth output rate Queues bursts Adds latency Protects downstream The leaky bucket enforces smooth output. Token bucket allows bursts followed by waiting. Leaky bucket spreads requests evenly over time. This protects downstream systems that cannot handle spikes. Payment processing APIs use leaky buckets. The payment gateway accepts only 5 transactions per second. Queueing requests ensures compliance with that limit. The queue absorbs bursts during peak traffic while maintaining steady processing. The tradeoff is latency. Token bucket serves bursts immediately. Leaky bucket queues everything for smooth processing. A user refreshing a page waits while their request sits in queue behind earlier arrivals. Sliding Window Algorithm Fixed windows reset at boundaries. A limit of 100 requests per minute resets at 00:00:00. A client sends 100 requests at 00:00:59. The counter resets at 00:01:00. The client sends another 100 at 00:01:01. That's 200 requests in 2 seconds, violating the spirit of the limit. 3/6

Fixed Window Problem: Boundary Gaming Limit: 100 requests per minute Window 1 00:00:00 - 00:00:59 100 at :59 RESET 00:01:00 Window 2 00:01:00 - 00:01:59 100 at :01 ⚠ 200 requests in 2 seconds! 100 at 00:00:59 + 100 at 00:01:01 = boundary gaming Sliding windows solve this. Track request timestamps for the past minute. When a new request arrives, count requests in the 60-second window ending now. If count exceeds limit, reject. At 14:32:45, check requests from 14:31:45 to 14:32:45. At 14:32:46, check from 14:31:46 to 14:32:46. The window slides continuously, preventing boundary gaming. Sliding Window Algorithm Limit: 100 requests per minute (window slides continuously) :15 :30 :45 14:32:45 14:33:15 Window at 14:32:45 14:31:45 → 14:32:45 80 requests counted slides → Window at 14:32:46 14:31:46 → 14:32:46 79 requests counted ✓ Window slides every second — no boundary gaming possible Implementation requires storing timestamps. For a limit of 100 requests per minute, store up to 100 timestamps per user. On each request, remove timestamps older than 60 seconds. Count remaining timestamps. If count is below limit, allow and add new timestamp. Memory usage grows with limit size. A limit of 10,000 requests per hour requires storing 10,000 timestamps per user. Redis sorted sets work well. Use timestamps as scores and member IDs as values. ZREMRANGEBYSCORE removes old entries. ZCARD counts current entries. 4/6

Sliding Window Counter (Approximation) Storing timestamps is expensive. The sliding window counter approximates using two fixed- window counters with weighted calculation. Track requests in current minute and previous minute. At 14:32:45, you have 15 seconds into current minute. Weight calculation: previous minute count × 0.75 + current minute count × 1.0. If the sum exceeds limit, reject. At 14:32:45 with 100 request limit, previous minute had 80 requests, current minute has 30. Weighted sum: 80 × (45/60) + 30 = 60 + 30 = 90. Allow the request. This approximation uses two counters instead of thousands of timestamps. Memory usage drops from O(limit) to O(1). The accuracy trade-off is acceptable for most systems. Errors stay within single-digit percentages. Distributed Rate Limiting Single-server rate limiting uses local memory. Distributed systems need coordination across servers. Server 1 and Server 2 each allow 100 requests per minute. A client routes requests evenly across both. They can send 200 requests per minute, violating the intended 100 limit. Centralized rate limiting uses Redis. All servers check the same counter. Atomic operations prevent race conditions. INCR increments and returns the new value atomically. If the value exceeds limit, reject. def is_allowed(user_id, limit): key = f"rate_limit:{user_id}:{current_minute}" count = redis.incr(key) if count == 1: redis.expire(key, 60) # Set expiration on first request return count <= limit Redis becomes a single point of failure. Use Redis Cluster or replicas for availability. The network call adds latency but ensures consistent limits. Local counting with periodic synchronization offers an alternative. Each server maintains local counters and syncs to Redis every second. Server 1 processes 23 requests locally, then adds 23 to Redis. Server 2 adds 19. Redis holds global count 42. Before allowing a request, check: local count + Redis count < limit. This reduces Redis calls from every request to once per second per server. The tradeoff is potential overages during the sync interval. Two servers might each allow 60 requests before syncing, totaling 120 when limit is 100. Choosing an Algorithm Token bucket fits most use cases. It allows bursts matching real traffic patterns while enforcing average rate. APIs use token buckets because client applications legitimately send bursts. Dashboard loads make many calls at once. Background jobs process batches. 5/6

Use leaky bucket when downstream systems require smooth traffic. Payment gateways with strict rate limits need steady flow. Video transcoding pipelines process at fixed capacity. Message queues with limited consumer throughput benefit from queueing. Sliding window provides strictest enforcement. Use it when limit violations have serious consequences. Billing APIs charging money need precise limits. Security-critical endpoints blocking brute force attacks require accuracy. The memory cost is worthwhile when accuracy matters. Fixed window with sliding window counter offers good balance. The approximation is accurate enough for general protection. Low memory usage scales to millions of users. Most production systems use this approach. Interview Application When asked to add rate limiting to an API, start with the resource being protected. Write operations cost more than reads. Complex queries cost more than simple lookups. Different endpoints need different limits. Explain the algorithm choice. For a Twitter-like API, suggest token bucket. Users post in bursts but average rate stays low. A user might compose 5 tweets in a minute, then nothing for an hour. Token bucket allows the burst while enforcing daily limits. For distributed rate limiting, mention Redis with atomic operations. Note the latency trade-off. Suggest local counting with periodic sync if latency matters. Explain the potential overage during sync intervals. Discuss user experience. When a user hits the limit, return HTTP 429 with Retry-After header. Tell them when they can try again. Log rate limit violations to detect abuse patterns. Provide higher limits for authenticated users versus anonymous traffic. Show you understand the trade-offs between burst allowance, smooth flow, accuracy, and implementation complexity. This demonstrates system design maturity. 6/6

Patterns & Templates › Patterns

Two-Stage Processing Pattern for System Design Interviews

Open PDF

Two-Stage Processing Pattern for System Design Interviews The Two-Stage Processing Pattern A search engine indexes 10 million documents. When a user searches for "distributed systems", the system must return the 10 most relevant results ranked by quality. Running a complex relevance algorithm on 10 million documents takes too long. The search times out or the user waits seconds instead of milliseconds. Two-stage processing solves this by filtering data first then applying the expensive algorithm to a small set. Fast Filter Then Precise Rank Two-stage processing splits the work into rapid filtering and precise processing. The first stage quickly eliminates irrelevant candidates using simple, fast techniques. The second stage applies the expensive algorithm to the small remaining set. 1/4

Two-Stage Processing Pattern Input Dataset 10 million items... Stage 1: Rapid Filtering Fast, Approximate • Hash lookups • Inverted indexes • Precomputed summaries Filtered Candidates 1,000 items 99.99% reduction Stage 2: Precision Processing Slow, Accurate Heavy computation • Complex ML models • Deep analysis • Detailed scoring Final Results Top 10 items Highly accurate & ranked Example: Search engine processes 10M documents → 1K keyword matches → Top 10 ranked results Stage 1 (inverted index): 20ms | Stage 2 (ranking algorithm): 80ms | Total: 100ms vs 10+ seconds direct Stage one prioritizes speed over precision. An inverted index maps keywords to documents. The query "distributed systems" looks up both words and intersects the document sets. This finds 50,000 documents containing both terms in milliseconds. The index uses hash lookups and boolean operations. No complex scoring happens yet. Stage two prioritizes precision. The relevance algorithm examines each of the 50,000 candidates. It scores them using term frequency, document popularity, user location, and click- through history. This computation is expensive. Running it on 50,000 documents instead of 10 million makes it feasible. The system sorts by score and returns the top 10. How This Scales The filtering stage reduces the dataset by orders of magnitude. Processing 10 million items with a complex algorithm might take 30 seconds. Filtering to 1,000 candidates in 20 milliseconds then processing those in 80 milliseconds completes in 100 milliseconds total. The fast stage does most of the work. The slow stage runs on a tiny fraction of the data. Each stage can scale independently. The filtering stage might run on machines with fast SSDs and lots of RAM for indexes. The precision stage might run on GPU machines for machine learning models. You can add more filtering machines to handle higher query rates. You can add more precision machines if scoring becomes the bottleneck. 2/4

Stage one can be approximate. A recommendation engine might use collaborative filtering to quickly find 500 similar users. Some of those users might not actually be similar. The second stage runs a deep learning model on those 500 profiles to predict which items to recommend. The deep model corrects mistakes from the fast filter. Applications Search engines use this pattern extensively. The inverted index finds keyword matches. The ranking algorithm scores relevance. Google processes billions of web pages this way. The index eliminates 99.99% of pages in milliseconds. Ranking runs on the remaining candidates. Recommendation systems generate candidate items quickly then score them precisely. Netflix might use viewing history to find 1,000 potentially relevant movies in stage one. Stage two runs a neural network to predict ratings for those 1,000 movies and returns the top 10. Running the neural network on the entire catalog would be too slow. Fraud detection flags suspicious patterns then investigates them deeply. Stage one checks transaction amounts, merchant categories, and geographic locations against simple rules. It flags 0.1% of transactions. Stage two runs machine learning models, checks external databases, and analyzes historical patterns for those flagged transactions. Image similarity searches embed images into vectors then compares candidates. Stage one uses locality-sensitive hashing to find images with similar embeddings. This reduces 10 million images to 500 candidates. Stage two computes precise similarity scores using detailed feature comparisons. Design Considerations Stage one must not filter out correct results. If a search query filters out the most relevant document, stage two cannot recover it. The filtering stage should err on the side of including too many candidates rather than too few. Better to pass 10,000 items to stage two than to miss the best result by filtering too aggressively. The intermediate data format matters. Passing 50,000 full documents from stage one to stage two wastes bandwidth. Pass document IDs instead. Stage two fetches the documents it needs. If stage two needs specific fields, include only those fields in the intermediate format. Monitor the reduction ratio between stages. If stage one outputs 100,000 items and stage two struggles to process them quickly enough, tighten the filtering criteria. If stage one outputs 10 items and stage two is mostly idle, loosen the filtering to improve result quality. Interview Application When asked to design a search system, explain that ranking algorithms are too slow to run on every document. Propose a two-stage approach where an inverted index finds keyword matches quickly and a ranking algorithm scores those matches for relevance. 3/4

Discuss the trade-off between stage one accuracy and performance. A simpler filter runs faster but might miss relevant results or pass too many candidates to stage two. Show that you understand both stages must be tuned together. Mention concrete techniques for each stage. Stage one might use hash lookups, inverted indexes, bloom filters, or locality-sensitive hashing. Stage two might use machine learning models, graph algorithms, or detailed similarity scoring. The choice depends on the specific problem. 4/4

Patterns & Templates › Patterns

Fan-Out Fan-In Pattern

Open PDF

Fan-Out/Fan-In Pattern A user opens their news feed. The system must fetch posts from 500 friends. Querying 500 users sequentially takes seconds. The user expects results in milliseconds. Fan-out/fan-in solves this by distributing work across multiple database shards in parallel then aggregating results. The Pattern Fan-out splits a single request into multiple parallel sub-requests. Each sub-request processes independently on different servers or shards. Fan-in collects all sub-request results and combines them into one response. Twitter timeline generation demonstrates this. When @alice loads her timeline, the system fans out to fetch recent tweets from each person she follows. If Alice follows 300 people, the system queries 300 user timelines. Running these queries in parallel across 10 database shards reduces latency from seconds to milliseconds. 1/5

Fan-Out/Fan-In Pattern Request Load feed FAN-OUT Shard 1 Friends 1-100 Shard 2 101-200 Shard 3 201-300 Shard 4 301-400 Shard 5 401-500 ⚡ Parallel processing (all shards query simultaneously) 10 posts 10 posts 10 posts 10 posts 10 posts FAN-IN Merged Result Top 50 posts The pattern appears in two scenarios: parallel reads and parallel writes. News feeds fan out reads across shards. Notification systems fan out writes to millions of user inboxes. Fan-Out Read Pattern Search engines use fan-out reads. A query for "system design" searches billions of documents. The search index partitions into 100 shards. The query fans out to all 100 shards. Each shard returns its top 10 matching documents. Fan-in merges the 1,000 results and returns the global top 10. Sequential processing would query each shard one after another. At 50ms per shard, 100 shards take 5,000ms. Parallel fan-out queries all shards simultaneously. The total latency equals the slowest shard, typically 50-100ms. The 50x speedup makes the search practical. 2/5

Fan-Out Read: Search Query Search Query "system design" Sequential (Slow) Shard 1: 50ms Shard 2: 50ms Shard 3: 50ms... Total: 5,000ms (100 shards × 50ms each) Parallel (Fast) Shard 1 Shard 2 Shard 3 Shard 4... (all 100 shards) All process simultaneously Total: 50-100ms (slowest shard latency) ⚡ 50x speedup with parallel fan-out E-commerce product search follows the same pattern. A query for "laptop under $1000" searches across product catalog shards. Each shard filters by price and category. Results merge and sort by relevance. The user sees top matches within milliseconds. The challenge is handling stragglers. If 99 shards respond in 50ms but one takes 500ms, the entire request waits. Solutions include timeouts with partial results, backup requests to replicas, or hedged requests that query multiple replicas and use the first response. Fan-Out Write Pattern Notification systems use fan-out writes. When a user posts a photo, the system sends notifications to all followers. A celebrity with 10 million followers triggers 10 million notification writes. Writing 10 million notifications sequentially is impossible within reasonable time. The system fans out writes across hundreds of servers. Each server handles 50,000 notifications. Total write time drops from hours to seconds. 3/5

Fan-Out Write: Notifications User Posts Photo 10M followers Fan-Out Coordinator Worker Pool 1 Write to users 1 - 50,000 Pool 2 50,001 - 100,000 Pool 3 Pool 199 Worker Pool 200 Write to users 9,950,001 - 10M ⚡ 200 workers write in parallel (50,000 notifications each) 10 Million User Devices... Total time: ~5 seconds (vs hours sequentially) Twitter's timeline architecture uses fan-out writes for the home timeline. When @alice tweets, the system writes her tweet to the inbox of every follower. This pre-computes the timeline. When a follower loads their feed, it reads from their pre-built inbox instead of querying all followees at read time. The trade-off is write amplification. One tweet generates millions of writes for popular accounts. Twitter uses a hybrid approach. Regular users get fan-out writes. Celebrities with millions of followers skip fan-out. Their followers merge celebrity tweets at read time to avoid billions of writes per tweet. Aggregation Strategies Fan-in must combine results from multiple sources. The aggregation strategy depends on the operation. Merge sort combines sorted lists. Search results from each shard come pre-sorted by relevance. Fan-in merges them maintaining sort order. This produces globally ranked results efficiently. Union combines all results without duplicates. A news feed pulls posts from multiple friend groups. Fan-in unions all posts and deduplicates by post ID. Intersection finds common results. A search for "red laptop" intersects results for "red" and results for "laptop". Only documents in both sets appear in final results. 4/5

Aggregation (sum, count, average) combines numeric values. An analytics query counts total orders across regional databases. Each region returns its count. Fan-in sums them for the global total. Handling Failures Partial failures are inevitable. When fan-out queries 100 shards, one might fail. The system must decide: wait indefinitely, timeout and fail, or return partial results. Returning partial results maintains availability. If 99 out of 100 shards respond, return those results with a warning. Search engines do this routinely. One missing shard removes 1% of results but keeps the service available. Timeouts prevent one slow shard from blocking everything. Set a deadline like 100ms. Shards responding within the deadline contribute results. Slow shards get excluded. This provides bounded latency at the cost of completeness. Backup requests improve reliability. Send the request to the primary shard. If it doesn't respond within 50ms, send to a replica. Use whichever responds first. This masks failures and slow nodes with minimal extra load. Interview Application When asked to design a news feed, explain the fan-out/fan-in pattern explicitly. Alice follows 500 people. Loading her timeline fans out to 500 user timelines across database shards. Each shard returns the 10 most recent posts from its users. Fan-in merges 5,000 posts and selects the 50 most recent. Discuss the write path alternative. Instagram pre-computes timelines using fan-out writes. When Bob posts, the system writes to all follower inboxes. This moves work from read time to write time. Reads become simple inbox lookups instead of complex aggregations. Mention the celebrity problem. Accounts with millions of followers create write amplification. One post generates millions of writes. Suggest a hybrid approach: fan-out writes for regular users, fan-in reads for celebrities. Show awareness of the trade-off. For notification systems, explain fan-out writes to millions of devices. Mention work queues to parallelize the writes. Discuss idempotency in case of retries. Show you understand both the distribution mechanism and the failure scenarios. This pattern appears in most large-scale system designs. Demonstrating understanding of when to fan-out reads versus writes, how to aggregate, and how to handle failures shows strong distributed systems knowledge. 5/5

Patterns & Templates › Patterns

Saga Pattern

Open PDF

Saga Pattern Saga Pattern: Success vs Failure Paths Example: Book flight, reserve hotel, rent car ✓ Success Path: All Steps Succeed ① Book Flight Flight Service Status: COMMITTED Success ② Reserve Hotel Hotel Service Status: COMMITTED Success ③ Rent Car Car Rental Service Status: COMMITTED Trip Booked All confirmed ✗ Failure Path: Car Rental Fails → Compensate ① Book Flight Flight Service Status: COMMITTED ✓ Success ② Reserve Hotel Hotel Service Status: COMMITTED ✓ Success ③ Rent Car Car Rental Service Status: FAILED ✗ Failure detected! Trigger compensations ④ Cancel Hotel Compensation TX Hotel reservation canceled Compensate ⑤ Cancel Flight Compensation TX Flight booking canceled Trip Booking Failed All compensations completed A user books a trip: reserve flight, book hotel, rent car. Three services. Three databases. The flight books successfully. The hotel reservation fails. Without coordination, the user has a flight but no hotel. The system must either complete all steps or undo completed ones. The Saga pattern coordinates multi-step operations across services using compensating transactions. The Problem Traditional database transactions use locks. Begin transaction, update multiple tables, commit or rollback. Either all changes apply or none do. This works within one database but fails across services. Microservices use separate databases. The Order Service cannot lock rows in the Payment Service database. Two-Phase Commit attempts to solve this but requires blocking, adds latency, and creates availability problems. Most systems need a better approach. 1/5

How Saga Works A saga breaks a transaction into local transactions, one per service. Each local transaction commits immediately to its database. If a later step fails, the saga executes compensating transactions to undo previous steps. For the trip booking example, the success path commits each reservation: 1. Book flight → committed to flight database 2. Reserve hotel → committed to hotel database 3. Rent car → committed to rental database The failure path uses compensating transactions: 1. Book flight → committed 2. Reserve hotel → committed 3. Rent car → fails 4. Cancel hotel (compensating transaction) 5. Cancel flight (compensating transaction) Saga Pattern: Success vs Failure Paths Example: Book flight, reserve hotel, rent car ✓ Success Path: All Steps Succeed ① Book Flight Flight Service Status: COMMITTED Success ② Reserve Hotel Hotel Service Status: COMMITTED Success ③ Rent Car Car Rental Service Status: COMMITTED Trip Booked All confirmed ✗ Failure Path: Car Rental Fails → Compensate ① Book Flight Flight Service Status: COMMITTED ✓ Success ② Reserve Hotel Hotel Service Status: COMMITTED ✓ Success ③ Rent Car Car Rental Service Status: FAILED ✗ Failure detected! Trigger compensations ④ Cancel Hotel Compensation TX Hotel reservation canceled Compensate ⑤ Cancel Flight Compensation TX Flight booking canceled Trip Booking Failed All compensations completed Each service provides both a forward operation and a compensating operation. The Flight Service exposes bookFlight and cancelBooking. The Hotel Service exposes reserveRoom and cancelReservation. When a saga step fails, the saga executor calls compensating operations in reverse order. 2/5

Implementation Approaches Sagas implement through choreography or orchestration. Choreography uses events. Services listen for events and react. The Order Service creates an order and publishes "OrderCreated". Payment Service listens, charges payment, publishes "PaymentCharged". Inventory Service listens, decrements stock, publishes "InventoryUpdated". If payment fails, Payment Service publishes "PaymentFailed". Order Service listens and cancels the order. Saga Choreography: Event-Driven Coordination No central coordinator - services react to events Event Bus (Kafka) ① OrderCreated ② PaymentCharged ③ InventoryUpdated Failure events PaymentFailed OrderCanceled Order Service Publishes: • OrderCreated Listens to: • PaymentFailed → Cancels order Orders DB Publish Listen Payment Service Listens to: • OrderCreated Publishes: • PaymentCharged • PaymentFailed Payments DB Listen Publish Inventory Service Listens to: • PaymentCharged Publishes: • InventoryUpdated • InventoryFailed Inventory DB Listen Publish Pros: • Decentralized, no single point of failure • Loosely coupled services Cons: • Hard to understand overall flow • Difficult to debug, no central state Orchestration uses a central coordinator. The Order Service acts as orchestrator. It calls Payment Service, waits for response. If payment succeeds, it calls Inventory Service. If inventory fails after payment succeeds, it calls Payment Service to issue a refund. 3/5

Saga Orchestration: Centralized Coordination Central orchestrator manages the saga flow Saga Orchestrator (Order Service) Saga State: ① Send: Charge Payment Wait for response... If success: ② Send: Decrement Inventory If failure: ③ Send: Cancel Order (Compensation) Payment Service Receives command: "Charge Payment" Returns: Success/Failure Payments DB ① Command Response Inventory Service Receives command: "Decrement Inventory" Returns: Success/Failure Inventory DB ② Command Response Order Service Receives command: "Cancel Order" (If payment fails) Orders DB ③ Compensate (on failure) Sequence: 1. Orchestrator → Payment 2. If success → Inventory 3. If failure → Cancel Order Pros: • Clear flow, easy to monitor • Centralized state management Cons: • Orchestrator is a dependency • Can be stateless & replicated Orchestration is simpler for most systems. The flow lives in one place. Debugging follows a clear path. State management centralizes. Use choreography only when maximum decoupling justifies the complexity. Trade-offs Sagas provide availability at the cost of temporary inconsistency. After step 1 completes, the system has a booked flight but no hotel yet. This state is visible to other operations. The user might see their flight in "My Trips" before the hotel books or before compensating transactions cancel everything. This eventual consistency is acceptable for most business operations. Users understand "processing" states. The booking completes within seconds. Clear communication makes the temporary inconsistency manageable. Compensating transactions are not true rollbacks. A canceled flight booking creates a cancellation record. The system cannot erase the fact that a booking existed. Compensation must handle idempotency. If the system retries a cancellation, calling cancelBooking twice with the same booking ID must work correctly. 4/5

Interview Application E-commerce checkout provides a clear saga example. A user checks out with three operations: create order, charge payment, decrement inventory. Using orchestration, the Order Service coordinates the saga. It creates the order record first. Then it calls Payment Service. If payment succeeds, it calls Inventory Service. If inventory fails after payment, it calls Payment Service to refund. Explain the compensating transactions explicitly. Each service must expose compensation endpoints. Payment Service has chargePayment and issueRefund. Inventory Service has decrementStock and returnStock. These compensations are business operations, not database rollbacks. Discuss failure scenarios. What if the network fails while calling a compensation? The orchestrator must retry until it succeeds. Compensations must be idempotent. Use unique idempotency keys to prevent duplicate refunds. Mention eventual consistency clearly. After the order creates, it appears in the order history. If payment fails 2 seconds later, the order transitions to "failed" state. Users see this transition. Clear status messages prevent confusion. For deeper understanding of distributed transactions, consistency guarantees, and comparisons with Two-Phase Commit, see Distributed Transactions. When to Use Use sagas when operations span multiple services with separate databases, availability matters more than immediate consistency, and you can design compensating transactions for each operation. E-commerce checkout, travel booking, and order fulfillment workflows fit this pattern. Avoid sagas when operations require perfect consistency at all times, compensating transactions cannot truly undo the operation, or the business logic cannot tolerate temporary inconsistent states visible to users. Most modern distributed systems use sagas. The pattern balances coordination needs with performance and availability requirements. 5/5

Patterns & Templates › Template

Message queue

Open PDF

Message queue Design Template The Template Here is the common template to design scalable services (and therefore solve many system design problems): A very high-level takeaway: Write to message queue Read from cache Let's take a look at it step-by-step. 1/4

Design Template: Component Breakdown Stateless Services Scalable and stateless, these services can be expanded by adding new machines and integrating them through a load balancer. Write Service: Receives client requests and forwards them to the message queue. Read Service: Handles read requests from clients by accessing the cache. Database Serves as cold storage and the source of truth. However, we do not normally read directly from the database since it can be quite slow when the request volume is high. Message Queue A buffer between writer services and data storage. Producers: Comprised of write services that send data changes to the queue. Consumers: Involved in updating both the database and the cache. Database Updater: Asynchronous workers update the database by retrieving jobs from the message queue. Cache Updater: Asynchronous workers refresh the cache by fetching jobs from the message queue. Cache Facilitates fast and efficient read operations. Now let's take a look in detail. Dataflow path Almost all applications can be broken down into read requests and write requests. Because read and write have completely different implications (read doesn’t mutate; write mutates database), we discuss write path and read path separately. Read path For modern large-scale applications with millions of daily users, we almost always read from cache instead of from the database directly. The database acts as a permanent storage solution. Asynchronous jobs frequently transfer data from the database to the cache. 2/4

Write path Write requests are pushed into a /fundamentals/message-queue, allowing backend workers to manage the writing process. This approach balances the processing speeds of different system components, offering a responsive user experience. Message queue is essential to scaling out our system to handle write requests. Producers: Insert messages into the queue. Consumers: Retrieve and process messages asynchronously. The necessity of message queues arises from: Varying Processing Rates: Producers and consumers handle data at different speeds, necessitating a buffer. e.g., the frontend posts comments a lot faster than the backend can write to the db e.g., the frontend booking request is a lot faster than the backend booking service (needs to contact 3rd party) Fault Tolerance: They ensure the persistence of messages, preventing data loss during failures. imagine having the write service calling the db updater (workers) directly. If the request fails, the request is lost with mq, messages are persisted in the queue so if workers are down, messages remain safe and can be processed when workers recover One database per service One additional design recommendation I would like to mention is to not expose the database directly to clients. You almost always create a service that goes with the database and expose the data through APIs. Using the Template Now let's take a look at a couple of concrete examples. 1. Design Twitter Twitter's timeline is a classic template example. Tweets in a user's timeline are pre-fetched for each user into an "inbox" inside the cache and displayed to the user when they open the app on their devices. 3/4

2. Design WhatsApp WhatsApp utilizes the design template with a slight modification. When a user is online, messages are directly transmitted to their web socket server for delivery. Conversely, if the user is offline, the messages are stored in their "inbox" within the cache. 4/4

Patterns & Templates › Template

Build a High-Performance Social Media Comment System with Kafka Redis

Open PDF

Build a High-Performance Social Media Comment System with Kafka & Redis System Design Template Application: Social Media Comment System In this lab, we are going to construct a social media comment application from the ground up, utilizing the essential components we've mastered in previous sections. This exercise will provide a practical understanding of: The role of message queues as a buffer in addressing the processing rate disparities between services. The concept and application of eventual consistency. The process of employing the template to design and develop applications with distinct read and write paths. For popular posts, there could be a burst of traffic with a large number of comments. This imposes a large load on the database which has a limited write capacity. We typically use a message queue as a buffer between the fast comment posting and the limited database write capacity. On the other hand, having millions of users reading the comments imposes an even greater load on the database. We also never have the user-facing services read directly from the database. What we do instead is to cache the data from the database and have the user-facing services read from the cache. System Design Overview Our system design : 1/7

System Design Overview Components: Comment Uploader: Exposes an HTTP POST API that allows a user to upload a comment. We will write a simple Flask server to do this. Message queue: A buffer between user facing service and database updater. We will use Kafka for this. Database Updater: Takes messages from the message queue and write to the database for permanent storage. We will write a simple Kafka consumer in python. Database: We can use a NoSQL database for a comment system like this without complex relationships. In this example, we will use CouchDB which has a feature called “changes feed” that acts as triggers that we can use to write to our cache. Cache: We will use Redis as our cache. Comment Fetcher: A simple service that exposes an HTTP GET API that allows a user to fetch all the comments for a post. Dataflow We separate write and read paths. For the write path: User creates a comment and send it to the Comment Uploader using Comment Uploader’s HTTP POST API. The Comment Uploader enqueues the comment as a message into the message queue. Message queue consumer Database updater fetches the messages, extracts comments and inserts them into the database. After the insert is completed, a database trigger is fired and sends an action to write the record into the cache. For the read path: Users opens a page with comments which sends an API request to the Comment Fetcher which fetches the comments from the cache and returns them to the client. 2/7

Data Schema docker-compose.yml This file sets up the infrastructure including message queue (Kafka), database (MongoDB) and cache (Redis) so we can write our application code using them. version: '3' services: kafka: image: wurstmeister/kafka ports: - "9092:9092" environment: KAFKA_ADVERTISED_HOST_NAME: localhost KAFKA_ZOOKEEPER_CONNECT: zookeeper:2181 KAFKA_AUTO_CREATE_TOPICS_ENABLE: 'true' KAFKA_DELETE_TOPIC_ENABLE: 'true' zookeeper: image: wurstmeister/zookeeper ports: - "2181:2181" redis: image: redis:latest ports: - "6379:6379" mongo: image: mongo:latest ports: - "27017:27017" volumes: couchdb_data: MongoDB We'll set up our database as socialnetwork and have a comments collection with the following schema: { "_id": ObjectId, "post_id": string, "comment": string } For example: socialnetwork> db.comments.find() [ { _id: ObjectId("6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12"), post_id: '1', comment: 'this is a great post thank you' } ] Redis In Redis, we'll use a Redis hash to store comments using post_id as the key: Key: "post:<post_id>" Value: List of comments For example: 127.0.0.1:6379> hgetall "post:1" 1) "6466a29c6fb786d66e584e5a" 2) "{\"post_id\": \"1\", \"comment\": \"this is a great post thank you\", \"_id\": \"6466a29c6fb786d66e584e5a\"}" 3) "6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12" 4) "{\"post_id\": \"1\", \"comment\": \"this is another great post thank you\", \"_id\": \"6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12\"}" Kafka Our comment_uploader will push comments as messages into a topic Kafka topic called comments. 3/7

Services Exposes an HTTP POST API that allows a user to upload a comment. We will write a simple Flask server to do this. It returns 200 right after it pushes the message into the queue so users will get an instant response. from flask import Flask, request from kafka import KafkaProducer import json app = Flask(__name__) producer = KafkaProducer(bootstrap_servers='localhost:9092') @app.route('/comments', methods=['POST']) def upload_comment(): data = request.get_json() post_id = data.get('post_id') comment = data.get('comment') producer.send('comments', value=json.dumps({'post_id': post_id, 'comment': comment}).encode()) return 'Comment uploaded', 200 if __name__ == '__main__': app.run(host='0.0.0.0', port=5001) Database Updater (Kafka Consumer) This is the Kafka consumer that listens for new messages on the comments topic. When a new message comes in, it first writes to the database and then update the cache. from kafka import KafkaConsumer from pymongo import MongoClient import redis import json from bson import ObjectId # encode mongodb's ObjectId to be compatible with JSON class JSONEncoder(json.JSONEncoder): def default(self, o): if isinstance(o, ObjectId): return str(o) return json.JSONEncoder.default(self, o) consumer = KafkaConsumer( 'comments', bootstrap_servers=['localhost:9092'], value_deserializer=lambda x: json.loads(x.decode('utf-8'))) client = MongoClient('mongodb://localhost:27017/') db = client['socialnetwork'] collection = db['comments'] redis_client = redis.Redis(host='localhost', port=6379, db=0) for message in consumer: comment = message.value # insert into database result = collection.insert_one(comment) # update the cache if insertion into database is successful if result.acknowledged: post_id = comment['post_id'] comment_id = str(result.inserted_id) # Store the comment in a hash in Redis. The hash key is the post_id, # and each field in the hash is a comment_id. redis_client.hset(f'post:{post_id}', comment_id, json.dumps(comment, cls=JSONEncoder)) else: print("Error: Failed to insert comment to MongoDB") A web server that provides an API to return comments from the Redis cache. from flask import Flask, request import redis import json app = Flask(__name__) # Create a connection to Redis redis_client = redis.Redis(host='localhost', port=6379, db=0, decode_responses=True) @app.route('/comments', methods=['GET']) def get_comments(): post_id = request.args.get('post_id') # Fetch all comments related to the post_id comments_dict = redis_client.hgetall(f'post:{post_id}') comments = [json.loads(comment) for comment in comments_dict.values()] return {'comments': comments} if __name__ == "__main__": app.run(host="0.0.0.0", port=5002, debug=True) Starting the system First, let’s get the infrastructure up and running using docker-compose up --build. This will start Kafka at port 9092, Redis at port 6379 and MongoDB at port 27017. You should see 4 services running; the extra one being Zookeeper which Kafka depends on for consensus. 4/7

Running components by docker You can install the command line tools to test them if you want: Kafka cli tools Redis cli Mongosh (mongodb cli) Next, let’s go to the app folder and install the pip dependencies pip install -r requirements.txt (and yes, you can use a virtualenv if you want. I’m just simplifying things here). Next, open another terminal window and start running the comment_uploader API server python comment_uploader.py. This will start the server at port 5051: ➜ app python comment_uploader.py * Serving Flask app 'comment_uploader' * Debug mode: off WARNING: This is a development server. Do not use it in a production deployment. Use a production WSGI server instead. * Running on all addresses (0.0.0.0) * Running on * Running on Next, open another terminal window and start running the Kafka consumer using python db_updater.py. The app will simply start and wait for messages to come through. ➜ app python db_updater.py 5/7

Finally, open another terminal window and start running the comment_fetcher API server that reads from Redis cache and returns comments: python comment_fetcher.py. Optionally, you could monitor new messages by running Kafka’s cli tool. kafka-console-consumer.sh --bootstrap-server localhost:9092 --topic comments -- from-beginning Testing the system Now, let’s try sending a comment by hitting our write API: curl -X POST -d '{"post_id":"1", "comment":"this is a great post thank you"}' -H "Content-Type: application/json" You should see the message propagating through our system. 1. Our write API at localhost:5001/comments should accept the request: 127.0.0.1 - - [18/May/2023 15:10:34] "POST /comments HTTP/1.1" 200 - 1. (optional) If you have Kafka cli running, you should see a new message in the log: {"post_id": "1", "comment": "this is a great post thank you"} 1. database_updater should see the new comment from ➜ app python db_updater.py {'post_id': '1', 'comment': 'this is a great post thank you', '_id': ObjectId('6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12')} 1. We should see a new value in our database: socialnetwork> db.comments.find() [ { _id: ObjectId("6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12"), post_id: '1', comment: 'this is a great post thank you' } ] 1. Redis should have a new record 127.0.0.1:6379> hgetall "post:1" 1) "6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12" 1. And finally, if we hit our user read API in localhost:5002/comments?post_id=1 we should see the new comment: { "comments": [ { "_id": "6466a681bec0bd23bec4ce12", "comment": "this is a great post thank you", "post_id": "1" } ] } Nice! It’s working! Handling Loads and Demonstrating Eventual consistency Now, that’s just one message. Let’s send a ton of messages and see what happens. Create a file called post_data.json that contains the post body we just sent. {"post_id":"1", "comment":"this is a great post thank you"} And then, let’s use Apache Bench to send 10000 messsages! Here’s the command: ab -n 10000 -c 100 -p post_data.json -T 'application/json' -n 10000 -c 100 means sending 100 messages each time until we’ve sent 10,000 messages. 6/7

You should see something like this output when you run the command: ➜ app ab -n 10000 -c 100 -p post_data.json -T 'application/json' This is ApacheBench, Version 2.3 <$Revision: 1879490 $> Copyright 1996 Adam Twiss, Zeus Technology Ltd, Licensed to The Apache Software Foundation, Benchmarking 127.0.0.1 (be patient) Completed 1000 requests Completed 2000 requests Completed 3000 requests... And monitor your Redis content using hgetall "post:1" (or hit the read comment API at 5002) and you should see it returns fewer number of records than the number of requests. This is because there are too many messages for the consumer to handle, and there is a delay between the time a message is sent to Kafka and the time it is processed by the consumer. Eventually (after a few seconds), the consumer should catch up and Redis and the read comment API should return exactly the same results as in the MongoDB database. What we have demonstrated here are: Eventual consistency: When user A submits a new comment, we return 200 right away to tell the user the request is successful even though if user B fetches comments using the read API he may not necessarily see the user A’s comment yet. There’s an inconsistency in the system. The inconsistency disappears after a few seconds (eventually consistent). This is tolerable for a non-mission-critical application like social network. Message queue as a buffer: Without a message queue (Kafka), our service wouldn’t be able to handle so many request as we cannot write to database fast enough. Kafka “buffers” the messages in-flight and allows us to scale the system. As we will see, this template of separating the read and write path with message queue as a buffer will be used again and again in our system design journey and can be used to solve a variety of problems. 7/7
